@track cpp
@title C++
@name C++, close to the machine
@blurb Compiled for real by clang++ in your browser: from main() and std::cout to vectors, strings, references, classes and pointers.
@plainvoice true

=== cpp-01 | Hello, main()
--- teach
In the Terminal course you typed one command at a time, pressed Enter, and the shell ran it straight away. C++ works in a different way. You write all your instructions first, in a file. Then a tool turns the whole file into something the computer can run, and only then does it run. This lesson shows you the smallest complete C++ program and what every piece of it does.

C++ is the language much of the world's [[flight software|cpp-in-flight]] is written in, so everything here is a first step toward code that flies.

## A program is a list of instructions

A **program** is a list of instructions the computer follows, in order, top to bottom, like a recipe. Every command you ran in the terminal, `ls` and `git` included, is a program somebody wrote.

The instructions you write are called **source code**: text a person can read. You keep it in a **source file**. C++ source files end in `.cpp`, and the file in the editor here is called `main.cpp`.

## The compiler translates it

The computer's processor cannot read C++. It only understands **machine code**: long runs of numbers, each one a tiny instruction such as "add these two" or "copy this over there".

A **[[compiler|compiler]]** is a program that translates your whole source file into machine code, ahead of time. Here the compiler is called clang++. When you press the run button, two things happen, one after the other:

1. The compiler reads all of `main.cpp` and builds a program out of it.
2. That program runs, and what it prints shows up in the **Console** tab.

## The smallest complete program

Here is a whole C++ program. The rest of the lesson walks through it one line at a time. The gray words after `//` on line 4 are a note for you, not for the compiler; there is more about notes at the end of this lesson.

```cpp
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Launch in T-minus 10\n";   // prints one line
    return 0;
}
```

It prints one line:

```
Launch in T-minus 10
```

### Line 1: bringing in a library

```cpp
#include <iostream>
```

Read it aloud as "hash include i-o-stream". A **library** is a collection of ready-made code that someone else wrote so you do not have to. `iostream` is the part of the C++ [[standard library|standard-library]] that handles input and output ("io" is input/output, and a **stream** is a flow of characters, one after another). Without this line, the compiler does not know what `std::cout` means.

### Line 3: the main function

```cpp
int main() {
```

A **function** is a named block of instructions. This one is called `main`, and it is special: **every C++ program starts in `main`**. When the program runs, `main` runs, line by line from top to bottom, and when `main` ends, the program ends. A program has exactly one `main`.

- `int` (short for integer, a whole number) says that `main` hands back a whole number when it finishes. You will see which number in a moment.
- The **parentheses** `( )` after the name are where a function's inputs go. `main` takes none here, so they are empty.
- The **curly braces** `{ }` hold the function's **body**: the instructions that belong to it. Everything between `{` and the matching `}` is part of `main`. A group of lines inside braces is called a **block**.

### Line 4: printing a line

```cpp
std::cout << "Launch in T-minus 10\n";   // prints one line
```

This is where the work happens. Take it piece by piece:

- `std::cout`, read "standard see-out", is the character output: whatever you send it appears on the screen (here, the Console tab).
- `::`, two colons, is the **scope operator**. Read it as "from". So `std::cout` is "`cout` from `std`", and `std` is the name of the standard library's [[namespace|namespace]].
- `<<`, two less-than signs, is read "put to". It puts the thing on its right into the output on its left. Picture the arrows pointing toward `cout`, showing which way the text flows.
- `"Launch in T-minus 10\n"` is text in double quotes, called a **string** (a string of characters, like beads on a thread). It is printed exactly as written, without the quotes.
- `\n`, a backslash and an n, is the [[newline character|escape-sequences]]. It ends the line. Without it, whatever you print next carries on along the same line.
- `;`, the semicolon, ends the **statement** (one complete instruction), the way a full stop ends a sentence. Every statement ends with `;`.

You can put several things in a row with more `<<`, like this: `std::cout << "a" << 42 << "\n";` prints `a42`. The next lesson is all about that.

### Line 5: reporting success

```cpp
return 0;
```

`return` ends `main` and hands the number `0` back to whoever started the program. `0` means "it worked". That number is the program's [[exit code|exit-code]], and it is the same one you can check in bash.

### Notes for humans

Two slashes, `//`, start a **comment**: a note for people reading the code. The compiler ignores everything from `//` to the end of that line. The starter code below has one:

```cpp
// Print the two lines here.
```

## Nothing runs until the whole file compiles

In languages like Python or JavaScript, a program is read and run one line at a time, so it can get halfway before it trips over a mistake. C++ is different: nothing runs until the whole file compiles. A missing `;` means no program at all, not even the lines that were fine.

**Watch out:** forgetting a semicolon is the mistake everyone makes first. Leave the `;` off line 4 and the compiler stops with an [[error message|compile-errors]] like this:

```
main.cpp:4:42: error: expected ';' after expression
```

That reads as: in `main.cpp`, line 4, column 42, a `;` was expected. Read the compiler's first error, not the last one: it points at the line to fix.

::: context cpp-in-flight Where C++ flies
C++ was created by Bjarne Stroustrup at Bell Labs. He began in 1979 with a language called "C with Classes", renamed it C++ in 1983, and it was first sold in 1985. It is still used wherever a program must be fast and sit close to the hardware. Lockheed Martin wrote a C++ coding standard in 2005 for the F-35 fighter's software, and SpaceX engineers have said publicly that the flight software for Falcon 9 and Dragon is written in C++. Its older sister, C, runs many NASA spacecraft, including the Curiosity rover. What you learn here carries over to both.
:::

::: context compiler Translating ahead of time
A compiler is a translator that works on the whole book before anyone reads it. You hand it your source file; it checks every line and writes out a finished program in machine code. The program is then run as often as you like, without the compiler.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="8" y="35" width="96" height="54" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="56" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">main.cpp</text>
  <text x="56" y="76" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">words you write</text>
  <rect x="132" y="35" width="96" height="54" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">compiler</text>
  <text x="180" y="76" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">clang++</text>
  <rect x="256" y="35" width="96" height="54" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="304" y="58" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">program</text>
  <text x="304" y="76" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">machine code</text>
  <line x1="106" y1="62" x2="126" y2="62" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="130,62 122,57 122,67" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="230" y1="62" x2="250" y2="62" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="254,62 246,57 246,67" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="116" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">one mistake here and no program comes out</text>
</svg>
```

A single mistake stops the compiler, so no program is made at all. That feels strict, but it means many errors are caught on your desk instead of in flight.
:::

::: context standard-library The toolbox that comes with C++
Every C++ compiler comes with the standard library: ready-made, well-tested code for printing, reading input, text, lists, maths and much more. Each part lives in a **header**, a file you pull in with `#include`. Lines that start with `#` are handled before the real compiling begins: `#include <iostream>` copies the contents of the iostream header into your file. You will meet more headers soon, such as `<string>` for text.
:::

::: context namespace Surnames for code
Two people in a class can both be called Alex, so you add a surname to tell them apart. A namespace does the same for code: it is a family name that everything in the standard library shares, and that name is `std`. So `std::cout` means "the `cout` from the `std` family". Someone else's library could have its own `cout` without a clash. You may see programs that write `using namespace std;` to drop the `std::`, but many teams avoid that, because the surname tells a reader exactly where a name comes from.
:::

::: context escape-sequences Characters you cannot type into a string
Inside a string, the backslash `\` means "the next character is special". It is called an escape. `\n` is a newline, `\t` is a tab, `\"` is a double quote that does not end the string, and `\\` is one real backslash. So `\n` is two characters in your source file but becomes a single invisible "end of line" character in the output.
:::

::: context exit-code The number a program leaves behind
When any program finishes, it leaves a small whole number called its exit code: `0` for success, anything else for some kind of failure. In bash, `echo $?` prints the exit code of the last command, and `&&` runs the next command only if the last one returned `0`. Scripts that launch other programs, including test systems that check flight software, rely on these codes. In `main` only, if you leave out `return 0;`, C++ returns 0 for you, but writing it makes your intent plain.
:::

::: context compile-errors Reading what the compiler says
The compiler reports each problem as `file:line:column: error: message`, then usually shows the line with a `^` pointing at the spot. One real mistake often causes several more errors after it, because the compiler gets confused about what follows. That is why you fix the first error, compile again, and see what is left. It also sometimes prints a **warning**: the program still builds, but something looks suspicious. Warnings are worth reading too.
:::
--- task
The starter already has the skeleton: `#include <iostream>`, `int main() { … }` and `return 0;`. Inside `main`, above `return 0;`, write two `std::cout` statements so the program prints exactly these two lines:

```
Hello, world!
C++ is compiled and running in your browser.
```

Each line needs its own `\n` at the end, and each statement ends with `;`. Spelling, capitals and the full stop must match exactly.
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    // Print the two lines here.
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Hello, world!\n";
    std::cout << "C++ is compiled and running in your browser.\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Use one `std::cout` statement for each line of output, placed between the `{` and `return 0;`.
--- hint
The first one is `std::cout << "Hello, world!\n";`.
--- hint
The second has the same shape: `std::cout << "…\n";` with the second sentence between the quotes, full stop included.
--- check output | Prints both lines, exactly
Hello, world!
C++ is compiled and running in your browser.

+++ practice | A mission patch in three lines
--- task
Write a program that prints exactly these three lines:

```
Mission: Artemis II
Crew: four astronauts
Destination: the Moon
```

Use one `std::cout` statement for each line, and end each line with `\n`. Capitals, spaces and colons must match exactly.
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    // Print the three lines here.
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Mission: Artemis II\n";
    std::cout << "Crew: four astronauts\n";
    std::cout << "Destination: the Moon\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Each line is its own statement: `std::cout << "…\n";`, placed between the `{` and `return 0;`.
--- hint
The first statement is `std::cout << "Mission: Artemis II\n";`. The other two have the same shape.
--- check output | Prints all three lines, exactly
Mission: Artemis II
Crew: four astronauts
Destination: the Moon
--- check includes | The last line is there
Destination: the Moon
--- check source | Prints with std::cout
std::cout\s*<<

+++ practice | Two lines from one statement
--- task
Print these two lines, but use only **one** `std::cout` statement:

```
Go for launch
Go for orbit
```

A string can hold more than one `\n`. Put a `\n` in the middle of the string to end the first line, and another at the end.
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Go for launch\n";
    std::cout << "Go for orbit\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Go for launch\nGo for orbit\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Everything goes inside one pair of double quotes, after one `std::cout <<`.
--- hint
Where the first line ends, write `\n` and carry straight on with the second line's words, inside the same string.
--- check output | Prints both lines
Go for launch
Go for orbit
--- check source absent | Uses only one std::cout
std::cout[\s\S]*std::cout
--- check source | Ends a line with \n inside the string
Go for launch\\nGo
?? The first \n goes straight after "launch", with no space, inside the same string.

+++ practice | A checklist with a gap
--- task
Print this pre-flight checklist. The third line is empty: print a line with nothing on it.

```
PRE-FLIGHT CHECKLIST
====================

1. Suits sealed
2. Hatch closed
3. Comms check
```

Also write a comment, starting with `//`, somewhere inside `main` that says what the program does. The compiler ignores it, but a reader will not.

    std::cout << "PRE-FLIGHT CHECKLIST\n";

}
--- solution

int main() {
    // Print the pre-flight checklist.
    std::cout << "PRE-FLIGHT CHECKLIST\n";
    std::cout << "====================\n";
    std::cout << "\n";
    std::cout << "1. Suits sealed\n";
    std::cout << "2. Hatch closed\n";
    std::cout << "3. Comms check\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
An empty line is a string with nothing in it but the newline: `"\n"`.
--- hint
The line of equals signs is ordinary text: twenty `=` characters between the quotes, then `\n`.

PRE-FLIGHT CHECKLIST
====================

1. Suits sealed
2. Hatch closed
3. Comms check
--- check includes | The numbered items are there
1. Suits sealed
3. Comms check
--- check source | Has a comment

+++ practice | Quotes and backslashes
--- task
Print exactly these two lines:

```
Say "Go" when ready
Save it in C:\flight\main.cpp
```

The first line has double quotes inside it, and the second has backslashes. Inside a string, a plain `"` would end the string, and a plain `\` starts an escape like `\n`. So write a double quote as `\"` (backslash, quote) and a backslash as `\\` (two backslashes).
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    // Print the two lines here.
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Say \"Go\" when ready\n";
    std::cout << "Save it in C:\\flight\\main.cpp\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Each quote mark that should appear in the output is written `\"` inside the string.
--- hint
Each backslash that should appear in the output is written `\\`. Watch out: `\f` and `\m` are not what you want.
--- check output | Prints both lines, exactly
Say "Go" when ready
Save it in C:\flight\main.cpp
--- check includes | The quotes come out
Say "Go" when ready
--- check includes | The backslashes come out
C:\flight\main.cpp

This program should print a countdown, one number per line:

```
T-minus 3
T-minus 2
T-minus 1
Liftoff!
```

Instead it prints `T-minus 3T-minus 2` on one line and `T-minus 1Liftoff!` on another. Two of the statements are missing something. Find them and fix them.

#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "T-minus 3\n";
    std::cout << "T-minus 2\n";
    std::cout << "T-minus 1\n";
    std::cout << "Liftoff!\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
A line only ends where the program prints `\n`. Which strings do not end with one?
--- hint
`"T-minus 3"` needs a `\n` at its end, and so does one other string.
--- check output | Prints the countdown, one line each

T-minus 2
T-minus 1
Liftoff!

Liftoff!

=== cpp-01b | Printing text and numbers together
--- teach
Last lesson each `std::cout` statement printed one string. Now you will put several pieces into one statement, mix words with numbers, and learn what the compiler tells you when it is unhappy.

## Chaining pieces with <<

Remember that `<<` is read "put to". You can use it again and again in one statement. Each `<<` puts the next piece into the output, [[one after another|chaining]], left to right:

```cpp
std::cout << "a" << 42 << "\n";
```

This prints:

```
a42
```

Three pieces went out: the string `"a"`, the number `42`, and the newline `"\n"`. Only one `;` is needed, at the very end, because it is all one statement.

## Numbers need no quotes

A number written in your code without quotes, like `42`, is a real number. Later you will do arithmetic with numbers like this. The same digits inside quotes, `"42"`, are just [[text that looks like a number|text-vs-number]]. Both print the same, but only the first is a number to C++.

```cpp
std::cout << 42 << "\n";     // a number
std::cout << "42" << "\n";   // a string made of the characters 4 and 2
```

Both lines print `42`.

## You choose every space

The pieces are glued together exactly as they are. C++ adds no spaces of its own, so any space you want must be inside the quotes:

```cpp
std::cout << "Crew:" << 4 << "\n";    // prints Crew:4
std::cout << "Crew: " << 4 << "\n";   // prints Crew: 4
```

The second line has a space after the colon, inside the quotes.

## Long statements can span lines

Outside quotes, the compiler ignores extra [[spaces and line breaks|whitespace]]. A statement ends at its `;`, not at the end of a line, so a long one can be split to make it easier to read:

```cpp
std::cout << "Stage 1: " << 9 << " engines\n"
          << "Stage 2: " << 1 << " engine\n";
```

That is one statement, printing two lines:

```
Stage 1: 9 engines
Stage 2: 1 engine
```

## Errors and warnings

When the compiler finds a mistake, it prints an **error** and no program is made. Sometimes it prints a [[warning|warnings]] instead: the program is built, but the compiler thinks something looks wrong. Read both.

**Watch out:** every piece needs its own `<<` in front of it. Leave one out and the compiler cannot tell where one piece ends and the next begins:

```cpp
std::cout << "Crew: " 4 << "\n";   // a << is missing before 4
```

The compiler says `error: expected ';' after expression`, because it thinks the statement ended after `"Crew: "`. The message does not always name the real problem, but the line number is right, so look closely at that line. Put the missing `<<` back and it compiles.

::: context chaining Why the arrows can keep going
`std::cout << "a"` does its printing and then hands back `std::cout` itself. So the next `<<` has `std::cout` on its left again, ready for the next piece. The computer works it out like this: `((std::cout << "a") << 42) << "\n"`.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="20" width="80" height="34" rx="5" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="50" y="42" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">"a"</text>
  <rect x="110" y="20" width="80" height="34" rx="5" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="150" y="42" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">42</text>
  <rect x="210" y="20" width="80" height="34" rx="5" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="250" y="42" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">"\n"</text>
  <text x="50" y="72" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">1st</text>
  <text x="150" y="72" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">2nd</text>
  <text x="250" y="72" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">3rd</text>
  <rect x="60" y="84" width="240" height="28" rx="5" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="103" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">Console:  a42  (then a new line)</text>
</svg>
```

The pieces go out in the order you wrote them, and nothing is added in between.
:::

::: context text-vs-number Two different things that look alike
Inside the computer, the number `42` is stored as one value the processor can add, multiply and compare. The string `"42"` is stored as two separate characters, a `4` and a `2`, each kept as its own code. You cannot do arithmetic on the string: to C++ it is no more a number than the word `"hello"` is. This difference matters a lot once your programs start calculating, which starts very soon.
:::

::: context whitespace What the compiler skips
Whitespace means spaces, tabs and line breaks. Outside quotes, the compiler treats any amount of it the same as one space, and between symbols it can often be left out entirely. So `std::cout<<42<<"\n";` compiles, but it is harder to read. Programmers use spaces and indenting (lining things up with spaces) purely for people. Inside quotes, every space is kept, because it is part of the text.
:::

::: context warnings When the compiler says "are you sure?"
A warning means "this compiles, but it is probably not what you meant". The compiler in this app is started with the options `-Wall` and `-Wextra`, which switch on a wide set of warnings. Professional flight-software teams usually go further and treat every warning as an error, so no program is built until the code is warning-free. Getting into the habit of reading warnings now will save you from real bugs later.
:::
--- task
Write a program that prints exactly these two lines:

```
Crew: 4
Days in orbit: 182
```

Write `4` and `182` as numbers, without quotes, and chain each one after its text with `<<`, like `std::cout << "Crew: " << 4 << "\n";`. Mind the space after each colon.
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    // Print the two lines here, with 4 and 182 as numbers.
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Crew: " << 4 << "\n";
    std::cout << "Days in orbit: " << 182 << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Each line is three pieces chained with `<<`: the text, the number, and `"\n"`.
--- hint
The first line is `std::cout << "Crew: " << 4 << "\n";`. The space after the colon sits inside the quotes.
--- hint
The second line has the same shape, with `"Days in orbit: "` and `182`.
--- check output | Prints both lines, exactly
Crew: 4
Days in orbit: 182
--- check source | Puts the number 4 into the output without quotes
<<\s*4\s*<<
--- check source | Puts the number 182 into the output without quotes
<<\s*182\s*<<

+++ practice | Launch pad numbers
--- task
Write a program that prints exactly these two lines:

```
Launch pad: 39
Engines: 9, height: 70 m
```

Write `39`, `9` and `70` as numbers, without quotes, and chain each one into the output with `<<`. The second line has two numbers in it, so it needs more pieces.
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Launch pad: 39\n";
    std::cout << "Engines: 9, height: 70 m\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "Launch pad: " << 39 << "\n";
    std::cout << "Engines: " << 9 << ", height: " << 70 << " m\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Split each line into text pieces and number pieces. The text pieces keep their spaces and punctuation inside the quotes.
--- hint
The second line is five pieces: `"Engines: "`, `9`, `", height: "`, `70`, `" m\n"`.
--- check output | Prints both lines, exactly
Launch pad: 39
Engines: 9, height: 70 m
--- check source | 39 is a number, not text
<<\s*39\s*<<
--- check source | 9 and 70 are numbers, not text
<<\s*9\s*<<[\s\S]*<<\s*70\s*<<

+++ practice | A countdown in one statement
--- task
Print these three lines with a **single** `std::cout` statement, split over several lines of code to keep it readable:

```
T-minus 3
T-minus 2
T-minus 1
```

Write the numbers `3`, `2` and `1` as numbers, not inside quotes. Only one `;` ends the whole statement.
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "T-minus 3\n";
    std::cout << "T-minus 2\n";
    std::cout << "T-minus 1\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution

int main() {
    std::cout << "T-minus " << 3 << "\n"
              << "T-minus " << 2 << "\n"
              << "T-minus " << 1 << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Start with `std::cout <<` once. Every later line of code starts with `<<` and carries on the same statement.
--- hint
Each output line is three pieces: `"T-minus "`, the number, and `"\n"`. Put the `;` only after the very last piece.
--- check output | Prints the countdown
T-minus 3
T-minus 2
T-minus 1
--- check source absent | Uses only one std::cout

--- check source | The numbers are not in quotes

Print this card. The first line has double quotes in it, and the fourth line is empty:

+++ practice | Zero, negatives and no space at all
--- task
Print exactly these three lines:

```

```

Write `-40`, `0`, `3` and `5` as numbers without quotes. Look closely at the spaces: the third line has **no** space after its colon, so nothing may come between `Level:` and `3`.

    std::cout << "Temperature: " << -40 << " C\n";
    std::cout << "Speed: " << 0 << " m/s\n";
    std::cout << "Level:" << 3 << "/" << 5 << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
A negative number is written with a minus sign in front, like `-40`, and it prints with its minus sign.
--- hint
C++ adds no spaces of its own. For the third line, the text piece is `"Level:"` with no space before the closing quote, and the slash is its own piece, `"/"`.
--- check output | Prints all three lines, exactly

Speed: 0 m/s
Level:3/5
--- check source | -40 and 0 are numbers

=== cpp-02 | Variables and types
--- teach
So far every value you printed was typed straight into the `std::cout` line. Real programs need to remember values and use them again: the fuel left, the number of crew, the name of a city. This lesson gives values names.

## A variable is a labeled box

Picture a box with a label on it. You put a value in the box, and later you ask for it by the label. In C++ that box is a **variable**: a named place that holds a value.

```cpp
int count = 3;
std::cout << count << "\n";   // prints 3
```

The first line is a **declaration**: it makes the variable. Read it as "an int called count, which [[gets|gets-not-equals]] 3":

- `int` is the **type**: what kind of value the box holds. `int` holds whole numbers.
- `count` is the variable's **name**.
- `=`, read "gets", puts the value `3` in the box.
- `;` ends the statement, as always.

The second line prints the value in the box, not the word "count", because the name has no quotes around it.

## Every variable has a fixed type

C++ is **[[statically typed|static-typing]]**: every variable has a type, fixed when it is declared, and it can never hold a different kind of value. The type also decides how much [[memory|memory-bytes]] the box takes. Here are the types you will use most, one at a time.

### Whole numbers and decimals

```cpp
int count = 3;               // whole number
double price = 4.99;         // decimal
```

An `int` holds a whole number with no decimal point. A `double` holds a number with a decimal point; its name comes from **[[double-precision floating point|floating-point]]**, the way the computer stores decimals.

### True or false

```cpp
bool ready = true;
```

A `bool` holds one of two values, `true` or `false`. It is named after George Boole, a mathematician who worked out the rules of true-and-false logic.

### One character

```cpp
char grade = 'A';            // one character, single quotes
```

A `char` holds exactly [[one character|char-codes]]: a letter, digit or symbol. It goes in **single quotes**. `'A'` is a char; `"A"` in double quotes is a string.

### Text

```cpp
std::string name = "Ada";    // needs #include <string>
```

A `std::string` holds text of any length, in double quotes. It lives in the standard library (the `std::` again) and needs `#include <string>` at the top of the file, next to `#include <iostream>`.

### Letting the compiler choose

```cpp
auto total = count * price;  // auto: let the compiler work out the type (double)
```

`auto` asks the compiler to work out the type from the value on the right. The star `*` means "times", so `count * price` is 3 times 4.99. A whole number times a decimal gives a decimal, so `total` becomes a `double`. It still has a fixed type; you just did not have to write it.

## Values that must not change

Put `const` (short for constant) in front, and the value can never change:

```cpp
const double pi = 3.14159;
pi = 3;   // compile error
```

Trying to assign to a [[const|const-values]] variable is a compile error:

```
error: cannot assign to variable 'pi' with const-qualified type 'const double'
```

The compiler catches the mistake before the program even exists.

## Printing variables

Variables chain into `std::cout` like any other piece:

```cpp
std::string name = "Ada";
int age = 36;
std::cout << name << " is " << age << "\n";   // prints Ada is 36
```

**Watch out:** a variable's name goes in the code without quotes. `std::cout << "name"` prints the word `name`. `std::cout << name` prints what is in the box, `Ada`.

::: context gets-not-equals Why "gets" and not "equals"
In maths, `=` says two things are already the same. In C++, a single `=` is an action: work out the value on the right, then store it in the box on the left. That is why programmers read it "gets". It also explains a line that would be nonsense in maths, `count = count + 1;`: take the value in `count`, add one, and put the result back. You will use exactly that idea in a few lessons.
:::

::: context static-typing Checked before launch
In Python, a variable can hold a number now and some text later, and a mix-up is only discovered when that line runs. In C++, the compiler knows the type of every variable and checks every use of it before the program is built. Put text where a number belongs and you get a compile error on your desk, not a surprise during a flight. For software that cannot be patched halfway to Mars, finding mistakes early is worth a lot.
:::

::: context memory-bytes How big each box is
Memory is a long row of tiny cells called **bytes**; each byte holds 8 bits (8 ones-and-zeros). On nearly every computer today a `char` is 1 byte, a `bool` 1 byte, an `int` 4 bytes and a `double` 8 bytes.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="10" y="31" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">char</text>
  <rect x="70" y="16" width="26" height="22" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="104" y="31" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">1 byte</text>
  <text x="10" y="71" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">int</text>
  <rect x="70" y="56" width="26" height="22" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="96" y="56" width="26" height="22" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="122" y="56" width="26" height="22" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="148" y="56" width="26" height="22" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="182" y="71" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">4 bytes</text>
  <text x="10" y="111" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">double</text>
  <rect x="70" y="96" width="26" height="22" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="96" y="96" width="26" height="22" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="122" y="96" width="26" height="22" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="148" y="96" width="26" height="22" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="174" y="96" width="26" height="22" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="200" y="96" width="26" height="22" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="226" y="96" width="26" height="22" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="252" y="96" width="26" height="22" fill="#1d6fd1" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="286" y="111" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">8 bytes</text>
</svg>
```

Four bytes give an `int` room for whole numbers from −2,147,483,648 to 2,147,483,647, about 2 billion either way. A later lesson shows what to do when that is not enough.
:::

::: context floating-point Decimals are stored approximately
A `double` stores a number as a set of digits plus a position for the decimal point, which can "float" left or right, so it holds both 0.000001 and 6,000,000,000 in the same 8 bytes. It keeps about 15 to 16 significant digits. Most decimals, like 0.1, cannot be stored exactly, so tiny rounding errors creep in. Also, `std::cout` shows only 6 significant digits by default: `std::cout << 3.14159265` prints `3.14159`. `1651.1` has 5, so it prints in full.
:::

::: context char-codes Letters are numbers underneath
Every character is stored as a small number called its character code. In the ASCII code, which C++ uses for ordinary English letters, `'A'` is 65, `'B'` is 66, and the digit `'7'` is 55, not 7. That is why a `char` fits in one byte: one byte can hold 256 different codes. When you print a `char`, `std::cout` shows the letter, not the number.
:::

::: context const-values Constants in engineering code
Some numbers should never change while a program runs: the value of pi, the standard gravity 9.80665 m/s², the number of engines on a stage. Marking them `const` does two jobs. It tells every reader "this is fixed", and it makes the compiler refuse any line that tries to change it by accident. Flight-software coding standards encourage making everything `const` that does not need to change, because a value that cannot change cannot be changed wrongly.
:::
--- task
Inside `main`, declare these three variables:

- `const std::string city = "Houston";`
- an `int` called `population` holding `2300000`
- a `double` called `area_km2` holding `1651.1`

Then print them in one `std::cout` statement so the output is exactly:

```
Houston: 2300000 people, 1651.1 km2
```

The starter already includes `<string>`.
--- starter
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

int main() {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

int main() {
    const std::string city = "Houston";
    int population = 2300000;
    double area_km2 = 1651.1;
    std::cout << city << ": " << population << " people, " << area_km2 << " km2\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Declare each variable on its own line, type first: `int population = 2300000;`, and the same shape for the `double`.
--- hint
Chain everything into one `std::cout << … << "\n";`. The variables go in without quotes; the words and punctuation between them go in quotes.
--- hint
The pieces are: `city`, `": "`, `population`, `" people, "`, `area_km2`, `" km2\n"`.
--- check output | Prints the line
Houston: 2300000 people, 1651.1 km2
--- check source | city is a const std::string
const\s+std::string\s+city
--- check source | population is an int
\bint\s+population\b

+++ practice | Seven, '7' and nothing at all

- a `std::string` called `gap` holding one space, `" "`
- an `int` called `debt` holding `-250`

+++ practice | Solar panel area
--- task
A spacecraft has 8 solar panels, each 2.5 m wide and 1.2 m tall. Declare these as `const` values:

- `const double width_m = 2.5;`
- `const double height_m = 1.2;`
- `const int panels = 8;`

Then use `auto` twice: `panel_area`, which is `width_m` times `height_m`, and `total_area`, which is `panel_area` times `panels`. Print exactly:

```
One panel: 2.5 m x 1.2 m = 3 m2
All 8 panels: 24 m2
```

Every number in the output comes from a variable. Do not type `3` or `24` yourself.
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    const double width_m = 2.5;
    const double height_m = 1.2;
    const int panels = 8;
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    const double width_m = 2.5;
    const double height_m = 1.2;
    const int panels = 8;
    auto panel_area = width_m * height_m;
    auto total_area = panel_area * panels;
    std::cout << "One panel: " << width_m << " m x " << height_m << " m = " << panel_area << " m2\n";
    std::cout << "All " << panels << " panels: " << total_area << " m2\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Work out `panel_area` first, then use it to work out `total_area`. The star `*` means times.
--- hint
`std::cout` shows `3.0` as `3` and `24.0` as `24`, so you do not need to do anything special to get those.
--- hint
The first line is seven pieces: `"One panel: "`, `width_m`, `" m x "`, `height_m`, `" m = "`, `panel_area`, then `" m2\n"`.
--- check output | Prints both lines
One panel: 2.5 m x 1.2 m = 3 m2
All 8 panels: 24 m2
--- check source | Uses auto
\bauto\s+\w+\s*=
--- check source absent | Does not type 24
\b24\b

=== cpp-02b | Writing your own function
--- teach
You have already met one function: `main`, where every program starts. Now you will write your own. A function lets you give a piece of work a name, and then use that name whenever you need the work done.

## A function is a small machine

Think of a vending machine. You put something in, it does its job, and something comes out. A function is the same: it takes some values in, works on them, and hands one value back.

```cpp
int times(int a, int b) {
    return a * b;
}
```

Read it from left to right:

- `int` at the front is the **return type**: the kind of value the function hands back. Here, a whole number.
- `times` is the function's **name**.
- `(int a, int b)` are the **[[parameters|parameters-copies]]**: the inputs, each with its type and a name, separated by a comma. Inside the function, `a` and `b` work like variables.
- `{ … }` is the **body**, the work the function does.
- `return a * b;` works out `a * b` and hands that value back. The star `*` means "times", because a keyboard has no × sign. `return` also ends the function straight away.

## Calling a function

To use a function, you **call** it: write its name with values in the parentheses.

```cpp
times(2, 3)        // gives back 6
```

The values a call passes in, `2` and `3` here, are the **arguments**. The first argument goes into the first parameter (`a` is 2) and the second into the second (`b` is 3).

A call can go anywhere a value can, for example straight into `std::cout`:

```cpp
#include <iostream>

int times(int a, int b) {
    return a * b;
}

int main() {
    std::cout << times(2, 3) << "\n";   // prints 6
    return 0;
}
```

The function is written above `main`, so the compiler has seen it by the time `main` uses it.

## Another example

The minus sign `-` subtracts, as it does on paper:

```cpp
int stages_left(int dropped) {
    return 3 - dropped;
}
```

This rocket has 3 stages, and the function takes one input: how many have been dropped. `stages_left(1)` gives back `2`. Function names follow the same [[naming rules|names]] as variable names.

## The checker brings its own main

From here on, some lessons ask for **functions** rather than a whole program. You write the function; [[the checker supplies main|test-harness]] and calls it with test values. So do **not** write a `main` in those lessons.

**Watch out:** a program may have only one `main`. If you write one in a lesson like this, the checker's `main` makes it two, and the compiler stops with:

```
error: redefinition of 'main'
```

The other slip is leaving out the `return` line. Then the function hands back [[no proper value|missing-return]], and the compiler warns you about it.

::: context parameters-copies Parameters get copies
When you call `times(2, 3)`, the function gets its own copies of the values: a new `a` holding 2 and a new `b` holding 3. If the function changed `a`, the caller's value would not change, the same way scribbling on a photocopy leaves the original clean. A parameter is the name in the recipe; an argument is the actual value you hand over. Later, in the lesson on references, you will see how a function can work on the caller's own variable instead of a copy.
:::

::: context names What you may call things
A name (programmers say **identifier**) may use letters, digits and the underscore `_`, but it may not start with a digit and may not contain spaces. So `fuel_left` and `stage2` are fine, but `2stage` and `fuel left` are not. Names are case-sensitive: `Count` and `count` are different. Words that C++ itself uses, like `int` and `return`, cannot be names. Many C++ programmers join words with underscores, a style called snake_case.
:::

::: context test-harness How the checking works
When you press run in a lesson like this, the checker adds its own `main` below your code. That `main` calls your functions with test values, such as `add(2, 3)`, and compares what comes back with the right answer. This is called a **unit test**: testing one small piece on its own. Professional teams do the same thing on a huge scale. Flight software is surrounded by thousands of tests that run on every change, so a broken function is caught long before it reaches a rocket.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="10" y="12" width="150" height="40" rx="5" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="85" y="37" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">your add(a, b)</text>
  <rect x="10" y="68" width="150" height="40" rx="5" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="85" y="93" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">checker's main()</text>
  <line x1="160" y1="80" x2="220" y2="45" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="228" y="40" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">add(2, 3) gives 5?</text>
  <line x1="160" y1="96" x2="220" y2="96" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="228" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1">pass</text>
  <text x="270" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">or</text>
  <text x="292" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c">fail</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context missing-return Falling off the end
If a function promises to return an `int` but reaches its closing `}` without a `return`, what comes back is not defined by the rules of C++. It could be any leftover number in memory, and it could change from one run to the next. The compiler warns "non-void function does not return a value". `main` is the one exception: reaching the end of `main` counts as `return 0;`.
:::
--- task
Write two functions. No `main`: the checker supplies it.

- `int add(int a, int b)`, which returns `a` plus `b`. For example, `add(2, 3)` returns `5`.
- `int fuel_left(int start, int used)`, which returns `start` minus `used`. For example, `fuel_left(500, 120)` returns `380`.
--- starter
// No main() in this lesson: the checker supplies it and calls your functions.

int add(int a, int b) {
    return 0;
}

int fuel_left(int start, int used) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
// No main() in this lesson: the checker supplies it and calls your functions.

int add(int a, int b) {
    return a + b;
}

int fuel_left(int start, int used) {
    return start - used;
}
--- hint
Each function needs only one line changed: the `return` line. Replace the `0` with the value the function should hand back.
--- hint
For `add`, hand back the two parameters added together: `return a + b;`.
--- hint
For `fuel_left`, the same shape with `-`: `return start - used;`.
--- check case | add(2, 3) is 5
add(2, 3)
=> 5
--- check case | add(-4, 10) is 6
add(-4, 10)
=> 6
--- check case | fuel_left(500, 120) is 380
fuel_left(500, 120)
=> 380

+++ practice | A fence around the pad
--- task
A landing pad is a rectangle. A fence runs all the way around its edge. Write a function `int perimeter(int width, int height)` that returns the length of the fence: two widths and two heights, added together.

So `perimeter(30, 20)` returns `100`, and `perimeter(5, 5)` returns `20`. No `main`: the checker supplies it and calls your function.
--- starter
int perimeter(int width, int height) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
int perimeter(int width, int height) {
    return width + width + height + height;
}
--- hint
Only the `return` line needs to change. Walk around the rectangle: along a width, up a height, back along a width, down a height.
--- hint
Hand back `width` plus `width` plus `height` plus `height`.
--- check case | perimeter(30, 20) is 100
perimeter(30, 20)
=> 100
--- check case | A square: perimeter(5, 5) is 20
perimeter(5, 5)
=> 20
--- check case | A flat pad: perimeter(7, 0) is 14
perimeter(7, 0)
=> 14

+++ practice | Three inputs
--- task
Write a function `int total_crew(int pilots, int engineers, int scientists)` that returns how many people there are in all. It takes three parameters, separated by commas.

For example, `total_crew(2, 1, 3)` returns `6`, and `total_crew(0, 0, 0)` returns `0`. No `main`.
--- starter
int total_crew(int pilots, int engineers) {
    return pilots + engineers;
}
--- solution
int total_crew(int pilots, int engineers, int scientists) {
    return pilots + engineers + scientists;
}
--- hint
The starter only takes two parameters. Add a third, `int scientists`, after a comma.
--- hint
Then add all three together in the `return` line.
--- check case | total_crew(2, 1, 3) is 6
total_crew(2, 1, 3)
=> 6
--- check case | total_crew(0, 0, 4) is 4
total_crew(0, 0, 4)
=> 4
--- check case | total_crew(0, 0, 0) is 0
total_crew(0, 0, 0)
=> 0

+++ practice | Fuel after several burns
--- task
A spacecraft starts with some fuel and fires its engine several times. Every burn uses the same amount.

Write `int fuel_after(int start, int burns, int per_burn)` that returns the fuel left. Inside the function, first make a variable `used` holding `burns` times `per_burn`, then return `start` minus `used`.

For example, `fuel_after(1000, 3, 120)` returns `640`: three burns of 120 use 360, and 1000 minus 360 is 640. No `main`.
--- starter
int fuel_after(int start, int burns, int per_burn) {
    return start;
}
--- solution
int fuel_after(int start, int burns, int per_burn) {
    int used = burns * per_burn;
    return start - used;
}
--- hint
A function body can hold more than one line. Declare `int used = …;` first, the same way you declared variables in `main`.
--- hint
`used` is `burns * per_burn`. The answer is `start - used`.
--- check case | fuel_after(1000, 3, 120) is 640
fuel_after(1000, 3, 120)
=> 640
--- check case | No burns leaves all the fuel
fuel_after(500, 0, 50)
=> 500
--- check case | Burning exactly all of it leaves 0
fuel_after(100, 5, 20)
=> 0

+++ practice | Below sea level
--- task
A probe starts at some altitude in metres, climbs a while, then drops a while. Write `int net_altitude(int start, int climbed, int dropped)` that returns where it ends up: the start, plus the climb, minus the drop.

The answer can be below 0, because the probe can go below sea level, and the start can be below 0 too. So `net_altitude(100, 50, 30)` returns `120`, `net_altitude(100, 0, 250)` returns `-150`, and `net_altitude(-30, 40, 0)` returns `10`. No `main`.
--- starter
int net_altitude(int start, int climbed, int dropped) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
int net_altitude(int start, int climbed, int dropped) {
    return start + climbed - dropped;
}
--- hint
Climbing adds to the altitude and dropping takes away from it.
--- hint
Hand back `start + climbed - dropped`. It works the same whether the numbers are above or below 0.
--- check case | net_altitude(100, 50, 30) is 120
net_altitude(100, 50, 30)
=> 120
--- check case | Ending below sea level: net_altitude(100, 0, 250) is -150
net_altitude(100, 0, 250)
=> -150
--- check case | Starting below sea level: net_altitude(-30, 40, 0) is 10
net_altitude(-30, 40, 0)
=> 10
--- check case | All zero gives 0
net_altitude(0, 0, 0)
=> 0

+++ practice | Fix the mass functions
--- task
These two functions should work out a rocket's mass:

- `cargo_mass(boxes, kg_per_box)` should return the mass of all the boxes: `cargo_mass(10, 25)` is `250`.
- `launch_mass(dry_kg, cargo_kg)` should return the rocket's empty mass plus its cargo: `launch_mass(1000, 250)` is `1250`.

Instead, `cargo_mass(10, 25)` returns `10`, and `launch_mass(1000, 250)` returns `750`. Each function has one wrong line. Fix them. No `main`.
--- starter
int cargo_mass(int boxes, int kg_per_box) {
    int mass = boxes * kg_per_box;
    return boxes;
}

int launch_mass(int dry_kg, int cargo_kg) {
    return dry_kg - cargo_kg;
}
--- solution
int cargo_mass(int boxes, int kg_per_box) {
    int mass = boxes * kg_per_box;
    return mass;
}

int launch_mass(int dry_kg, int cargo_kg) {
    return dry_kg + cargo_kg;
}
--- hint
`cargo_mass` works out the right number, then hands back something else. Which variable holds the answer?
--- hint
`launch_mass` should add the two masses, not take one from the other.
--- check case | cargo_mass(10, 25) is 250
cargo_mass(10, 25)
=> 250
--- check case | cargo_mass(0, 25) is 0
cargo_mass(0, 25)
=> 0
--- check case | launch_mass(1000, 250) is 1250
launch_mass(1000, 250)
=> 1250
--- check case | The two work together
launch_mass(500, cargo_mass(4, 30))
=> 620

+++ practice | Mission elapsed time
--- task
A mission clock shows hours, minutes and seconds. Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int to_seconds(int hours, int minutes, int seconds)` returns the whole time in seconds. An hour is 3600 seconds and a minute is 60. So `to_seconds(1, 2, 3)` is `3723`.
- `int elapsed(int start_h, int start_m, int start_s, int end_h, int end_m, int end_s)` returns how many seconds passed from the start time to the end time, on the same day. Use `to_seconds` inside it, once for each time. So `elapsed(9, 0, 0, 10, 30, 15)` is `5415`.

Write `to_seconds` above `elapsed`, so the compiler has seen it by the time `elapsed` calls it.
--- starter
int to_seconds(int hours, int minutes, int seconds) {
    return 0;
}

int elapsed(int start_h, int start_m, int start_s, int end_h, int end_m, int end_s) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
int to_seconds(int hours, int minutes, int seconds) {
    int from_hours = hours * 3600;
    int from_minutes = minutes * 60;
    return from_hours + from_minutes + seconds;
}

int elapsed(int start_h, int start_m, int start_s, int end_h, int end_m, int end_s) {
    int start = to_seconds(start_h, start_m, start_s);
    int end = to_seconds(end_h, end_m, end_s);
    return end - start;
}
--- hint
In `to_seconds`, turn the hours into seconds and the minutes into seconds, then add up all three parts. Variables inside the function keep each step clear.
--- hint
A call can go anywhere a value can. In `elapsed`, `int start = to_seconds(start_h, start_m, start_s);` turns the start time into seconds.
--- hint
Do the same for the end time, then return the end in seconds minus the start in seconds.
--- check case | to_seconds(1, 2, 3) is 3723
to_seconds(1, 2, 3)
=> 3723
--- check case | to_seconds(0, 0, 0) is 0
to_seconds(0, 0, 0)
=> 0
--- check case | elapsed(9, 0, 0, 10, 30, 15) is 5415
elapsed(9, 0, 0, 10, 30, 15)
=> 5415
--- check case | The same time twice gives 0
elapsed(13, 45, 20, 13, 45, 20)
=> 0

=== cpp-03 | Arithmetic, and integer division
--- teach
Last lesson you wrote `add` and `fuel_left`. Now you will do real arithmetic inside functions, and meet the one trap in C++ arithmetic that catches everybody.

## A quick recap of functions

From here on, some lessons ask for **functions** rather than a whole program. You write the function; the checker supplies `main()` and calls it, so do **not** write a `main` in those lessons.

```cpp
int add(int a, int b) {
    return a + b;
}
```

The return type comes first, then the name, then the typed **parameters** (`a` and `b`). The values a call passes in, `2` and `3` in `add(2, 3)`, are the **arguments**.

## The five arithmetic operators

An **operator** is a symbol that does something to values. C++ has five for arithmetic:

```cpp
7 + 2    // 9    plus
7 - 2    // 5    minus
7 * 2    // 14   the star: times
7 / 2    // 3    the slash: divided by (see below!)
7 % 2    // 1    the percent sign: remainder
```

They follow the same [[order as in maths|precedence]]: `*`, `/` and `%` happen before `+` and `-`, and parentheses go first of all. So `2 + 3 * 4` is 14, and `(2 + 3) * 4` is 20.

## Dividing two whole numbers

Here is the trap everyone meets: dividing an `int` by an `int` gives an `int`, throwing the remainder away.

```cpp
7 / 2        // 3, not 3.5
```

Think of sharing 7 sweets between 2 people with no cutting: each gets 3 and one is left over. That is **[[integer division|integer-division]]**. The answer is cut down to a whole number, not rounded: `9 / 10` is `0`.

## The remainder

The leftover has its own operator. `%` is read "mod" or "remainder":

```cpp
7 % 2        // 1  (the remainder)
17 % 5       // 2  (17 is 3 fives, which make 15, with 2 left over)
```

## Getting the decimal answer

If at least one side of the `/` is a `double`, the division keeps the decimal part:

```cpp
7.0 / 2      // 3.5
```

When the values are `int` variables, you cannot add `.0` to them. Instead, you convert one of them to a `double` first:

```cpp
static_cast<double>(7) / 2   // 3.5: convert first, then divide
```

Read `static_cast` as "convert to". This kind of conversion is called a [[cast|static-cast]]. The type you want goes in the **angle brackets** `< >`, and the value to convert goes in the parentheses. In a function it looks like `static_cast<double>(total) / count`.

**Watch out:** the order matters. Convert the value, not the answer.

```cpp
static_cast<double>(7 / 2)   // 3.0: the division already threw the .5 away
```

The same thing happens without any cast: if a function returns a `double` but does `return total / count;` with two ints, the division makes `3` first, and only then is it turned into `3.0`. Dividing by zero is a [[different kind of mistake|undefined-behaviour]], and losing the part of a number that does not fit has [[brought down a rocket|ariane-5]].

::: context precedence Who goes first
**Precedence** is the rule for which operator is worked out first when several share a line. C++ uses the order you learned in maths class: parentheses, then multiply, divide and remainder from left to right, then add and subtract from left to right. When you are not sure, add parentheses. They cost nothing, and they make your intent plain to every reader. Arithmetic also comes before `<<`, which is why `std::cout << 2 + 3` prints 5.
:::

::: context integer-division Groups and the leftover
`7 / 2` asks "how many whole groups of 2 fit in 7?" and `7 % 2` asks "how many are left over?". Together they always rebuild the start: 3 × 2 + 1 = 7.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 110" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="12" y="14" width="64" height="36" rx="8" fill="none" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <circle cx="32" cy="32" r="10" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="56" cy="32" r="10" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="88" y="14" width="64" height="36" rx="8" fill="none" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <circle cx="108" cy="32" r="10" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="132" cy="32" r="10" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="164" y="14" width="64" height="36" rx="8" fill="none" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <circle cx="184" cy="32" r="10" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="208" cy="32" r="10" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="262" cy="32" r="10" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="120" y="76" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">3 groups: 7 / 2 is 3</text>
  <text x="262" y="76" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">7 % 2 is 1</text>
  <text x="180" y="100" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">the half is thrown away, not rounded</text>
</svg>
```

With negative numbers, C++ cuts toward zero: `-7 / 2` is `-3`, and `-7 % 2` is `-1`.
:::

::: context static-cast Why the long name
C++ inherited a short way to convert from the older language C: `(double)total`. Modern C++ prefers `static_cast<double>(total)` because it is easy to spot and easy to search for, and because the compiler checks that the conversion makes sense. Conversions are a common source of bugs, so making them loud is a feature. The word "static" means the conversion is worked out by the compiler, before the program runs.
:::

::: context undefined-behaviour When the rules say nothing
Dividing an `int` by zero has no answer, and C++ does not promise what happens: the program may crash, or carry on with nonsense. This is called **undefined behavior**, and it is one of the most dangerous things in C++, because the program might seem fine in testing and fail later. With a `double`, dividing by zero gives a special value on nearly every computer: `1.0 / 0` prints `inf`, short for infinity. Careful code checks a divisor before dividing.
:::

::: context ariane-5 The conversion that cost a rocket
On 4 June 1996, the first Ariane 5 rocket broke up about 37 seconds after lift-off. Its guidance software, reused from the smaller Ariane 4, converted a 64-bit decimal value into a 16-bit whole number. Ariane 5 flew faster, the value was too big to fit, and the conversion failed. The guidance computers shut down and the rocket veered off course. The code was written in a language called Ada, not C++, but the lesson is the same in any language: every time you convert a number, ask whether it can lose something.
:::
--- task
Write two functions. No `main`: the checker has it.

- `double average(int total, int count)`, which returns the exact average, decimals kept. So `average(7, 2)` is `3.5` and `average(10, 4)` is `2.5`. Convert `total` to a `double` with `static_cast` before dividing.
- `int remainder_of(int a, int b)`, which returns the remainder of `a / b`. So `remainder_of(17, 5)` is `2`.
--- starter
// No main() in this lesson: the checker supplies it and calls your functions.

double average(int total, int count) {
    return total / count;
}

int remainder_of(int a, int b) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
// No main() in this lesson: the checker supplies it and calls your functions.

double average(int total, int count) {
    return static_cast<double>(total) / count;
}

int remainder_of(int a, int b) {
    return a % b;
}
--- hint
`total / count` divides two ints, so the .5 is lost before the function hands anything back. One side has to become a `double` first.
--- hint
Convert before dividing: `return static_cast<double>(total) / count;`.
--- hint
For the remainder, use the percent sign: `return a % b;`.
--- check case | average(7, 2) is 3.5
average(7, 2)
=> 3.5
--- check case | average(10, 4) is 2.5
average(10, 4)
=> 2.5
--- check case | remainder_of(17, 5) is 2
remainder_of(17, 5)
=> 2

+++ practice | Samples into racks
--- task
A lab packs rock samples into racks. Each rack holds the same number of samples, and only full racks are shipped. Write two functions. No `main`.

- `int full_racks(int samples, int per_rack)` returns how many racks can be filled completely. `full_racks(17, 5)` is `3`.
- `int samples_left(int samples, int per_rack)` returns how many samples are left over after filling those racks. `samples_left(17, 5)` is `2`.
--- starter
int full_racks(int samples, int per_rack) {
    return 0;
}

int samples_left(int samples, int per_rack) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
int full_racks(int samples, int per_rack) {
    return samples / per_rack;
}

int samples_left(int samples, int per_rack) {
    return samples % per_rack;
}
--- hint
Dividing two ints throws the remainder away. Here that is exactly what you want.
--- hint
The leftover is the remainder, which has its own operator: the percent sign.
--- check case | full_racks(17, 5) is 3
full_racks(17, 5)
=> 3
--- check case | samples_left(17, 5) is 2
samples_left(17, 5)
=> 2
--- check case | Too few for one rack: full_racks(4, 5) is 0
full_racks(4, 5)
=> 0
--- check case | An exact fit leaves 0
samples_left(20, 5)
=> 0

+++ practice | What percent?
--- task
Write `double percent(int part, int whole)` that returns what percent `part` is of `whole`, decimals kept. That is `part` times 100, divided by `whole`.

So `percent(1, 4)` is `25`, `percent(1, 3)` is about `33.333`, and `percent(0, 7)` is `0`. The inputs are ints, so convert before you divide, or the decimals are lost. No `main`.
--- starter
double percent(int part, int whole) {
    return part * 100 / whole;
}
--- solution
double percent(int part, int whole) {
    return static_cast<double>(part) * 100 / whole;
}
--- hint
The starter divides two ints, so `percent(1, 3)` comes out as exactly 33, not 33.333.
--- hint
Convert `part` to a `double` with `static_cast` first. Then every step after it works in decimals.
--- check case | percent(1, 4) is 25
percent(1, 4)
=> 25.0
--- check test | percent(1, 3) keeps its decimals
std::abs(percent(1, 3) - 33.333333333) < 1e-6
--- check case | percent(0, 7) is 0
percent(0, 7)
=> 0.0
--- check case | More than the whole: percent(3, 2) is 150
percent(3, 2)
=> 150.0

+++ practice | The average of three readings
--- task
Write `double average3(int a, int b, int c)` that returns the exact average of three whole-number readings: their sum divided by 3, with the decimals kept.

So `average3(1, 2, 2)` is about `1.6667`, `average3(3, 3, 3)` is `3`, and `average3(-1, 0, 0)` is about `-0.3333`. No `main`.
--- starter
double average3(int a, int b, int c) {
    return a + b + c / 3;
}
--- solution
double average3(int a, int b, int c) {
    return static_cast<double>(a + b + c) / 3;
}
--- hint
The starter has two problems. Division happens before addition, so only `c` is divided by 3. And even with the sum first, int divided by int drops the decimals.
--- hint
Put the sum in parentheses, convert it to a `double` with `static_cast`, and then divide by 3.
--- check test | average3(1, 2, 2) is about 1.6667
std::abs(average3(1, 2, 2) - 5.0 / 3) < 1e-9
--- check case | average3(3, 3, 3) is 3
average3(3, 3, 3)
=> 3.0
--- check test | average3(-1, 0, 0) is about -0.3333
std::abs(average3(-1, 0, 0) + 1.0 / 3) < 1e-9
--- check case | average3(10, 20, 60) is 30
average3(10, 20, 60)
=> 30.0

+++ practice | Boxes needed, rounded up
--- task
Supplies are packed into crates. A crate holds `per_crate` items, and a crate that is only partly full still counts as a crate.

Write `int crates_needed(int items, int per_crate)` that returns how many crates are needed. So `crates_needed(17, 5)` is `4` (three full crates and one with 2 items), `crates_needed(20, 5)` is `4` (exactly four full crates), `crates_needed(1, 5)` is `1`, and `crates_needed(0, 5)` is `0`.

Use only whole-number arithmetic. No `main`.
--- starter
int crates_needed(int items, int per_crate) {
    return items / per_crate + 1;
}
--- solution
int crates_needed(int items, int per_crate) {
    return (items + per_crate - 1) / per_crate;
}
--- hint
The starter's `items / per_crate + 1` looks right for 17 items. Try it on 20 items and on 0 items: it asks for one crate too many.
--- hint
A trick for rounding up: add `per_crate - 1` to the items before dividing. Then any leftover, however small, pushes the answer up by one, but an exact fit does not.
--- hint
Use parentheses so the addition happens before the division: `(items + per_crate - 1) / per_crate`.
--- check case | crates_needed(17, 5) is 4
crates_needed(17, 5)
=> 4
--- check case | An exact fit: crates_needed(20, 5) is 4
crates_needed(20, 5)
=> 4
--- check case | One item needs one crate
crates_needed(1, 5)
=> 1
--- check case | No items need no crates
crates_needed(0, 5)
=> 0

+++ practice | Fix the temperature converter
--- task
`to_fahrenheit` should turn a temperature in Celsius into Fahrenheit, using the rule: times 9, divided by 5, plus 32. So 100 °C is 212 °F, 0 °C is 32 °F, and -40 °C is -40 °F.

Instead, `to_fahrenheit(100.0)` returns `132`. The bug is in the one `return` line: find it and fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
double to_fahrenheit(double celsius) {
    return 9 / 5 * celsius + 32;
}
--- solution
double to_fahrenheit(double celsius) {
    return celsius * 9 / 5 + 32;
}
--- hint
C++ works left to right through `*` and `/`. What is `9 / 5` on its own, when both are ints?
--- hint
`9 / 5` is `1`, so the starter only adds 32. Start with `celsius`, which is a `double`, so every step after it keeps its decimals.
--- check case | 100 C is 212 F
to_fahrenheit(100.0)
=> 212.0
--- check case | 0 C is 32 F
to_fahrenheit(0.0)
=> 32.0
--- check case | -40 C is -40 F
to_fahrenheit(-40.0)
=> -40.0
--- check test | 37 C is about 98.6 F
std::abs(to_fahrenheit(37.0) - 98.6) < 1e-9

+++ practice | Hours, minutes and seconds
--- task
A mission clock counts seconds since launch. Split that count into hours, minutes and seconds, the way a clock shows them. Write three functions. No `main`.

- `int hours_part(int total_seconds)`: the whole hours. `hours_part(3725)` is `1`.
- `int minutes_part(int total_seconds)`: the minutes left over after the whole hours, from 0 to 59. `minutes_part(3725)` is `2`.
- `int seconds_part(int total_seconds)`: the seconds left over after the whole minutes, from 0 to 59. `seconds_part(3725)` is `5`.

An hour is 3600 seconds and a minute is 60.
--- starter
int hours_part(int total_seconds) {
    return 0;
}

int minutes_part(int total_seconds) {
    return total_seconds / 60;
}

int seconds_part(int total_seconds) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
int hours_part(int total_seconds) {
    return total_seconds / 3600;
}

int minutes_part(int total_seconds) {
    return (total_seconds / 60) % 60;
}

int seconds_part(int total_seconds) {
    return total_seconds % 60;
}
--- hint
The hours are the whole number of 3600s in the total. The seconds are what is left after taking out every whole minute.
--- hint
For the minutes: `total_seconds / 60` is the total in whole minutes. That can be more than 59, so keep only the part left after the whole hours: its remainder when divided by 60.
--- check test | 3725 seconds is 1 h 2 min 5 s
hours_part(3725) == 1 && minutes_part(3725) == 2 && seconds_part(3725) == 5
--- check test | 59 seconds is 0 h 0 min 59 s
hours_part(59) == 0 && minutes_part(59) == 0 && seconds_part(59) == 59
--- check test | 86399 seconds is 23 h 59 min 59 s
hours_part(86399) == 23 && minutes_part(86399) == 59 && seconds_part(86399) == 59
--- check test | Exactly one hour is 1 h 0 min 0 s
hours_part(3600) == 1 && minutes_part(3600) == 0 && seconds_part(3600) == 0

=== cpp-03b | Reading a value with std::cin
--- teach
Last lesson your functions got their numbers from the checker. Real programs get data from outside: a sensor, a file, a person typing. This lesson shows how a program reads values in.

## Where input comes from

`std::cout` sends text out. Its partner, `std::cin` (read "standard see-in"), brings text in. It reads from **[[standard input|standard-input]]**: here, whatever is in the **Input** tab under the editor.

## Reading one value

```cpp
int crew;
std::cin >> crew;
std::cout << "Crew: " << crew << "\n";
```

- `int crew;` makes a box with [[no value in it yet|uninitialized]].
- `>>`, two greater-than signs, is read "get from". It gets the next value from the input and puts it into the variable. The arrows point from `cin` toward `crew`, the way the data flows.
- It also converts the text to the variable's type. The input holds the characters `4`; the box gets the whole number 4.

If the Input tab holds `4`, this prints:

```
Crew: 4
```

## Reading two values

You can declare two variables of the same type in one line by separating the names with a comma. And, like `<<`, the `>>` can be chained:

```cpp
int a, b;
std::cin >> a >> b;
std::cout << a + b << "\n";
```

With the input `3 4`, this prints `7`. The arithmetic happens before the printing, so `a + b` goes out as one number.

## Spaces and new lines are skipped

`>>` skips any spaces and line breaks before a value, and stops at the first one after it. So these two inputs are read in exactly the same way:

```
3 4
```

```
3
4
```

Both give `a` the value 3 and `b` the value 4.

## Reading a whole line

`>>` into a `std::string` stops at the first space. With the input `Apollo 11`, reading one string gets only `Apollo`. To read a whole line, spaces included, use `std::getline(std::cin, line)`, which reads a whole line into a `std::string` instead:

```cpp
std::string line;
std::getline(std::cin, line);
std::cout << line << "\n";   // prints Apollo 11
```

`std::string` needs `#include <string>`, as before. Mixing `>>` and `getline` has [[one catch|getline-catch]].

**Watch out:** the arrows for `cin` point the other way from `cout`. Writing `std::cin << a;` by mistake gives a whole screenful of confusing errors, starting with `invalid operands to binary expression`. If you see that near a `cin`, check the arrows: `cout <<` puts to the screen, `cin >>` gets from the input. And if the input holds a word where a number was expected, the read [[fails|failed-read]].

::: context standard-input A stream you already know from bash
Every program starts with three streams: standard input (in), standard output (out) and standard error (for error messages). You met them in the Terminal course. In bash, `echo "3 4" | ./program` pipes text into a program's standard input, and `./program < numbers.txt` feeds it a file. `std::cin` reads whatever arrives that way. In this app, the Input tab plays the part of that file.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 110" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="8" y="30" width="92" height="44" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="54" y="24" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">Input tab</text>
  <text x="54" y="57" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3 4</text>
  <line x1="102" y1="52" x2="126" y2="52" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="132,52 124,47 124,57" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="134" y="30" width="120" height="44" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="194" y="57" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">std::cin &gt;&gt; a &gt;&gt; b</text>
  <line x1="256" y1="44" x2="280" y2="36" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="256" y1="60" x2="280" y2="70" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <rect x="284" y="20" width="66" height="28" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="317" y="39" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">a: 3</text>
  <rect x="284" y="58" width="66" height="28" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="317" y="77" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">b: 4</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context uninitialized A box with leftovers in it
A variable declared without a value, like `int crew;`, holds whatever happened to be in that piece of memory before. Reading it before you put a value in is undefined behavior: you might get 0, or a random-looking number. Here it is safe, because `std::cin >> crew` fills it before anything reads it. MISRA C, a coding standard used in cars and aircraft, forbids reading a variable before it has been given a value, and many teams give every variable a starting value when they declare it.
:::

::: context getline-catch The leftover line break
After `std::cin >> n` reads a number, the line break that followed it is still waiting in the input. A `std::getline` straight after that reads up to that line break and gets an empty line. If you read a number and then a line of text, skip the leftover first, for example with `std::cin >> std::ws;` (read "skip white space") before the `getline`.
:::

::: context failed-read When the input is not a number
If `std::cin >> crew` meets text like `four` where it needs a whole number, the read fails. The variable is set to 0, and `std::cin` switches into a failed state in which every later `>>` does nothing until the problem is cleared. Programs that read real data, from sensors or files, always check whether each read worked. The next lesson shows how to check.
:::
--- task
The Input tab holds two whole numbers:

```
7 5
```

Read them into two `int` variables, `a` and `b`, with `std::cin >>`, then print exactly:

```
7 + 5 = 12
```

Build the line from the variables: print `a`, then `" + "`, then `b`, then `" = "`, then `a + b`, then `"\n"`. Do not type the answer 12 yourself; let the program work it out.
--- stdin
7 5
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int a, b;
    // Read a and b from the input, then print the line.
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int a, b;
    std::cin >> a >> b;
    std::cout << a << " + " << b << " = " << a + b << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
First read both numbers in one statement: `std::cin >> a >> b;`.
--- hint
Then print with one `std::cout`, chaining the variables and the text pieces with `<<`.
--- hint
The print line is `std::cout << a << " + " << b << " = " << a + b << "\n";`.
--- check output | Prints the sum line
7 + 5 = 12
--- check source | Reads with std::cin
std::cin\s*>>
--- check source absent | Works out 12 instead of typing it
\b12\b

+++ practice | A mission's length
--- task
The Input tab holds a mission's name on the first line, and on the second line how long the mission lasted, in seconds:

```

703115
```

Read the name with `std::getline`, because it contains a space. Then read the seconds into an `int` with `>>`. Print the length in hours, minutes and seconds, like this:

```
Apollo 11: 195 h 18 min 35 s
```

An hour is 3600 seconds and a minute is 60. The minutes and seconds are what is left over, so each is from 0 to 59. Work every number out from the input.

703115
