=== cpp-04 | Reading input
--- teach
Last lesson you read two numbers, because you knew there would be two. But often you do not know how many values are coming: a sensor sends readings until it stops. This lesson reads every number until the input runs out, and keeps a count, a total and the largest along the way.

As a reminder: `std::cin >> variable` reads the next value from standard input (whatever is in the **Input** box under the editor), skipping spaces and newlines, and converting to the variable's type:

```cpp
int a, b;
std::cin >> a >> b;
std::cout << a + b << "\n";
```

## A read also answers "did it work?"

`std::cin >> n` does two jobs. It reads a value, and it also reports whether it managed to. Used as a **condition** (a question with a yes-or-no answer), `std::cin >> n` is true while it managed to read something, and false once there is nothing left to read.

## Repeating with while

A **loop** repeats a block of code. The `while` loop repeats its block for as long as its condition is true:

```cpp
while (condition) {
    // this block runs again and again while condition is true
}
```

Put the read in as the condition, and you get a loop that reads every number until the input runs out:

```cpp
int n;
while (std::cin >> n) {
    std::cout << n << "\n";
}
```

With the input `4 8 11`, the block runs three times and prints each number on its own line. On the fourth try there is [[nothing left to read|end-of-input]], the condition is false, and the loop stops. [[Loops|while-loop]] get a whole lesson of their own later.

## Counting with ++

To count the numbers, start a counter at 0 before the loop and add one each time round:

```cpp
int count = 0;
while (std::cin >> n) {
    count++;
}
```

`++`, read "plus plus", [[adds one|plus-plus]] to a variable. `count++;` does the same as `count = count + 1;`.

## Adding up with +=

To total the numbers, start a sum at 0 and add each number to it:

```cpp
sum += n;
```

`+=`, read "plus-equals", adds the value on the right to the variable on the left. It is [[shorthand|compound-assignment]] for `sum = sum + n;`. A total that grows one value at a time like this is called a [[running total|running-total]].

## Keeping the largest with if

To remember the largest number, you only change it when a bigger one arrives. `if` runs the statement after it only when its condition is true (it too gets a whole lesson later):

```cpp
if (n > max) max = n;
```

`>` means "is greater than". So this reads: "if n is greater than max, max gets n".

## Starting from the first number

What should `max` start at? The safe answer is the first number itself. So the rule becomes: "if this is the first number, or it is bigger than max, max gets n":

```cpp
if (count == 0 || n > max) max = n;
```

- `==`, two equals signs, asks "is equal to?". One `=` stores a value; two `==` compare. Mixing them up is a classic bug.
- `||`, two vertical bars, means [[or|short-circuit]]: the whole condition is true if either side is true.

Do this check before `count++`, while `count` is still 0 for the first number.

**Watch out:** do not start `max` at 0 and compare from there. It works for `4 8 11 2 5`, but if every input is [[negative|negative-readings]], like `-3 -7`, no number is ever greater than 0, and your program reports a largest value of 0 that was never in the input.

::: context end-of-input How the input runs out
When `std::cin` reaches the end of its input, the next read fails and the stream is marked "end of file" (EOF for short), even when the input is not a file. In this app, that happens after the last character in the Input box. If you run a program in a real terminal and type the input yourself, you signal the end by pressing Ctrl-D at the start of a line on Linux or macOS. The failed read makes `std::cin >> n` false, and the loop ends.
:::

::: context while-loop Loops, and loops that must end
The `while` loop comes back in the Loops lesson, alongside the counting `for` loop. Loops are where programs spend most of their time, and a loop that never ends freezes the program. That is why the NASA JPL "Power of Ten" rules for safety-critical code, written by Gerard Holzmann in 2006, require every loop to have a fixed upper limit that a checking tool can prove. Reading "until the input runs out" is fine here, but flight code would also cap how many values it accepts.
:::

::: context plus-plus The name of the language
`++` adds one, and `--` subtracts one. The language's name is a programmer's joke built on this: C++ is "C, plus one", one step beyond the language C it grew from. The name was suggested by Rick Mascitti in 1983. You will see `++` in almost every loop you write from now on.
:::

::: context compound-assignment A whole family of shortcuts
`+=` has relatives for every arithmetic operator: `-=` subtracts, `*=` multiplies, `/=` divides and `%=` keeps the remainder. So `fuel -= 20;` means `fuel = fuel - 20;`, and `total *= 2;` doubles `total`. They say "change this variable by that much" in one short line, and they save you from typing the variable's name twice, which is one less place for a typo.
:::

::: context running-total Following the loop by hand
Here is what the three variables hold after each number of the input `4 8 11 2 5` is read. Writing a table like this, called a trace, is how programmers check a loop by hand.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="20" y="8" width="320" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0"/>
  <g font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">
    <text x="70" y="26">read n</text>
    <text x="150" y="26">count</text>
    <text x="230" y="26">sum</text>
    <text x="300" y="26">max</text>
    <text x="70" y="54" fill="#6c7a93">start</text>
    <text x="150" y="54">0</text>
    <text x="230" y="54">0</text>
    <text x="300" y="54">0</text>
    <text x="70" y="80">4</text>
    <text x="150" y="80">1</text>
    <text x="230" y="80">4</text>
    <text x="300" y="80">4</text>
    <text x="70" y="106">8</text>
    <text x="150" y="106">2</text>
    <text x="230" y="106">12</text>
    <text x="300" y="106">8</text>
    <text x="70" y="132">11</text>
    <text x="150" y="132">3</text>
    <text x="230" y="132">23</text>
    <text x="300" y="132">11</text>
    <text x="70" y="158">2</text>
    <text x="150" y="158">4</text>
    <text x="230" y="158">25</text>
    <text x="300" y="158">11</text>
    <text x="70" y="184">5</text>
    <text x="150" y="184">5</text>
    <text x="230" y="184">30</text>
    <text x="300" y="184" fill="#b4232c">11</text>
  </g>
  <line x1="20" y1="34" x2="340" y2="34" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <line x1="20" y1="192" x2="340" y2="192" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
</svg>
```

The last row is the answer: count 5, sum 30, max 11.
:::

::: context short-circuit Or, and why the order helps
`a || b` is true when `a` is true, when `b` is true, or both. C++ checks the left side first, and if that is already true it does not look at the right side at all, because the answer cannot change. This is called short-circuit evaluation. Here it means that on the very first number, `count == 0` is true and `n > max` is never asked, which is good, because `max` has no sensible value yet.
:::

::: context negative-readings Real data goes below zero
Plenty of real measurements are negative: the temperature of a spacecraft in Earth's shadow, a descent rate, the altitude of a probe below a reference level. Code that quietly assumes "everything is at least 0" works in every test with positive numbers, then gives a wrong answer the first time real data dips below zero. Starting `max` from the first value read, instead of from a made-up number, removes the assumption entirely.
:::
--- task
The Input tab holds a list of whole numbers (here `4 8 11` on one line and `2 5` on the next). Read them all with a `while (std::cin >> n)` loop, and keep:

- `count`: how many numbers there were,
- `sum`: all of them added up,
- `max`: the largest, starting from the first number read (not from 0).

After the loop, print them in exactly this form:

```
count 5, sum 30, max 11
```

Your program should work for any list of whole numbers, including one where every number is negative.
--- stdin
4 8 11
2 5
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    int count = 0;
    int sum = 0;
    int max = 0;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        if (count == 0 || n > max) max = n;
        count++;
        sum += n;
    }
    std::cout << "count " << count << ", sum " << sum << ", max " << max << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Declare `int count = 0;`, `int sum = 0;` and `int max = 0;` before the loop. `while (std::cin >> n) { … }` then runs once per number.
--- hint
Inside the loop: `if (count == 0 || n > max) max = n;` first, then `count++;` and `sum += n;`. Starting `max` from the first number, rather than from 0, keeps negative inputs working.
--- hint
After the loop's closing `}`: `std::cout << "count " << count << ", sum " << sum << ", max " << max << "\n";`.
--- check output | Prints count, sum and max for the input
count 5, sum 30, max 11
--- check source | Reads with std::cin
std::cin\s*>>

+++ practice | The coldest reading
--- task
The Input tab holds temperature readings, whole numbers, as many as there are:

```
14 9 22
11 17
```

Read them all with a `while (std::cin >> t)` loop. Keep `count`, how many readings there were, and `lowest`, the smallest reading, starting from the first reading (not from 0). After the loop, print:

```
5 readings, lowest 9
```

Your program should work for any list, including one where every reading is above 0.
--- stdin
14 9 22
11 17
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int t;
    int count = 0;
    int lowest = 0;
    while (std::cin >> t) {
        count++;
    }
    std::cout << count << " readings, lowest " << lowest << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int t;
    int count = 0;
    int lowest = 0;
    while (std::cin >> t) {
        if (count == 0 || t < lowest) lowest = t;
        count++;
    }
    std::cout << count << " readings, lowest " << lowest << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
This is the "largest so far" idea turned around: change `lowest` whenever a reading is smaller. `<` means "is less than".
--- hint
Starting `lowest` at 0 and comparing from there would report 0, which was never read. Take the first reading instead: `if (count == 0 || t < lowest) lowest = t;`, before `count++`.
--- check output | Prints the count and the lowest reading
5 readings, lowest 9
--- check source | Reads in a while loop
while\s*\(\s*std::cin\s*>>

+++ practice | How many over the limit?
--- task
The first number in the Input tab is a limit. Every number after it is a reading:

```
100
95 101 130 100 99 250
```

Read the limit first with `std::cin >> limit;`. Then read the readings in a `while` loop, and count how many there are and how many are **over** the limit (strictly greater: a reading equal to the limit is not over). Print:

```
3 of 6 readings over 100
```
--- stdin
100
95 101 130 100 99 250
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int limit;
    int n;
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int limit;
    int n;
    int count = 0;
    int over = 0;
    std::cin >> limit;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        count++;
        if (n > limit) over++;
    }
    std::cout << over << " of " << count << " readings over " << limit << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Two counters, both starting at 0: one goes up for every reading, the other only when `n > limit`.
--- hint
The limit is read once, before the loop. The loop then reads everything after it.
--- check output | Counts the readings over the limit
3 of 6 readings over 100
--- check source | Reads in a while loop
while\s*\(\s*std::cin\s*>>
--- check source absent | Reads the limit instead of typing it
\b100\b

+++ practice | The average reading
--- task
The Input tab holds whole-number readings:

```
4 8 11
2 5 10
```

Read them all in a `while` loop, keeping a count and a sum. After the loop, print the count, the sum and the exact average (the sum divided by the count, decimals kept):

```
count 6, sum 40, average 6.66667
```

`std::cout` shows 6 significant digits, so the average comes out as `6.66667` on its own. The count and the sum are ints, so convert before you divide, as in the arithmetic lesson.
--- stdin
4 8 11
2 5 10
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    int count = 0;
    int sum = 0;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        count++;
        sum += n;
    }
    std::cout << "count " << count << ", sum " << sum << ", average " << sum / count << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    int count = 0;
    int sum = 0;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        count++;
        sum += n;
    }
    double average = static_cast<double>(sum) / count;
    std::cout << "count " << count << ", sum " << sum << ", average " << average << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
`sum / count` divides two ints, so the starter prints `6`, not `6.66667`.
--- hint
Convert the sum first: `static_cast<double>(sum) / count`.
--- check output | Prints count, sum and exact average
count 6, sum 40, average 6.66667
--- check source | Converts with static_cast
static_cast<double>

+++ practice | Every reading below zero
--- task
The Input tab holds whole-number readings from a probe in deep space. Every one of them is below zero:

```
-5 -12
-3 -9
```

Read them all and print the smallest, the largest, and the range (the largest minus the smallest):

```
min -12, max -3, range 9
```

Both `min` and `max` must start from the first reading, not from 0. Your program should also work for a single reading, where the range is 0.
--- stdin
-5 -12
-3 -9
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    int min = 0;
    int max = 0;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        if (n < min) min = n;
        if (n > max) max = n;
    }
    std::cout << "min " << min << ", max " << max << ", range " << max - min << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    int count = 0;
    int min = 0;
    int max = 0;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        if (count == 0 || n < min) min = n;
        if (count == 0 || n > max) max = n;
        count++;
    }
    std::cout << "min " << min << ", max " << max << ", range " << max - min << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Starting `max` at 0 means no reading below zero can ever beat it, so the starter reports a largest reading of 0.
--- hint
Keep a count, and let the first reading set both `min` and `max`: `if (count == 0 || n > max) max = n;`, and the same shape for `min`. Then `count++`.
--- check output | Prints min, max and range for all-negative readings
min -12, max -3, range 9
--- check source | Uses the first reading as the start
count\s*==\s*0

+++ practice | Fix the negative totals
--- task
This program reads whole numbers and should print how many there were, their sum, and the largest. With this input:

```
-8 -3
-11 -7
```

it should print:

```
count 4, sum -29, max -3
```

Instead it prints `count 4, sum -7, max 0`. There are two bugs, each on one line. Fix them.
--- stdin
-8 -3
-11 -7
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    int count = 0;
    int sum = 0;
    int max = 0;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        if (n > max) max = n;
        count++;
        sum = n;
    }
    std::cout << "count " << count << ", sum " << sum << ", max " << max << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    int count = 0;
    int sum = 0;
    int max = 0;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        if (count == 0 || n > max) max = n;
        count++;
        sum += n;
    }
    std::cout << "count " << count << ", sum " << sum << ", max " << max << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
A sum of `-7` is just the last number. Look at the line that should add each number on: `=` replaces, `+=` adds.
--- hint
A max of 0 is a number that was never read. The first reading has to set `max`, whatever it is.
--- check output | Prints the right count, sum and max
count 4, sum -29, max -3
--- check source | Adds each number to the sum
sum\s*\+=\s*n

+++ practice | Climbs and drops
--- task
The Input tab holds a rocket's altitude, one reading per second:

```
100 180 240 235
300 290 290
```

From one reading to the next, the rocket climbs (the new reading is higher), drops (the new reading is lower) or stays level. Work out:

- `best`: the biggest climb between two readings in a row (here it is from 100 to 180, a climb of 80),
- `drops`: how many times the reading went down (here 240 to 235 and 300 to 290, so 2).

Print:

```
biggest climb 80, drops 2
```

Read the first reading into `prev` before the loop. In the loop, compare each new reading with `prev`, then set `prev` to the new reading, ready for the next one.
--- stdin
100 180 240 235
300 290 290
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int prev;
    int n;
    std::cin >> prev;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
    }
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int prev;
    int n;
    int count = 0;
    int best = 0;
    int drops = 0;
    std::cin >> prev;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        if (count == 0 || n - prev > best) best = n - prev;
        if (prev > n) drops++;
        count++;
        prev = n;
    }
    std::cout << "biggest climb " << best << ", drops " << drops << "\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
The change from one reading to the next is `n - prev`. The biggest climb is the largest of those changes, so use the "largest so far" pattern on `n - prev`.
--- hint
Let the first change set `best`, as with `max`: keep a count and use `count == 0 ||`. A drop is when `prev > n`: add one to `drops` then.
--- hint
At the end of each pass, `prev = n;` so the next reading is compared with this one.
--- check output | Finds the biggest climb and counts the drops
biggest climb 80, drops 2
--- check source | Keeps the previous reading
prev\s*=\s*n\s*;

=== cpp-05 | Making decisions
--- teach
Last lesson your program read numbers with `std::cin` and kept going `while` there was more input. Inside that loop it already made a small decision: "is this number bigger than the biggest one so far?" This lesson is about decisions — how a program looks at a value and picks which lines to run.

Picture a launch director a minute before liftoff. She asks one question: "Is the weather good?" If yes, the countdown goes on. If not, it holds. A program decides in exactly that way.

### if: run a block only when something is true

The **`if` statement** means "run these lines only if this is true":

```cpp
if (fuel_percent < 10) {
    std::cout << "low fuel\n";
}
```

Read it aloud: "if fuel percent is less than 10, print low fuel." The part inside the round brackets `( )` is the **[[condition|condition-bool]]** — a question whose answer is true or false. The curly braces `{ }` hold the **block** — the lines that run only when the answer is true. When the answer is false, the program skips the whole block and carries on after the closing `}`.

### else: the other way

Add **`else`**, meaning "otherwise", for lines that run when the condition is false:

```cpp
if (fuel_percent < 10) {
    std::cout << "low fuel\n";
} else {
    std::cout << "fuel ok\n";
}
```

Exactly one of the two blocks runs. Never both, and never neither.

### else if: more than two choices

For three or more choices, chain the questions with **[[else if|else-if]]**, meaning "otherwise, if this other thing is true". The program asks the questions from top to bottom, runs the block of the *first* one that is true, and skips all the rest:

```cpp
if (temperature > 30) {
    std::cout << "hot\n";
} else if (temperature > 15) {
    std::cout << "mild\n";
} else {
    std::cout << "cold\n";
}
```

With `temperature` at 20: is 20 more than 30? No. Is 20 more than 15? Yes — so it prints `mild` and never looks at the last `else`.

With `temperature` at 35 it prints `hot`. 35 is more than 15 too, but the program already stopped at the first true question. That is why the order matters: ask about the highest band first.

### Comparing two values

The questions are built from **comparison operators** — symbols that compare two values and give back true or false:

| Symbol | Read it as | An example that is true |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `==` | is equal to | `3 == 3` |
| `!=` | is not equal to | `3 != 4` |
| `<` | is less than | `2 < 5` |
| `<=` | is less than or equal to | `5 <= 5` |
| `>` | is greater than | `9 > 1` |
| `>=` | is greater than or equal to | `70 >= 70` |

Look closely at the last row. `>=` includes the edge: `70 >= 70` is true, but `70 > 70` is false. Getting each [[boundary|boundary-values]] on the right side is most of the work in a decision.

### Combining questions

Three **logical operators** join two conditions or flip one:

- `&&`, read "and": true only when both sides are true.
- `||`, read "or": true when at least one side is true. (The `|` key is the vertical bar, usually above Enter.)
- `!`, read "not": turns true into false and false into true.

```cpp
if (fuel_ok && weather_ok) {
    std::cout << "go\n";
}
if (!engines_armed || wind > 40) {
    std::cout << "hold\n";
}
```

The first prints `go` only when both are true. Read the second as "if the engines are not armed, or the wind is over 40, print hold". C++ also [[stops checking early|short-circuit]] once it knows the answer.

### Deciding inside a function

In your task the decision lives inside a function (you wrote your first ones in the lesson on writing your own function) that gives back a word. `return` hands the value back *and* leaves the function right away, so nothing below it runs:

```cpp
#include <string>

std::string fuel_status(int percent) {
    if (percent < 10) {
        return "empty";
    }
    if (percent < 50) {
        return "low";
    }
    return "plenty";
}
```

`fuel_status(5)` gives `"empty"`, `fuel_status(30)` gives `"low"` and `fuel_status(80)` gives `"plenty"`. This one asks about the lowest band first, using `<`. The second `if` and the last `return` need no `else` in front of them: the program only reaches those lines when every `if` above them did not return.

**Watch out:** one equals sign `=` **assigns** (puts a value into a variable); two, `==`, **compares**. `if (score = 70)` does not ask whether the score is 70. It *sets* the score to 70, and then treats 70 as true. The program still compiles and quietly does the wrong thing. clang, the [[compiler that builds your code|compiler-warnings]] on this page, prints a warning about it — one reason to read warnings, not only errors.

::: context condition-bool Every condition is a bool
In the variables lesson you met `bool`, the type that holds only `true` or `false`. A condition is any expression that works out to a bool. `temperature > 30` becomes `true` or `false` first, and only then does `if` look at it. So you can store a condition in a variable and use it later: `bool hot = temperature > 30;` and then `if (hot) { … }`.

Numbers can stand in as conditions too: 0 counts as false, and every other number counts as true. That rule is why `if (score = 70)` sneaks past the compiler — the assignment's value is 70, which is not 0, so the block always runs.
:::

::: context else-if Not a new word
C++ has no special `elseif` keyword. `else if` is an ordinary `else` whose "otherwise" part is another `if`. Written out in full, `else if (b) { … }` means `else { if (b) { … } }` — the braces are left off because the inner `if` is a single statement. That is why you can chain as many as you like: each `else` holds the next question. Python shortens it to `elif`; JavaScript writes it the same way C++ does.
:::

::: context boundary-values Test the edges
Most bugs in decisions sit exactly on an edge: a score of 70 when the rule says "70 or more". Testers call this **boundary testing**: for every edge, try the value one below it, the value on it, and the value one above it — 69, 70 and 71. That is why the checks for this lesson try `grade(70)` on its own. A filled dot on a number line means "this value belongs to the band on its right":

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 130" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="30" y="40" width="180" height="26" fill="#f2b880" opacity="0.5"/>
  <rect x="210" y="40" width="30" height="26" fill="#8fb8f0" opacity="0.6"/>
  <rect x="240" y="40" width="90" height="26" fill="#1d6fd1" opacity="0.35"/>
  <text x="120" y="58" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">fail</text>
  <text x="225" y="32" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">close</text>
  <text x="285" y="58" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">pass</text>
  <line x1="30" y1="80" x2="330" y2="80" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="30" y1="74" x2="30" y2="86" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="330" y1="74" x2="330" y2="86" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <circle cx="210" cy="80" r="5" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <circle cx="240" cy="80" r="5" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="30" y="102" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">0</text>
  <text x="210" y="102" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">60</text>
  <text x="240" y="102" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">70</text>
  <text x="330" y="102" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">100</text>
  <text x="180" y="122" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">60 and 70 each belong to the band on their right</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context short-circuit The right side may never run
With `&&`, if the left side is false the whole answer is already false, so C++ never even works out the right side. With `||`, if the left side is true the answer is already true, so again the right side is skipped. This is called **short-circuit evaluation**. Programmers use it as a guard: `if (count != 0 && total / count > 5)` never divides by zero, because when `count` is 0 the division is never reached. Put the safety check on the left.
:::

::: context compiler-warnings Warnings are free bug reports
The **compiler** is the program that turns your C++ into a program the computer can run. An *error* means it could not build your program at all. A *warning* means it built it, but something looks like a mistake. Warnings cost nothing to read and often point straight at a bug.

Flight software takes this seriously. In 2006 Gerard Holzmann of NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory published "The Power of Ten", ten rules for code that must not fail. The last rule: turn on every compiler warning at the strictest setting from the first day, and fix the code until there are none at all.
:::
--- task
Write a function `std::string grade(int score)` that returns:

- `"pass"` for a score of 70 or more,
- `"close"` for a score from 60 to 69,
- `"fail"` for anything lower.

The starter already has `#include <string>` at the top. No `main` in this lesson: the checker supplies it and calls `grade` with scores like 85, 70, 64 and 12.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string grade(int score) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string grade(int score) {
    if (score >= 70) {
        return "pass";
    } else if (score >= 60) {
        return "close";
    }
    return "fail";
}
--- hint
Use an `if` / `else if` chain, the way `fuel_status` does, and ask about the highest band first.
--- hint
Test the highest band first: `if (score >= 70) { return "pass"; }`. Then `else if (score >= 60)` for `"close"`. Use `>=` so that 70 and 60 land in the right band.
--- hint
The shape is: `if (score >= 70)` returns `"pass"`, `else if (score >= 60)` returns `"close"`, and a last `return "fail";` after the chain catches everything lower.
--- check case | 85 passes
grade(85)
=> "pass"
--- check case | 70 passes (the boundary)
grade(70)
=> "pass"
--- check case | 64 is close
grade(64)
=> "close"
--- check case | 12 fails
grade(12)
=> "fail"

+++ practice | Above, below or on zero
--- task
Write `std::string sign_of(int n)` that returns:

- `"positive"` when `n` is greater than 0,
- `"negative"` when `n` is less than 0,
- `"zero"` when `n` is 0.

The starter already has `#include <string>`. No `main`: the checker calls `sign_of` with numbers like 5, -3 and 0.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string sign_of(int n) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string sign_of(int n) {
    std::string answer = "zero";
    if (n > 0) {
        answer = "positive";
    } else if (n < 0) {
        answer = "negative";
    }
    return answer;
}
--- hint
Three choices means an `if`, an `else if`, and something for the last case.
--- hint
Ask `n > 0` first, then `n < 0`. If neither is true, `n` must be 0. You can return from each branch, or keep the answer in a `std::string` variable and return it once at the end.
--- check case | 5 is positive
sign_of(5)
=> "positive"
--- check case | -3 is negative
sign_of(-3)
=> "negative"
--- check case | 0 is zero
sign_of(0)
=> "zero"

+++ practice | The largest of three
--- task
Write `int max_of_three(int a, int b, int c)` that returns the largest of the three numbers. Two or all three of them may be equal, and they may be negative.

For example, `max_of_three(1, 5, 3)` is `5`, `max_of_three(7, 7, 2)` is `7`, and `max_of_three(-4, -9, -1)` is `-1`. No `main`.
--- starter
int max_of_three(int a, int b, int c) {
    return a;
}
--- solution
int max_of_three(int a, int b, int c) {
    int best = a;
    if (b > best) {
        best = b;
    }
    if (c > best) {
        best = c;
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
Keep a "best so far", starting with `a`. Then look at `b`, then at `c`.
--- hint
`if (b > best) { best = b; }` and the same for `c`. After both checks, `best` is the largest.
--- check case | max_of_three(1, 5, 3) is 5
max_of_three(1, 5, 3)
=> 5
--- check case | The last one can be largest
max_of_three(2, 3, 9)
=> 9
--- check case | A tie: max_of_three(7, 7, 2) is 7
max_of_three(7, 7, 2)
=> 7
--- check case | All negative: max_of_three(-4, -9, -1) is -1
max_of_three(-4, -9, -1)
=> -1

+++ practice | Leap years
--- task
A year has 366 days (it is a **leap year**) when:

- it can be divided by 4 with nothing left over, **and** it cannot be divided by 100 with nothing left over,
- **or** it can be divided by 400 with nothing left over.

So 2024 is a leap year, 2023 is not, 1900 is not (it divides by 100 but not by 400), and 2000 is (it divides by 400).

Write `bool is_leap_year(int year)` that returns `true` for a leap year and `false` otherwise. Remember `%`, the remainder, from the arithmetic lesson. No `main`.
--- starter
bool is_leap_year(int year) {
    return year % 4 == 0;
}
--- solution
bool is_leap_year(int year) {
    return (year % 4 == 0 && year % 100 != 0) || year % 400 == 0;
}
--- hint
"Can be divided by 4 with nothing left over" is `year % 4 == 0`. "Cannot be divided by 100" is `year % 100 != 0`.
--- hint
Join the first two with `&&`, put that in parentheses, and join it to the 400 rule with `||`.
--- check case | 2024 is a leap year
is_leap_year(2024)
=> true
--- check case | 2023 is not
is_leap_year(2023)
=> false
--- check case | 1900 is not (divides by 100, not by 400)
is_leap_year(1900)
=> false
--- check case | 2000 is (divides by 400)
is_leap_year(2000)
=> true

+++ practice | Go or hold
--- task
A launch director uses these rules. Write `std::string launch_call(int wind_kmh, int temp_c)` that returns:

- `"hold: wind"` if the wind is 40 km/h or more,
- otherwise `"hold: temperature"` if the temperature is below 2 or above 35,
- otherwise `"go"`.

The edges count: a wind of exactly 40 is a hold, and temperatures of exactly 2 or exactly 35 are fine. If both the wind and the temperature are bad, the answer is `"hold: wind"`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string launch_call(int wind_kmh, int temp_c) {
    return "go";
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string launch_call(int wind_kmh, int temp_c) {
    if (wind_kmh >= 40) {
        return "hold: wind";
    } else if (temp_c < 2 || temp_c > 35) {
        return "hold: temperature";
    }
    return "go";
}
--- hint
The wind is checked first, so it wins when both are bad. Use `>=` so that exactly 40 is a hold.
--- hint
"Below 2 or above 35" is `temp_c < 2 || temp_c > 35`. With `<` and `>`, the values 2 and 35 themselves are fine.
--- check case | Calm and mild: go
launch_call(10, 20)
=> "go"
--- check case | Wind of exactly 40 is a hold
launch_call(40, 20)
=> "hold: wind"
--- check case | Wind of 39 is fine
launch_call(39, 20)
=> "go"
--- check case | Exactly 2 and exactly 35 degrees are fine
launch_call(5, 2) + launch_call(5, 35)
=> "gogo"
--- check case | 1 degree is too cold, 36 too hot
launch_call(5, 1) + "/" + launch_call(5, 36)
=> "hold: temperature/hold: temperature"
--- check case | Both bad: the wind wins
launch_call(55, -10)
=> "hold: wind"

+++ practice | Fix the battery gauge
--- task
`battery_level` should return:

- `"full"` for 80 percent or more,
- `"ok"` for 21 to 79 percent,
- `"low"` for 20 percent or less.

It never returns `"full"`: `battery_level(95)` gives `"ok"`. And `battery_level(80)` should be `"full"` too. Fix the order of the questions and the edge. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string battery_level(int percent) {
    if (percent > 20) {
        return "ok";
    } else if (percent > 80) {
        return "full";
    }
    return "low";
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string battery_level(int percent) {
    if (percent >= 80) {
        return "full";
    } else if (percent > 20) {
        return "ok";
    }
    return "low";
}
--- hint
The first true question wins. 95 is more than 20, so the starter stops at `"ok"` before it ever asks about 80.
--- hint
Ask about the highest band first. And "80 or more" is `>=`, not `>`.
--- check case | 95 is full
battery_level(95)
=> "full"
--- check case | Exactly 80 is full
battery_level(80)
=> "full"
--- check case | 50 is ok
battery_level(50)
=> "ok"
--- check case | 21 is ok, 20 is low
battery_level(21) + "/" + battery_level(20)
=> "ok/low"

+++ practice | What kind of triangle?
--- task
Write `std::string triangle_type(int a, int b, int c)` that takes three side lengths and returns:

- `"invalid"` if any side is 0 or less, or if the sides cannot make a triangle. They cannot when one side is as long as the other two added together, or longer. For example 1, 2, 3 is invalid: 1 + 2 is only 3, so the shape lies flat.
- otherwise `"equilateral"` if all three sides are equal,
- otherwise `"isosceles"` if exactly two sides are equal (any two),
- otherwise `"scalene"`.

No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string triangle_type(int a, int b, int c) {
    if (a == b && b == c) {
        return "equilateral";
    }
    return "scalene";
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string triangle_type(int a, int b, int c) {
    if (a <= 0 || b <= 0 || c <= 0) {
        return "invalid";
    }
    if (a + b <= c || a + c <= b || b + c <= a) {
        return "invalid";
    }
    if (a == b && b == c) {
        return "equilateral";
    }
    if (a == b || b == c || a == c) {
        return "isosceles";
    }
    return "scalene";
}
--- hint
Check for "invalid" first, before anything else. Each side must be greater than 0, and each side must be shorter than the other two added together.
--- hint
One side is too long when, for example, `a + b <= c`. Check all three ways round, joined with `||`.
--- hint
After the equilateral check, "exactly two equal" is simply `a == b || b == c || a == c`, because the all-equal case has already returned.
--- check case | 3, 3, 3 is equilateral
triangle_type(3, 3, 3)
=> "equilateral"
--- check case | 3, 3, 5 and 5, 3, 3 are isosceles
triangle_type(3, 3, 5) + "/" + triangle_type(5, 3, 3)
=> "isosceles/isosceles"
--- check case | 4, 7, 4 is isosceles
triangle_type(4, 7, 4)
=> "isosceles"
--- check case | 3, 4, 5 is scalene
triangle_type(3, 4, 5)
=> "scalene"
--- check case | 1, 2, 3 lies flat: invalid
triangle_type(1, 2, 3)
=> "invalid"
--- check case | A side of 0 or less is invalid
triangle_type(0, 1, 1) + "/" + triangle_type(-2, 5, 5)
=> "invalid/invalid"
--- check case | One side far too long is invalid
triangle_type(10, 2, 3)
=> "invalid"

=== cpp-06 | Loops
--- teach
Last lesson your program chose which lines to run. This lesson it runs the same lines again and again. That repeating is called a **loop**.

Think of a countdown: "10, 9, 8, …, 1, liftoff." Nobody writes ten separate print lines for that. You describe the pattern once — "say the number, then make it one smaller, until you reach zero" — and the loop repeats it for you. You have met one loop already: `while (std::cin >> n)` in the lesson on reading input repeated once for every number in the input.

### The counting for loop

The **`for` loop** repeats a block a counted number of times:

```cpp
for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) {
    std::cout << i << "\n";
}
```

This prints 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5, one per line. Inside the round brackets are three parts, split by semicolons `;`:

1. **Start:** `int i = 1` runs once, before anything else. It makes a counter [[variable called i|loop-variable]] and sets it to 1.
2. **Keep going while:** `i <= 5` ("i is less than or equal to 5") is [[checked before every pass|loop-order]]. While it is true, the block runs. The first time it is false, the loop ends.
3. **Step:** `i++` runs after every pass. [[The ++ sign|plus-plus]], read "plus plus", adds one to `i`. It does the same as `i = i + 1`.

Each trip through the block is one **pass** (programmers also say an **iteration** — one repeat). Follow the counter:

| `i` | Is `i <= 5`? | What happens |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | yes | prints 1, then `i` becomes 2 |
| 2 | yes | prints 2, then `i` becomes 3 |
| … | … | … |
| 5 | yes | prints 5, then `i` becomes 6 |
| 6 | no | the loop ends |

The block ran 5 times, and the counter stopped at 6.

A loop can count down, too. `i--`, read "minus minus", takes one away:

```cpp
for (int i = 3; i >= 1; i--) {
    std::cout << "T-minus " << i << "\n";
}
std::cout << "Liftoff!\n";
```

That prints `T-minus 3`, `T-minus 2`, `T-minus 1`, then `Liftoff!`.

### while: repeat until something changes

A **`while` loop** has only the middle part: it repeats as long as its condition is true.

```cpp
int fuel = 20;
while (fuel > 0) {
    std::cout << "burning, fuel " << fuel << "\n";
    fuel = fuel - 7;
}
```

This prints fuel 20, then 13, then 6. After that `fuel` is -1, so `fuel > 0` is false and the loop ends. Use `for` when you know how many passes you want. Use `while` when you repeat until something happens. Either way, the block must change something the condition depends on, or you get an [[infinite loop|infinite-loop]].

### break: leave the loop early

**`break`** jumps straight out of the loop, even if its condition is still true:

```cpp
for (int i = 1; i <= 100; i++) {
    if (i * i > 50) {
        break;
    }
    std::cout << i << "\n";
}
```

This prints 1 to 7. At `i` = 8, `8 * 8` is 64, more than 50, so `break` ends the loop before 8 is printed.

### continue: skip to the next pass

**`continue`** skips the rest of this pass and goes on to the next one:

```cpp
for (int i = 1; i <= 6; i++) {
    if (i % 2 == 0) {
        continue;
    }
    std::cout << i << "\n";
}
```

This prints 1, 3 and 5. Remember `%` from the arithmetic lesson, the remainder: `i % 2 == 0` means "dividing `i` by 2 leaves nothing over", so `i` is even, and the even numbers are skipped.

### A decision inside a loop

Put an `if` chain from the decisions lesson inside a loop, and the program makes a fresh decision on every pass:

```cpp
for (int n = 1; n <= 4; n++) {
    if (n % 2 == 0) {
        std::cout << "even\n";
    } else {
        std::cout << n << "\n";
    }
}
```

This prints `1`, `even`, `3`, `even`. "`n` is a multiple of 3" works the same way: `n % 3 == 0`. That is all you need for your task, a counting game called [[FizzBuzz|fizzbuzz]]. Remember the rule from the decisions lesson: in an `else if` chain the *first* true question wins.

**Watch out:** `<` and `<=` differ by one pass. `for (int i = 1; i < 5; i++)` stops at 4, because `5 < 5` is false. When a loop runs one time too many or one too few, it is called an [[off-by-one error|off-by-one]]. Check the last value by hand: "when `i` is 5, is the condition true?"

::: context loop-variable Why everyone calls it i
A loop counter is an ordinary `int` variable, and you may call it anything — `n`, `second`, `stage`. The name `i` is a habit from mathematics, where i, j and k label the items in a list. Early FORTRAN, a language from the 1950s, even treated every variable starting with the letters I to N as a whole number by default, so programmers reached for `i` first.

One C++ detail: a variable made in the start part of a `for` exists only inside that loop. After the closing `}` it is gone, and using `i` there is a compile error.
:::

::: context loop-order What happens on each pass
The order is always the same: start once, then check, run the block, step, and back to the check. The check comes *before* each pass, so if it is false the very first time, the block never runs at all — `for (int i = 10; i <= 5; i++)` prints nothing.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="a" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0 0 L10 5 L0 10 z" fill="#1f2a44"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <rect x="20" y="16" width="120" height="32" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="80" y="37" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">start: int i = 1</text>
  <rect x="20" y="82" width="120" height="32" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="80" y="103" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">check: i &lt;= 5 ?</text>
  <rect x="210" y="82" width="130" height="32" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="275" y="103" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">block: print i</text>
  <rect x="210" y="16" width="130" height="32" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="275" y="37" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">step: i++</text>
  <rect x="20" y="152" width="120" height="32" rx="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="80" y="173" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">after the loop</text>
  <line x1="80" y1="48" x2="80" y2="80" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#a)"/>
  <line x1="140" y1="104" x2="208" y2="104" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#a)"/>
  <text x="174" y="124" font-size="11" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">true</text>
  <line x1="275" y1="82" x2="275" y2="50" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#a)"/>
  <path d="M210 32 L170 32 L170 90 L142 90" fill="none" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#a)"/>
  <line x1="80" y1="114" x2="80" y2="150" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="1.5" marker-end="url(#a)"/>
  <text x="96" y="136" font-size="11" fill="#b4232c">false</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context plus-plus Where C++ got its name
`++` adds one to a variable, and `--` takes one away. `+=` is the bigger cousin: `total += 5` adds 5 to `total`. The `++` sign came from C, the older language C++ grew out of. Bjarne Stroustrup began C++ in 1979 under the name "C with Classes"; in 1983 it was renamed C++, a programmer's joke meaning "C, plus one" — one step better than C.
:::

::: context infinite-loop Loops that never end
If nothing inside a `while` loop ever makes its condition false, the loop runs forever: the program looks frozen and has to be stopped from outside.

Flight software is strict about this. NASA JPL's "Power of Ten" rules for safety-critical code say every loop must have a fixed upper limit on its passes, so that a checking tool can prove it ends. The one allowed exception is a loop meant never to end, like the main loop of a flight computer that runs for the whole mission — and for that one, it must be provable that it never stops.
:::

::: context fizzbuzz A children's game turned test
FizzBuzz began as a counting game for teaching children division: players take turns counting, and say "Fizz" for multiples of 3, "Buzz" for multiples of 5 and "FizzBuzz" for multiples of both. In 2007 two blog posts, by Imran Ghory and Jeff Atwood, made it famous as a quick test in programming job interviews.

A handy fact: a number is a multiple of both 3 and 5 exactly when it is a multiple of 15, because 3 and 5 share no factor. So `n % 15 == 0` asks "both?" in one step.
:::

::: context off-by-one Count the fence posts
A fence 30 meters long with a post every 10 meters needs 4 posts, not 3: one at each end plus two between. Counting the gaps when you meant the posts (or the other way round) is the oldest counting mistake there is.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 110" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="60" y1="50" x2="300" y2="50" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="3"/>
  <rect x="55" y="30" width="10" height="40" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="135" y="30" width="10" height="40" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="215" y="30" width="10" height="40" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <rect x="295" y="30" width="10" height="40" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="100" y="44" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">10 m</text>
  <text x="180" y="44" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">10 m</text>
  <text x="260" y="44" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">10 m</text>
  <text x="180" y="96" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">3 gaps, 4 posts</text>
</svg>
```

In loops it shows up as `<` versus `<=`. It comes back in the vector lesson: a list's positions start at 0, so a list of 5 items uses positions 0 to 4, and the loop is written `i < 5`.
:::
--- task
Write a whole program (with `main`, as in the lesson on reading input) that goes through the numbers 1 to 15 with a loop and prints one line for each number:

- `FizzBuzz` if the number is a multiple of both 3 and 5,
- otherwise `Fizz` if it is a multiple of 3,
- otherwise `Buzz` if it is a multiple of 5,
- otherwise the number itself.

The output starts `1`, `2`, `Fizz`, `4`, `Buzz` and ends with `14`, `FizzBuzz`.
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    for (int n = 1; n <= 15; n++) {
        if (n % 15 == 0) {
            std::cout << "FizzBuzz\n";
        } else if (n % 3 == 0) {
            std::cout << "Fizz\n";
        } else if (n % 5 == 0) {
            std::cout << "Buzz\n";
        } else {
            std::cout << n << "\n";
        }
    }
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Start with the loop: `for (int n = 1; n <= 15; n++) { … }`. Inside it goes an `if` / `else if` chain that prints one line per number.
--- hint
`n % 3 == 0` means n is a multiple of 3. A multiple of both 3 and 5 is a multiple of 15 — check 15 first, because the first true question in the chain wins.
--- hint
The chain: `if (n % 15 == 0)` prints `"FizzBuzz\n"`, `else if (n % 3 == 0)` prints `"Fizz\n"`, `else if (n % 5 == 0)` prints `"Buzz\n"`, and the last `else` prints `n << "\n"`.
--- check output | Prints FizzBuzz from 1 to 15
1
2
Fizz
4
Buzz
Fizz
7
8
Fizz
Buzz
11
Fizz
13
14
FizzBuzz
--- check source | Uses a loop
\b(for|while)\b

+++ practice | The seven times table
--- task
Write a whole program (with `main`) that uses a `for` loop to print the seven times table, from `7 x 1` to `7 x 10`, one line each:

```
7 x 1 = 7
7 x 2 = 14
7 x 3 = 21
7 x 4 = 28
7 x 5 = 35
7 x 6 = 42
7 x 7 = 49
7 x 8 = 56
7 x 9 = 63
7 x 10 = 70
```

Let the loop work out every answer. Do not type the answers yourself.
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    std::cout << "7 x 1 = 7\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    for (int i = 1; i <= 10; i++) {
        std::cout << "7 x " << i << " = " << 7 * i << "\n";
    }
    return 0;
}
--- hint
The loop counter goes from 1 to 10: `for (int i = 1; i <= 10; i++)`.
--- hint
Each line is `"7 x "`, then `i`, then `" = "`, then `7 * i`, then `"\n"`.
--- check output | Prints the whole table

7 x 3 = 21

+++ practice | The first square past a limit
--- task
Write `int first_square_over(int limit)` that returns the smallest whole number `n`, starting from 1, whose square `n * n` is greater than `limit`.

For example, `first_square_over(50)` is `8`, because 7 × 7 is 49 (not over 50) and 8 × 8 is 64. `first_square_over(100)` is `11`, because 10 × 10 is exactly 100, which is not over. And `first_square_over(0)` is `1`.

Use a `while` loop. No `main`.
--- starter
int first_square_over(int limit) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
int first_square_over(int limit) {
    int n = 1;
    while (n * n <= limit) {
        n++;
    }
    return n;
}
--- hint
Start `n` at 1 and keep making it one bigger while its square is **not yet** over the limit.
--- hint
"Not over" is `n * n <= limit`. When the loop stops, `n` is the answer.
--- check case | first_square_over(50) is 8
first_square_over(50)
=> 8
--- check case | Exactly 100 is not over: first_square_over(100) is 11
first_square_over(100)
=> 11
--- check case | first_square_over(99) is 10
first_square_over(99)
=> 10
--- check case | first_square_over(0) is 1
first_square_over(0)
=> 1

+++ practice | Stop at zero, skip the glitches
--- task
The Input tab holds sensor readings. A negative reading is a glitch and must be skipped. A reading of `0` means "end of data": stop there, even though more numbers follow it.

```
5 -2 7 3
-9 4 0 8 6
```

Read the readings in a `while (std::cin >> n)` loop. Use `break` to stop at the 0, and `continue` to skip the negative readings. Add up the good readings and count them. Print:

```
sum 19 from 4 readings
```

The 0 itself is not a reading, and nothing after it counts.
--- stdin
5 -2 7 3
-9 4 0 8 6
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    int sum = 0;
    int count = 0;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        sum += n;
        count++;
    }
    std::cout << "sum " << sum << " from " << count << " readings\n";
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int n;
    int sum = 0;
    int count = 0;
    while (std::cin >> n) {
        if (n == 0) {
            break;
        }
        if (n < 0) {
            continue;
        }
        sum += n;
        count++;
    }
    std::cout << "sum " << sum << " from " << count << " readings\n";
    return 0;
}
--- hint
Put the two checks at the top of the loop body, before the reading is added: first the 0 check with `break`, then the negative check with `continue`.
--- hint
`if (n == 0) { break; }` leaves the loop. `if (n < 0) { continue; }` skips the rest of this pass, so the glitch is never added.
--- check output | Adds the good readings before the 0
sum 19 from 4 readings
--- check source | Uses break
\bbreak\s*;
--- check source | Uses continue
\bcontinue\s*;

+++ practice | How many digits?
--- task
Write `int count_digits(int n)` that returns how many digits `n` has when written out. The minus sign of a negative number is not a digit.

So `count_digits(12345)` is `5`, `count_digits(7)` is `1`, `count_digits(-45)` is `2`, and `count_digits(0)` is `1`: zero is written with one digit.

Chop digits off the end with `n = n / 10` until nothing is left. No `main`.
--- starter
int count_digits(int n) {
    int count = 0;
    while (n > 0) {
        n = n / 10;
        count++;
    }
    return count;
}
--- solution
int count_digits(int n) {
    int count = 1;
    n = n / 10;
    while (n != 0) {
        n = n / 10;
        count++;
    }
    return count;
}
--- hint
Try the starter on 0 and on -45 by hand. With `n > 0`, the loop never runs for either, so it returns 0.
--- hint
Every number has at least one digit, so start the count at 1 and chop that digit off first. Dividing a negative number by 10 moves it toward 0, just like a positive one, so loop while `n != 0`.
--- check case | count_digits(12345) is 5
count_digits(12345)
=> 5
--- check case | count_digits(7) is 1
count_digits(7)
=> 1
--- check case | Zero has one digit
count_digits(0)
=> 1
--- check case | count_digits(-45) is 2
count_digits(-45)
=> 2
--- check case | count_digits(1000000000) is 10
count_digits(1000000000)
=> 10

+++ practice | Fix the range sum
--- task
`sum_range(a, b)` should add up every whole number from `a` to `b`, **both included**. So `sum_range(1, 4)` is 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10, and `sum_range(5, 5)` is just `5`. If `a` is bigger than `b`, there are no numbers to add, and the answer is `0`.

Instead, `sum_range(1, 4)` returns `6` and `sum_range(5, 5)` returns `0`. Fix the one wrong line. No `main`.
--- starter
int sum_range(int a, int b) {
    int total = 0;
    for (int i = a; i < b; i++) {
        total += i;
    }
    return total;
}
--- solution
int sum_range(int a, int b) {
    int total = 0;
    for (int i = a; i <= b; i++) {
        total += i;
    }
    return total;
}
--- hint
Check the last pass by hand: when `i` is 4 and `b` is 4, is the condition true?
--- hint
The loop stops one number too early. `<` leaves `b` out; `<=` keeps it.
--- check case | sum_range(1, 4) is 10
sum_range(1, 4)
=> 10
--- check case | sum_range(5, 5) is 5
sum_range(5, 5)
=> 5
--- check case | sum_range(-2, 2) is 0
sum_range(-2, 2)
=> 0
--- check case | a bigger than b gives 0
sum_range(3, 2)
=> 0

+++ practice | Counting primes
--- task
A **prime** is a whole number greater than 1 that can only be divided evenly by 1 and by itself. The first primes are 2, 3, 5, 7, 11 and 13. The number 9 is not prime, because 3 divides it evenly.

Write `int count_primes(int limit)` that returns how many primes there are from 2 up to `limit`, including `limit` itself if it is prime. So `count_primes(10)` is `4` (2, 3, 5 and 7), `count_primes(2)` is `1`, and `count_primes(1)` is `0`.

The checker also tries `count_primes(20000)`, so your answer has to be quick. A number `n` that has a divisor has one no bigger than its square root, so you only need to try divisors `d` while `d * d <= n`. No `main`.
--- starter
int count_primes(int limit) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
int count_primes(int limit) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int n = 2; n <= limit; n++) {
        bool prime = true;
        for (int d = 2; d * d <= n; d++) {
            if (n % d == 0) {
                prime = false;
                break;
            }
        }
        if (prime) {
            count++;
        }
    }
    return count;
}
--- hint
Use one loop over every `n` from 2 to `limit`, and inside it a second loop that tries divisors `d`. A loop can sit inside another loop.
--- hint
Start each `n` with `bool prime = true;`. If `n % d == 0` for some `d`, it is not prime: set `prime` to `false` and `break` out of the inner loop.
--- hint
The inner loop is `for (int d = 2; d * d <= n; d++)`. After it, add one to the count if `prime` is still `true`.
--- check case | count_primes(10) is 4
count_primes(10)
=> 4
--- check case | count_primes(2) is 1
count_primes(2)
=> 1
--- check case | count_primes(1) is 0
count_primes(1)
=> 0
--- check case | count_primes(100) is 25
count_primes(100)
=> 25
--- check case | count_primes(20000) is 2262
count_primes(20000)
=> 2262

+++ practice | Predict the loop
--- task
Read this program carefully, but do **not** run it:

```cpp
for (int i = 10; i >= 1; i -= 3) {
    if (i % 2 == 0) {
        continue;
    }
    if (i < 3) {
        break;
    }
    std::cout << i << "\n";
}
std::cout << "done\n";
```

`i -= 3` means `i = i - 3`. Work out by hand what it prints. Then write a program whose `main` prints exactly that output, using plain `std::cout` lines. Your program must not use a loop: the point is to work it out yourself.

    // Print what the program above prints.
    return 0;
}
--- solution

    std::cout << "7\n";
    std::cout << "done\n";

--- hint
Follow `i` in a table: it takes the values 10, 7, 4 and 1. For each one, check the two `if`s in order.

=== cpp-07 | Functions
--- teach
Since you wrote `add`, you have been writing functions like `average` and `grade`, and the checker has been calling them. This lesson looks at functions properly: what each part means, functions that give nothing back, and how to pick a type big enough for the answer.

A **function** is a named piece of work you can run whenever you need it. It is like a recipe card with a title: write "pancakes" once, and from then on "make pancakes" is enough. Running a function is called **calling** it.

### The parts, one more time

```cpp
bool is_positive(int n) {
    return n > 0;
}
```

- `bool` is the **return type**: the kind of value that comes back. Here, true or false.
- `is_positive` is the name. Names for yes-or-no functions often start with `is_`, so a call reads like a question.
- `(int n)` is the **parameter** list: each input's type and name.
- `return n > 0;` works out the condition `n > 0` (a bool, from the decisions lesson) and hands it back.

Now `is_positive(5)` is `true` and `is_positive(-3)` is `false`. You can [[call it|function-call]] anywhere a bool fits, such as `if (is_positive(altitude)) { … }`.

The parameter types and the return type are part of what the function *is*. `is_positive` takes an `int` and gives a `bool`, and the compiler checks every call against that. `is_positive("hello")` is a compile error, caught before the program ever runs.

### Define it before you use it

The compiler reads your file from top to bottom. A function must be [[declared before it is used|declaration]] — it has to appear above any line that calls it:

```cpp
long long square(long long x) {
    return x * x;
}

long long fourth_power(long long x) {
    return square(square(x));   // fine: square is already above
}
```

Swap the two around and clang stops with an error like "use of undeclared identifier 'square'" — it has not met `square` yet.

### void: a function that gives nothing back

**`void`** — "nothing" — as the return type means the function does its work and returns no value:

```cpp
void announce(int seconds) {
    std::cout << "T-minus " << seconds << "\n";
}
```

`announce(10);` prints `T-minus 10`. There is nothing to catch, so `int x = announce(10);` is a compile error. Inside a `void` function, `return;` on its own leaves early.

### Big numbers: int and long long

Every whole-number type is a box of fixed size. An **`int`** holds numbers up to 2,147,483,647 — about 2 billion — and down to about minus 2 billion. A **`long long`** is a [[much bigger box|int-sizes]]: up to 9,223,372,036,854,775,807, about 9 × 10¹⁸ (a 9 followed by 18 more digits). Pick `long long` when values can get big.

That is why `square` above takes and returns a `long long`. Squares grow fast: 100,000 squared is 10,000,000,000 — ten billion, far too big for an `int`. Putting a number into a box too small for it is called **[[overflow|overflow]]**, and the answer that comes out is garbage.

### Adding up and multiplying in a loop

Many functions build an answer in a loop. Two shortcuts help:

- `total += i`, read "plus equals", means `total = total + i`.
- `result *= i`, read "times equals", means `result = result * i`.

```cpp
long long sum_to(int n) {
    long long total = 0;
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
        total += i;
    }
    return total;
}
```

`sum_to(4)` works out 0 + 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10. The sum starts at 0, because adding 0 changes nothing.

Your task needs the multiplying version: the **factorial** of n, written n! and read "n factorial", is 1 × 2 × 3 × … × n. So 5! = 1 × 2 × 3 × 4 × 5 = 120. A product has to start at 1, because multiplying by 1 changes nothing (starting at 0 would make everything 0). By agreement [[0! is 1|zero-factorial]]. Factorials grow very fast: 13! is already too big for an `int`, and 20! = 2,432,902,008,176,640,000 is close to the top of a `long long`.

A function can even call itself. That is called **recursion**, and it works as long as some case stops it. It gets its own lesson next.

**Watch out:** to test for even, use `n % 2 == 0`. Do not test for odd with `n % 2 == 1`: in C++ the remainder of a negative number is negative, so `-7 % 2` is `-1`, not `1`, and that test would call -7 "not odd". `n % 2 == 0` is right for negative numbers too.

::: context function-call What happens during a call
When the program reaches `is_positive(altitude)`, it pauses where it is and jumps into the function. The parameter `n` is made as a fresh variable holding a **copy** of the value you passed in (the argument). The body runs until `return`. Then the program jumps back, and the returned value takes the place of the whole call — as if you had written `true` right there.

Because `n` is a copy, changing it inside the function does not change `altitude` outside. The references lesson shows how to let a function change the caller's own variable, with references.
:::

::: context declaration Declarations and header files
You can also tell the compiler about a function before writing its body. The first line on its own, ending in a semicolon, is a **declaration**: `long long square(long long x);` says "a function with this name and these types exists; the body comes later." After that line, calls anywhere below it compile.

This is what a header file is for. `#include <iostream>` pastes in a file full of declarations like this one, which is how the compiler knows about `std::cout` without you writing it. Large programs, flight software included, keep their declarations in headers so many files can share them.
:::

::: context int-sizes Boxes of bits
Memory is made of **bits**, each a 0 or a 1, grouped eight at a time into **bytes**. On almost every computer today, including this page, an `int` is 4 bytes (32 bits) and a `long long` is 8 bytes (64 bits). One bit records the sign, so an `int` reaches 2³¹ − 1 = 2,147,483,647 and a `long long` reaches 2⁶³ − 1.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="12" y="42" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">int</text>
  <g fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44">
    <rect x="96" y="24" width="28" height="28"/><rect x="124" y="24" width="28" height="28"/>
    <rect x="152" y="24" width="28" height="28"/><rect x="180" y="24" width="28" height="28"/>
  </g>
  <text x="96" y="70" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">4 bytes = 32 bits, up to 2,147,483,647</text>
  <text x="12" y="112" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">long long</text>
  <g fill="#1d6fd1" fill-opacity="0.45" stroke="#1f2a44">
    <rect x="96" y="94" width="28" height="28"/><rect x="124" y="94" width="28" height="28"/>
    <rect x="152" y="94" width="28" height="28"/><rect x="180" y="94" width="28" height="28"/>
    <rect x="208" y="94" width="28" height="28"/><rect x="236" y="94" width="28" height="28"/>
    <rect x="264" y="94" width="28" height="28"/><rect x="292" y="94" width="28" height="28"/>
  </g>
  <text x="96" y="140" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93">8 bytes = 64 bits, up to 9,223,372,036,854,775,807</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context overflow A rocket lost to a number too big
On 4 June 1996 the first Ariane 5 rocket veered off course about 37 seconds after liftoff and was destroyed. Its guidance software converted a 64-bit number with a decimal point, linked to the rocket's sideways speed, into a 16-bit whole number. On Ariane 5's faster flight path the value was too big for 16 bits. The conversion failed, and both guidance computers — the main one and its backup, running the same code — shut down. The code had been reused from Ariane 4, whose flights never produced a value that large.

In C++, overflowing an `int` is **undefined behavior**: the language makes no promise at all about what happens. Choosing a big enough type is the fix.
:::

::: context zero-factorial Why 0! is 1
Factorials count arrangements. Three books can stand on a shelf in 3! = 6 orders; n things can be lined up in n! orders. How many ways can you arrange no books at all? Exactly one: the empty shelf. So 0! = 1.

It also keeps the pattern n! = n × (n − 1)! working. At n = 1 it says 1! = 1 × 0!, and since 1! is 1, 0! must be 1. That pattern is the heart of the next lesson, on recursion.
:::
--- task
Write two functions. No `main`: the checker supplies it.

- `bool is_even(int n)` returns `true` when `n` is even and `false` when it is odd.
- `long long factorial(int n)` returns the product 1 × 2 × … × n, with `factorial(0)` equal to 1. The checker tries `factorial(5)` (which is 120), `factorial(0)` and `factorial(20)`, so the answer must be a `long long`.
--- starter
bool is_even(int n) {
    return false;
}

long long factorial(int n) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
bool is_even(int n) {
    return n % 2 == 0;
}

long long factorial(int n) {
    long long result = 1;
    for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++) {
        result *= i;
    }
    return result;
}
--- hint
`is_even`: a number is even when dividing by 2 leaves no remainder. `factorial`: build the answer in a loop, like `sum_to`, but multiplying.
--- hint
`is_even` can be one line: `return n % 2 == 0;`. For `factorial`, start `long long result = 1;` — not 0.
--- hint
`factorial`: start `result` at 1 and multiply by 2, 3, … n in a loop: `for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++) { result *= i; }`, then `return result;`. When n is 0 the loop never runs, so you get 1.
--- check test | is_even works both ways
is_even(10) && !is_even(7)
--- check case | factorial(5) is 120
factorial(5)
=> 120
--- check case | factorial(0) is 1
factorial(0)
=> 1
--- check case | factorial(20) fits in a long long
factorial(20)
=> 2432902008176640000LL

+++ practice | Inside the safe range
--- task
Write `bool in_range(int value, int low, int high)` that returns `true` when `value` is from `low` to `high`, both ends included, and `false` otherwise.

So `in_range(5, 1, 10)` is `true`, `in_range(1, 1, 10)` and `in_range(10, 1, 10)` are `true` (the ends count), and `in_range(0, 1, 10)` is `false`. No `main`.
--- starter
bool in_range(int value, int low, int high) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
bool in_range(int value, int low, int high) {
    return value >= low && value <= high;
}
--- hint
Two conditions must both be true: the value is not below `low`, and it is not above `high`.
--- hint
Hand back `value >= low && value <= high` directly. It is already a `bool`.
--- check case | 5 is in 1 to 10
in_range(5, 1, 10)
=> true
--- check case | Both ends count
in_range(1, 1, 10) && in_range(10, 1, 10)
=> true
--- check case | 0 is below the range
in_range(0, 1, 10)
=> false
--- check case | 11 is above the range
in_range(11, 1, 10)
=> false

+++ practice | A bar of hashes
--- task
Write `void print_bar(std::string label, int n)` that prints the label, a colon and a space, then `n` hash signs `#`, then a new line. It returns nothing.

So `print_bar("fuel", 3)` prints:

```
fuel: ###
```

and `print_bar("empty", 0)` prints `empty: ` with no hashes. The checker calls it a few times and compares everything printed. The starter includes `<iostream>` and `<string>`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

void print_bar(std::string label, int n) {
    std::cout << label << "\n";
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>
#include <string>

void print_bar(std::string label, int n) {
    std::cout << label << ": ";
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        std::cout << "#";
    }
    std::cout << "\n";
}
--- hint
Print the label and `": "` first. Then a loop prints one `#` on each pass, `n` passes in all. The new line comes last, after the loop.
--- hint
`for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)` runs exactly `n` times, and not at all when `n` is 0.
--- check test | print_bar runs for fuel, empty and oxygen
[] { print_bar("fuel", 3); print_bar("empty", 0); print_bar("oxygen", 5); return true; }()
--- check output | Prints the three bars
fuel: ###
empty:
oxygen: #####
--- check includes | Zero hashes still prints the label
