With `for (int x : v)`, the loop changes a copy of each element. A function that changes a vector in place needs both: `std::vector<int>& v` and `for (int& x : v)`.

### Swapping needs a spare

To swap the juice in two glasses, you need a third, empty glass. [[Swapping two variables|std-swap]] works the same way: a **temporary** variable holds one value while you move the other.

```cpp
int a = 1;
int b = 2;
int tmp = a;   // tmp is 1
a = b;         // a is 2
b = tmp;       // b is 1
```

Without `tmp`, `a = b; b = a;` leaves both at 2: the first line wipes out the old value of `a` before anything saved it.

### Reading the checks

The checks in this lesson look like this:

```cpp
[] { int a = 1, b = 2; swap_values(a, b); return a == 2 && b == 1; }()
```

That is a **lambda**: a small function with no name, [[defined and called on the spot|lambda-parts]]. The square brackets `[]` start it, the curly braces `{ … }` hold its body, and the `()` at the very end calls it right away. Inside, `int a = 1, b = 2;` declares two ints on one line. A lambda lets a check set up variables, call your function and test the result. You will write lambdas yourself later; here you only need to read them.

::: context bound-for-life A reference is bound for life
A reference must be tied to a variable the moment it is made: `int& b;` on its own does not compile. After that it can never be moved to a different variable. `b = c;` does not make `b` a name for `c`; it copies the value of `c` into `a`, the variable `b` names. And there is no such thing as a reference to nothing. Pointers, in the last lesson of this course, are the tool that can change what it points at and can point at nothing, which is exactly why they need more care.
:::

::: context one-box-two-names Copies and references, side by side
Pass by value makes a second box with the same number in it; the function scribbles on its own box. Pass by reference makes no new box at all: the function's name is a second label stuck on the caller's box, so every change lands in the one box there is.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 160" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="85" y="20" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">by value: int n</text>
  <rect x="25" y="50" width="50" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="95" y="50" width="50" height="40" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="50" y="75" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">4</text>
  <text x="120" y="75" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">5</text>
  <text x="50" y="42" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">x</text>
  <text x="120" y="42" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">n (a copy)</text>
  <text x="85" y="120" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">two boxes: x stays 4</text>
  <line x1="180" y1="30" x2="180" y2="140" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-dasharray="4 3"/>
  <text x="270" y="20" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">by reference: int&amp; n</text>
  <rect x="245" y="50" width="50" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="270" y="75" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">5</text>
  <text x="250" y="42" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">x</text>
  <text x="290" y="42" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">n</text>
  <text x="270" y="120" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">one box, two names: x is 5</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context cost-of-a-copy What a copy costs
An `int` takes 4 bytes on nearly every computer today, so a vector of one million ints holds 4 million bytes, about 4 megabytes. Passing it by value copies all of it on every call: the computer has to find 4 more megabytes and fill them, then throw them away when the function ends. Called inside a loop, that adds up fast. A reference is about the size of one memory address, 8 bytes on a 64-bit computer, however big the vector is.
:::

::: context return-or-change Return it, or change it?
A function can hand back a result with `return`, or change a variable the caller passed by reference. The C++ Core Guidelines, a widely used rule book edited by C++'s creator Bjarne Stroustrup, advise preferring return values for results, because `int y = f(x);` shows at a glance what changes. Pass by non-const reference is for when changing the caller's object is the job itself, as when you update a big vector in place instead of building a new one.
:::

::: context std-swap The library's own swap
Swapping two values is so common that the standard library has it ready-made: `std::swap(a, b)`, from the header `<utility>`, does exactly what you write in this lesson, and works for any type. Sorting a list is mostly a long series of swaps. Writing your own `swap_values` once is still worth it, because it shows why the parameters must be references: a swap done on copies would swap nothing.
:::

::: context lambda-parts The parts of a lambda
The name comes from the Greek letter lambda, used by the logician Alonzo Church in the 1930s for functions without names. In C++ the `[]` is the **capture list**: it can bring in variables from outside the lambda, and here it is empty. The `{ … }` is the body, like any function's. A lambda is a value, so the `()` after it calls it, exactly as `add_one(x)` calls a named function; an empty `()` means "no arguments". Lambdas come back later in the course, when you hand a small rule to a library function, such as how to sort a list.
:::
--- task
Write two functions that change their caller's variables. No `main`: the checker supplies it.

- `void double_all(std::vector<int>& v)` doubles every element of `v` **in place**, so the caller's own vector changes: `{1, 2, 3}` becomes `{2, 4, 6}`.
- `void swap_values(int& a, int& b)` swaps the two values: if `a` was 1 and `b` was 2, afterwards `a` is 2 and `b` is 1.

The starter has the right names, but it takes everything by value, so nothing it does reaches the caller. Fix the parameters and the loop, and fill in the swap.
--- starter
#include <vector>

void double_all(std::vector<int> v) {
    for (int x : v) {
        x = x * 2;
    }
}

void swap_values(int a, int b) {
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

void double_all(std::vector<int>& v) {
    for (int& x : v) {
        x = x * 2;
    }
}

void swap_values(int& a, int& b) {
    int tmp = a;
    a = b;
    b = tmp;
}
--- hint
Reread the Watch out: in `double_all` there are two places that each make a copy. Which two?
--- hint
Both the parameter and the loop variable need `&`: `std::vector<int>& v` and `for (int& x : v)`. `swap_values` needs it on both parameters: `int& a, int& b`.
--- hint
Swapping needs a temporary: `int tmp = a; a = b; b = tmp;`
--- check test | double_all changes the caller's vector
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 2, 3}; double_all(v); return v == std::vector<int>{2, 4, 6}; }()
--- check test | swap_values swaps
[] { int a = 1, b = 2; swap_values(a, b); return a == 2 && b == 1; }()

+++ practice | Burn some fuel
--- task
Write `void burn(double& fuel, double used)` that takes `used` away from the caller's `fuel`. Fuel can never go below 0: if `used` is more than what is left, `fuel` becomes exactly `0`.

So with `fuel` at 100, `burn(fuel, 30)` leaves it at 70. With `fuel` at 10, `burn(fuel, 25)` leaves it at 0. The function returns nothing: it changes the caller's variable. No `main`.
--- starter
void burn(double fuel, double used) {
    fuel = fuel - used;
}
--- solution
void burn(double& fuel, double used) {
    fuel = fuel - used;
    if (fuel < 0) {
        fuel = 0;
    }
}
--- hint
The starter changes its own copy of `fuel`. One `&` makes it the caller's variable.
--- hint
After taking `used` away, check whether `fuel` went below 0, and if so set it to 0.
--- check test | 100 minus 30 leaves 70
[] { double f = 100; burn(f, 30); return f == 70; }()
--- check test | Burning more than is left leaves 0
[] { double f = 10; burn(f, 25); return f == 0; }()
--- check test | Burning 0 changes nothing
[] { double f = 42.5; burn(f, 0); return f == 42.5; }()

+++ practice | Clamp every reading
--- task
Write `void clamp_all(std::vector<int>& v, int low, int high)` that changes the caller's vector **in place**: every element below `low` becomes `low`, every element above `high` becomes `high`, and the rest stay as they are.

So `{-5, 3, 12}` clamped to 0 and 10 becomes `{0, 3, 10}`. An empty vector stays empty. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

void clamp_all(std::vector<int>& v, int low, int high) {
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x < low) x = low;
        if (x > high) x = high;
    }
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

void clamp_all(std::vector<int>& v, int low, int high) {
    for (int& x : v) {
        if (x < low) x = low;
        if (x > high) x = high;
    }
}
--- hint
The parameter is already a reference. But the loop variable is a copy of each element.
--- hint
Make the loop variable a reference too: `for (int& x : v)`.
--- check test | {-5, 3, 12} clamped to 0..10
[] { std::vector<int> v{-5, 3, 12}; clamp_all(v, 0, 10); return v == std::vector<int>{0, 3, 10}; }()
--- check test | The edges themselves stay
[] { std::vector<int> v{0, 10, 5}; clamp_all(v, 0, 10); return v == std::vector<int>{0, 10, 5}; }()
--- check test | An empty vector stays empty
[] { std::vector<int> v; clamp_all(v, 0, 10); return v.size() == 0; }()

+++ practice | Two answers at once
--- task
A function can only `return` one value. But it can hand back more through reference parameters.

Write `void min_max(const std::vector<int>& v, int& lo, int& hi)` that sets `lo` to the smallest element of `v` and `hi` to the largest. The vector is not empty. The vector is only read, so it stays `const`; `lo` and `hi` are written, so they are plain references. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

void min_max(const std::vector<int>& v, int lo, int hi) {
    lo = v[0];
    hi = v[0];
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

void min_max(const std::vector<int>& v, int& lo, int& hi) {
    lo = v[0];
    hi = v[0];
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x < lo) lo = x;
        if (x > hi) hi = x;
    }
}
--- hint
`lo` and `hi` need `&`, or the caller never sees them change.
--- hint
Start both at `v[0]`, then visit every element and move `lo` down or `hi` up as needed.
--- check test | {4, -2, 9} gives -2 and 9
[] { int lo = 0, hi = 0; min_max({4, -2, 9}, lo, hi); return lo == -2 && hi == 9; }()
--- check test | One element is both
[] { int lo = 0, hi = 0; min_max({7}, lo, hi); return lo == 7 && hi == 7; }()
--- check test | All negative
[] { int lo = 0, hi = 0; min_max({-8, -3, -5}, lo, hi); return lo == -8 && hi == -3; }()

+++ practice | Sort three in place
--- task
Write `void sort3(int& a, int& b, int& c)` that rearranges the caller's three variables so that afterwards `a` holds the smallest value, `c` the largest, and `b` the one in between.

Write a helper `void swap_ints(int& x, int& y)` first, above `sort3`, and use it. Then sort with three compare-and-swap steps: put the smaller of `a` and `b` into `a`, then the smaller of `a` and `c` into `a`, then the smaller of `b` and `c` into `b`.

Every order must work, including duplicates, negatives, and three that are already sorted. No `main`.
--- starter
void swap_ints(int& x, int& y) {
}

void sort3(int& a, int& b, int& c) {
    if (a > b) swap_ints(a, b);
    if (b > c) swap_ints(b, c);
}
--- solution
void swap_ints(int& x, int& y) {
    int tmp = x;
    x = y;
    y = tmp;
}

void sort3(int& a, int& b, int& c) {
    if (a > b) swap_ints(a, b);
    if (a > c) swap_ints(a, c);
    if (b > c) swap_ints(b, c);
}
--- hint
`swap_ints` needs a temporary, as in the lesson. Without it, both variables end up with the same value.
--- hint
After the first two steps, `a` is the smallest of all three. The last step only has `b` and `c` left to put in order.
--- check test | 3, 1, 2 becomes 1, 2, 3
[] { int a = 3, b = 1, c = 2; sort3(a, b, c); return a == 1 && b == 2 && c == 3; }()
--- check test | Backwards: 9, 5, 1 becomes 1, 5, 9
[] { int a = 9, b = 5, c = 1; sort3(a, b, c); return a == 1 && b == 5 && c == 9; }()
--- check test | Already sorted stays sorted
[] { int a = 1, b = 2, c = 3; sort3(a, b, c); return a == 1 && b == 2 && c == 3; }()
--- check test | Duplicates: 2, 2, 1 becomes 1, 2, 2
[] { int a = 2, b = 2, c = 1; sort3(a, b, c); return a == 1 && b == 2 && c == 2; }()
--- check test | Negatives: -1, -5, -3 becomes -5, -3, -1
[] { int a = -1, b = -5, c = -3; sort3(a, b, c); return a == -5 && b == -3 && c == -1; }()

+++ practice | Fix the in-place changes
--- task
Two functions should change the caller's vector in place:

- `add_offset(readings, offset)` should add `offset` to every reading. Instead, the readings do not change at all.
- `swap_first_last(v)` should swap the first and last elements. `{1, 2, 3}` should become `{3, 2, 1}`, but it becomes `{3, 2, 3}`. A vector with fewer than 2 elements is left alone.

Each function has one wrong line. Fix them. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

void add_offset(std::vector<double>& readings, double offset) {
    for (double r : readings) {
        r += offset;
    }
}

void swap_first_last(std::vector<int>& v) {
    if (v.size() < 2) return;
    int last = v.size() - 1;
    v[0] = v[last];
    v[last] = v[0];
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

void add_offset(std::vector<double>& readings, double offset) {
    for (double& r : readings) {
        r += offset;
    }
}

void swap_first_last(std::vector<int>& v) {
    if (v.size() < 2) return;
    int last = v.size() - 1;
    int tmp = v[0];
    v[0] = v[last];
    v[last] = tmp;
}
--- hint
In `add_offset`, the loop changes copies of the readings. Which `&` is missing?
--- hint
In `swap_first_last`, `v[0] = v[last];` wipes out the old first element before it was saved anywhere. Keep it in a temporary first.
--- check test | add_offset changes every reading
[] { std::vector<double> r{1.5, -2, 0}; add_offset(r, 10); return r == std::vector<double>{11.5, 8, 10}; }()
--- check test | swap_first_last swaps
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 2, 3}; swap_first_last(v); return v == std::vector<int>{3, 2, 1}; }()
--- check test | Two elements swap too
[] { std::vector<int> v{8, 9}; swap_first_last(v); return v == std::vector<int>{9, 8}; }()
--- check test | One element is left alone
[] { std::vector<int> v{5}; swap_first_last(v); return v == std::vector<int>{5}; }()

+++ practice | Smooth the noisy readings
--- task
A sensor's readings are noisy. To smooth them, each reading that has a neighbour on both sides is replaced by the average of three: the reading before it, itself, and the reading after it. The first and last readings stay as they are.

Write `void smooth(std::vector<double>& v)` that does this in place. Every average must use the **original** readings, not ones you have already smoothed. So `{0, 3, 0, 3, 0}` becomes `{0, 1, 2, 1, 0}`: the middle 0 becomes (3 + 0 + 3) / 3 = 2, using the original 3s.

A vector with fewer than 3 readings stays as it is. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

void smooth(std::vector<double>& v) {
    int n = v.size();
    for (int i = 1; i < n - 1; i++) {
        v[i] = (v[i - 1] + v[i] + v[i + 1]) / 3;
    }
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

void smooth(std::vector<double>& v) {
    std::vector<double> original = v;
    int n = v.size();
    for (int i = 1; i < n - 1; i++) {
        v[i] = (original[i - 1] + original[i] + original[i + 1]) / 3;
    }
}
--- hint
The starter changes `v[1]` first, and then uses the **new** `v[1]` when it works out `v[2]`. That is not the original reading.
--- hint
Make a copy before the loop: `std::vector<double> original = v;` copies the whole vector. Read from `original`, write into `v`.
--- check test | {0, 3, 0, 3, 0} becomes {0, 1, 2, 1, 0}
[] { std::vector<double> v{0, 3, 0, 3, 0}; smooth(v); return v == std::vector<double>{0, 1, 2, 1, 0}; }()
--- check test | A straight line stays the same
[] { std::vector<double> v{3, 6, 9, 12}; smooth(v); return v == std::vector<double>{3, 6, 9, 12}; }()
--- check test | Three readings: only the middle changes
[] { std::vector<double> v{1, 4, 1}; smooth(v); return v == std::vector<double>{1, 2, 1}; }()
--- check test | Two readings or none stay as they are
[] { std::vector<double> a{5, 7}; std::vector<double> b; smooth(a); smooth(b); return a == std::vector<double>{5, 7} && b.size() == 0; }()

=== cpp-11 | Structs and classes
--- teach
Last lesson you passed variables by reference, so a function could change the caller's own `int` or vector. So far, every variable you made held one kind of thing: one number, one piece of text, one list. This lesson lets you invent a type of your own that bundles several values together, and then give that type rules it enforces by itself.

### A struct: several values under one name

Think of where a rover is on a flat map. That is not one number but two: how far across (call it x) and how far up (call it y). You could keep two separate `double` variables, but they belong together, and a function that needs "the position" should get both at once.

A **struct** (short for *structure*) is a new type you design that [[groups related values|struct-memory]] under one name:

```cpp
struct Point {
    double x;
    double y;
};
```

Read it as: "a `Point` is made of a `double` called `x` and a `double` called `y`." Each value inside is a **member**: one named part of the struct. This only describes the shape. No point exists yet, the same way a blank form is not a filled-in form.

**Watch out:** a struct ends with `};`, a closing brace *and* a semicolon. The braces you have closed so far, after an `if`, a loop or a function, need no `;`, so this one is easy to forget. (A class, coming up below, ends with `};` too.) Leave it off and the compiler stops with an error like `expected ';' after struct`.

### Making a Point and reading its parts

Now make a real point, the same way you make an `int`:

```cpp
Point p{3.0, 4.0};           // x is 3.0, y is 4.0
std::cout << p.x << "\n";    // prints 3
```

The braces `{ }` fill in the members in the order the struct lists them: first `x`, then `y`. The dot `.` is the **member access** operator: read `p.x` as "p's x". You can change a member the same way, with `p.y = 10.0;`.

A `Point` is a type like any other, so a function can take one as a parameter and read its members:

```cpp
double height_of(Point p) {
    return p.y;
}
```

### The distance between two points

To get the straight-line distance between two points, use [[Pythagoras' rule|pythagoras]]. Find how far apart they are across (`dx`, the difference of the x's) and how far apart they are up (`dy`, the difference of the y's). The distance is the square root of `dx * dx + dy * dy`.

`std::sqrt` gives a square root. It lives in the `<cmath>` header (the maths library), so a file that uses it starts with `#include <cmath>`:

```cpp
std::sqrt(25.0)    // 5
```

### A class: data that keeps its own rules

Picture a bank account. Anyone should be able to put money in and read the balance, but nobody should be able to reach inside and write any number they like into it. A struct's members are open to everyone. A **class** is the same thing as a struct, except its members are **[[private|why-private]]** by default: only the class's own functions can touch them.

```cpp
class Account {
public:
    explicit Account(int start) : balance_(start) {}
    void deposit(int amount) { balance_ += amount; }
    int balance() const { return balance_; }   // const: does not change the object
private:
    int balance_;
};
```

Take it one piece at a time:

- `public:` is a label. Everything below it, until the next label, is **public**: code outside the class may use it.
- `private:` is the other label. Everything below it is private: only the class's own functions may use it. Here that is `int balance_;`, the data. The underscore at the end of the name is only a habit that marks it as private data; the compiler does not care.
- `deposit` is a **member function**: a function that lives inside the class and works on one object's own data. `balance_ += amount` means "add `amount` to `balance_`" (`+=` is read "add to").
- `balance()` hands back the balance. The `const` after its parentheses is a [[promise that it only looks|const-member]] and never changes the object.

### The constructor

The first line inside `public:` is the **constructor**: a special member function that runs once, when an object is created, to set it up. It has the same name as the class and no return type.

```cpp
explicit Account(int start) : balance_(start) {}
```

- `(int start)` is its parameter, like any function's.
- `: balance_(start)` is the **[[member initializer list|init-list]]**. After the colon, each member gets its first value before the body runs. Here `balance_` starts out equal to `start`.
- `{}` is the body. It is empty because there is nothing more to do.
- `explicit` means the constructor runs only when you ask for it by name. It stops C++ from [[quietly turning a plain number into an Account|explicit-keyword]].

Now use it. Put the starting value in parentheses after the object's name:

```cpp
Account savings(100);                     // the constructor runs: balance_ starts at 100
savings.deposit(50);
std::cout << savings.balance() << "\n";   // prints 150
// savings.balance_ = 1000000;            // compile error: balance_ is private
```

You can also make an object with no name and use it at once: `Account(100).balance()` is `100`.

**Watch out:** calling a member function needs its parentheses, even when it takes nothing: `savings.balance()`, not `savings.balance`.

::: context struct-memory How a Point sits in memory
A struct's members are stored one after another in the computer's memory, in the order you listed them. On ordinary computers a `double` takes 8 bytes, so a `Point` is 16 bytes: 8 for `x`, then 8 for `y`. When you pass a `Point` to a function, all 16 bytes are copied, just like copying an `int`. Flight software uses structs constantly for exactly this reason: a telemetry packet, a sensor reading or a position is a fixed bundle of numbers laid out in a known order.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 120" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="180" y="20" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">Point p{3.0, 4.0}: 16 bytes in a row</text>
  <rect x="30" y="35" width="150" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="180" y="35" width="150" height="40" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="105" y="60" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">x = 3.0</text>
  <text x="255" y="60" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">y = 4.0</text>
  <text x="105" y="95" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">8 bytes</text>
  <text x="255" y="95" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">8 bytes</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context pythagoras The rule behind distance
Walk 3 steps across and 4 steps up, and the straight line back to where you started is exactly 5 steps long. That is Pythagoras' rule for a right-angled triangle: the long side squared equals the two short sides squared, added. Here 3 × 3 + 4 × 4 = 9 + 16 = 25, and the square root of 25 is 5. For two points, the short sides are `dx` and `dy`. The sign of `dx` does not matter, because squaring a negative number gives a positive one.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 200" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="60" y1="170" x2="150" y2="170" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="150" y1="170" x2="150" y2="50" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="60" y1="170" x2="150" y2="50" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="3"/>
  <rect x="140" y="160" width="10" height="10" fill="none" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <circle cx="60" cy="170" r="4" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <circle cx="150" cy="50" r="4" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="40" y="190" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">a (0, 0)</text>
  <text x="160" y="48" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">b (3, 4)</text>
  <text x="105" y="188" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">dx = 3</text>
  <text x="158" y="115" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">dy = 4</text>
  <text x="80" y="105" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="end">5</text>
  <text x="215" y="130" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">√(3×3 + 4×4)</text>
  <text x="215" y="148" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">= √25 = 5</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context why-private Why hide the data at all
A rule a type must always keep is called an **invariant**. "A counter is never negative" or "an account's balance only changes through deposits" are invariants. If the data is public, any line anywhere in a program of a million lines could break the rule, and you would have to search all of them to find which one did. If the data is private, only the class's own few functions can change it, so there are only a few places to check. That is why big, careful programs, flight software included, keep data private. You will build classes that guard their own rules in the next course's "Classes that keep their own rules".
:::

::: context const-member A promise the compiler checks
The `const` after `balance()` is not a comment; the compiler holds you to it. If the body tried to change `balance_`, the program would not compile. It also lets the function be called on an object you are only allowed to look at, such as one passed in as a `const Account&`. Without that `const`, even reading the balance of such an object would be a compile error. The next course opens with a whole lesson on this, "const correctness".
:::

::: context init-list Why members get their value before the body
You could write `Account(int start) { balance_ = start; }` and for an `int` it works the same. But the list after the colon does something the body cannot: it gives a member its very first value, instead of changing it afterwards. Some members must be set at birth and can never be assigned later, such as a `const` member or a reference member, so they can only be set in this list. One more detail: members are always set up in the order they are declared in the class, whatever order you write them in the list.
:::

::: context explicit-keyword What explicit prevents
Without `explicit`, a constructor that takes one value also works as an automatic conversion. Then `Account a = 100;` would compile, and so would passing the number `100` to a function that expects an `Account`: C++ would quietly build an account from it. That is almost never what you meant, and it hides mistakes. With `explicit`, both are compile errors, and you have to write `Account a(100);` on purpose. Many style guides ask for `explicit` on every one-value constructor.
:::
--- task
Write `struct Point { double x; double y; };` and a function `double distance(Point a, Point b)` that returns the straight-line distance between the two points (use `std::sqrt`, from `<cmath>`, which the starter already includes).

Then write a `class Counter` with:

- a constructor that takes the starting value as an `int`,
- `void increment()`, which adds one,
- `void reset()`, which sets the count back to `0`,
- `int value() const`, which returns the current count.

No `main`: the checker supplies it.
--- starter
#include <cmath>

--- solution
#include <cmath>

struct Point {
    double x;
    double y;
};

double distance(Point a, Point b) {
    double dx = a.x - b.x;
    double dy = a.y - b.y;
    return std::sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy);
}

class Counter {
public:
    explicit Counter(int start) : value_(start) {}
    void increment() { value_++; }
    void reset() { value_ = 0; }
    int value() const { return value_; }
private:
    int value_;
};
--- hint
For the distance, make two `double`s: `dx = a.x - b.x` and `dy = a.y - b.y`. Then return `std::sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy)`. Remember the `;` after the struct's closing brace.
--- hint
Model `Counter` on `Account`: a `public:` part with the constructor and the three functions, and a `private:` part holding the count, for example `int value_;`.
--- hint
The constructor is `explicit Counter(int start) : value_(start) {}`. Then `increment` does `value_++;`, `reset` does `value_ = 0;`, and `value() const` returns `value_`.
--- check test | distance of a 3-4-5 triangle is 5
std::abs(distance(Point{0, 0}, Point{3, 4}) - 5.0) < 1e-9
--- check case | Counter starts where it is told
Counter(5).value()
=> 5
--- check test | increment and reset
[] { Counter c(1); c.increment(); c.increment(); bool three = c.value() == 3; c.reset(); return three && c.value() == 0; }()

+++ practice | A rectangle type
--- task
Write `struct Rectangle { double width; double height; };` and two functions that take a `Rectangle`:

- `double area(Rectangle r)` returns the width times the height,
- `double perimeter(Rectangle r)` returns the distance all the way round: two widths plus two heights.

So for `Rectangle{3, 4}`, the area is 12 and the perimeter is 14. No `main`.
--- starter
struct Rectangle {
    double width;
    double height;
};
--- solution
struct Rectangle {
    double width;
    double height;
};

double area(Rectangle r) {
    return r.width * r.height;
}

double perimeter(Rectangle r) {
    return 2 * (r.width + r.height);
}
--- hint
Inside each function, reach the members with a dot: `r.width` and `r.height`.
--- hint
The perimeter is `2 * (r.width + r.height)`.
--- check case | area of 3 by 4 is 12
area(Rectangle{3, 4})
=> 12.0
--- check case | perimeter of 3 by 4 is 14
perimeter(Rectangle{3, 4})
=> 14.0
--- check case | A flat rectangle has no area
area(Rectangle{0, 5})
=> 0.0
--- check case | perimeter of 2.5 by 1 is 7
perimeter(Rectangle{2.5, 1})
=> 7.0

+++ practice | Halfway between two points
--- task
Write `struct Point { double x; double y; };` and a function `Point midpoint(Point a, Point b)` that returns a **new** `Point` exactly halfway between `a` and `b`. Its `x` is the average of the two x's, and its `y` is the average of the two y's.

So `midpoint(Point{0, 0}, Point{4, 2})` is the point with x 2 and y 1. You can make the answer with braces, as in `Point{mx, my}`. No `main`.
--- starter
struct Point {
    double x;
    double y;
};

Point midpoint(Point a, Point b) {
    return a;
}
--- solution
struct Point {
    double x;
    double y;
};

Point midpoint(Point a, Point b) {
    double mx = (a.x + b.x) / 2;
    double my = (a.y + b.y) / 2;
    return Point{mx, my};
}
--- hint
Work out the new x and the new y as two `double`s: add the two values and divide by 2.
--- hint
Hand back a new point built from them: `return Point{mx, my};`.
--- check test | Between (0, 0) and (4, 2) is (2, 1)
[] { Point m = midpoint(Point{0, 0}, Point{4, 2}); return m.x == 2 && m.y == 1; }()
--- check test | Negative coordinates
[] { Point m = midpoint(Point{-3, 5}, Point{1, -1}); return m.x == -1 && m.y == 2; }()
--- check test | Halfway between a point and itself is the point
[] { Point m = midpoint(Point{7.5, 2}, Point{7.5, 2}); return m.x == 7.5 && m.y == 2; }()

+++ practice | The hottest sensor
--- task
Write `struct Reading { std::string sensor; double value; };` for one sensor reading. Then write `std::string hottest(const std::vector<Reading>& readings)` that returns the name of the sensor with the highest value. If two readings tie for the highest, the first one wins. If the vector is empty, return `""`.

So for `{{"cabin", 21.5}, {"engine", 88}, {"hull", -40}}` the answer is `"engine"`. The starter includes `<string>` and `<vector>`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Reading {
    std::string sensor;
    double value;
};

std::string hottest(const std::vector<Reading>& readings) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <string>
#include <vector>

struct Reading {
    std::string sensor;
    double value;
};

std::string hottest(const std::vector<Reading>& readings) {
    if (readings.size() == 0) {
        return "";
    }
    Reading best = readings[0];
    for (Reading r : readings) {
        if (r.value > best.value) {
            best = r;
        }
    }
    return best.sensor;
}
--- hint
It is the "largest so far" pattern, on the `value` member. Keep the whole best `Reading`, not just its value, so you still know its name at the end.
--- hint
Start `best` at `readings[0]`, after checking the vector is not empty. Use `>`, not `>=`, so a later tie does not replace the first one.
--- check case | The engine is hottest
hottest({{"cabin", 21.5}, {"engine", 88}, {"hull", -40}})
=> "engine"
--- check case | All below zero
hottest({{"a", -10}, {"b", -2}, {"c", -7}})
=> "b"
--- check case | A tie: the first one wins
hottest({{"left", 50}, {"right", 50}})
=> "left"
--- check case | No readings gives ""
hottest({})
=> ""

+++ practice | A tank that cannot overflow
--- task
Write a `class Tank` that keeps its level between 0 and its capacity, whatever the caller asks for:

- a constructor that takes the capacity as an `int`. A new tank starts **empty**.
- `void fill(int amount)` adds `amount`, but never beyond the capacity: any extra spills away.
- `void drain(int amount)` takes `amount` away, but never below 0.
- `int level() const` returns the current level.
- `bool is_full() const` returns `true` when the level equals the capacity.

Keep the capacity and the level private. No `main`.
--- starter
class Tank {
public:
    explicit Tank(int capacity) : capacity_(capacity), level_(0) {}
    void fill(int amount) { level_ += amount; }
    void drain(int amount) { level_ -= amount; }
    int level() const { return level_; }
    bool is_full() const { return false; }
private:
    int capacity_;
    int level_;
};
--- solution
class Tank {
public:
    explicit Tank(int capacity) : capacity_(capacity), level_(0) {}

    void fill(int amount) {
        level_ += amount;
        if (level_ > capacity_) {
            level_ = capacity_;
        }
    }

    void drain(int amount) {
        level_ -= amount;
        if (level_ < 0) {
            level_ = 0;
        }
    }

    int level() const { return level_; }
    bool is_full() const { return level_ == capacity_; }

private:
    int capacity_;
    int level_;
};
--- hint
A member initializer list can set more than one member, separated by commas: `: capacity_(capacity), level_(0)`.
--- hint
After adding in `fill`, if the level went over the capacity, set it back to the capacity. After taking away in `drain`, if it went below 0, set it to 0.
--- check case | A new tank is empty
Tank(100).level()
=> 0
--- check test | Filling past the top stops at the capacity
[] { Tank t(100); t.fill(70); t.fill(50); return t.level() == 100 && t.is_full(); }()
--- check test | Draining too much stops at 0
[] { Tank t(100); t.fill(30); t.drain(45); return t.level() == 0 && !t.is_full(); }()
--- check test | Fill and drain in between
[] { Tank t(50); t.fill(20); t.drain(5); t.fill(10); return t.level() == 25 && !t.is_full(); }()
--- check test | Exactly full is full
[] { Tank t(40); t.fill(40); return t.is_full(); }()

+++ practice | Fix the odometer
--- task
An `Odometer` counts the kilometres a rover has driven. `Odometer(100)` starts at 100 km, and each `drive(km)` adds to the total. So after `Odometer o(100); o.drive(20); o.drive(5);`, `o.km()` should be `125`.

It has two bugs. `Odometer(100).km()` returns `0`, and after two drives only the last one counts. Each bug is on one line. Fix both. No `main`.
--- starter
class Odometer {
public:
    explicit Odometer(int start) : km_(0) {}
    void drive(int km) { km_ = km; }
    int km() const { return km_; }
private:
    int km_;
};
--- solution
class Odometer {
public:
    explicit Odometer(int start) : km_(start) {}
    void drive(int km) { km_ += km; }
    int km() const { return km_; }
private:
    int km_;
};
--- hint
Look at the member initializer list. What does `km_` start as, and what should it start as?
--- hint
In `drive`, `=` replaces the total. You want to add to it.
--- check case | Odometer(100) starts at 100
Odometer(100).km()
=> 100
--- check test | Two drives add up
[] { Odometer o(100); o.drive(20); o.drive(5); return o.km() == 125; }()
--- check test | Starting from 0
[] { Odometer o(0); o.drive(7); return o.km() == 7; }()

+++ practice | Drive the rover
--- task
A rover sits on a grid. It knows its position, `x` (east is bigger) and `y` (north is bigger), and which way it faces: `'N'`, `'E'`, `'S'` or `'W'`.

Write a `class Rover` with:

- a constructor that takes the starting `x` and `y` as two `int`s. A new rover faces `'N'`.
- `void turn_left()` and `void turn_right()`, which turn it a quarter turn. Turning right from `'N'` gives `'E'`, then `'S'`, then `'W'`, then `'N'` again. Turning left goes the other way.
- `void forward(int steps)`, which moves it `steps` squares the way it faces: north adds to `y`, south takes from `y`, east adds to `x`, west takes from `x`.
- `int x() const`, `int y() const` and `char heading() const`, which report where it is and which way it faces.

No `main`.
--- starter
class Rover {
public:
    explicit Rover(int x, int y) : x_(x), y_(y), heading_('N') {}
    void turn_left() {}
    void turn_right() {}
    void forward(int steps) {}
    int x() const { return x_; }
    int y() const { return y_; }
    char heading() const { return heading_; }
private:
    int x_;
    int y_;
    char heading_;
};
--- solution
class Rover {
public:
    explicit Rover(int x, int y) : x_(x), y_(y), heading_('N') {}

    void turn_right() {
        if (heading_ == 'N') {
            heading_ = 'E';
        } else if (heading_ == 'E') {
            heading_ = 'S';
        } else if (heading_ == 'S') {
            heading_ = 'W';
        } else {
            heading_ = 'N';
        }
    }

    void turn_left() {
        turn_right();
        turn_right();
        turn_right();
    }

    void forward(int steps) {
        if (heading_ == 'N') {
            y_ += steps;
        } else if (heading_ == 'S') {
            y_ -= steps;
        } else if (heading_ == 'E') {
            x_ += steps;
        } else {
            x_ -= steps;
        }
    }

    int x() const { return x_; }
    int y() const { return y_; }
    char heading() const { return heading_; }

private:
    int x_;
    int y_;
    char heading_;
};
--- hint
`turn_right` is an `if` / `else if` chain on `heading_`: each heading gets the next one round the circle.
--- hint
Three right turns face the same way as one left turn. A member function can call another member function of the same object.
--- hint
`forward` is another chain on `heading_`: `y_ += steps;` for north, `y_ -= steps;` for south, and the same with `x_` for east and west.
--- check test | A new rover faces north where it was put
[] { Rover r(2, 3); return r.x() == 2 && r.y() == 3 && r.heading() == 'N'; }()
--- check test | Four right turns come back to north
[] { Rover r(0, 0); r.turn_right(); bool e = r.heading() == 'E'; r.turn_right(); r.turn_right(); bool w = r.heading() == 'W'; r.turn_right(); return e && w && r.heading() == 'N'; }()
--- check test | Turning left from north faces west
[] { Rover r(0, 0); r.turn_left(); return r.heading() == 'W'; }()
--- check test | Forward, right, forward
[] { Rover r(0, 0); r.forward(3); r.turn_right(); r.forward(2); return r.x() == 2 && r.y() == 3 && r.heading() == 'E'; }()
--- check test | Driving south and west goes negative
[] { Rover r(1, 1); r.turn_left(); r.forward(4); r.turn_left(); r.forward(6); return r.x() == -3 && r.y() == -5 && r.heading() == 'S'; }()

=== cpp-12 | std::map
--- teach
Last lesson you built types of your own with `struct` and `class`. This lesson uses a ready-made type from the standard library that you will reach for again and again: the map.

### Looking things up by name

A `std::vector` finds things by position: `v[0]`, `v[1]`, `v[2]`. Often you want to find things by name instead. Think of a space station's supply list: you do not ask "what is item number 2?", you ask "how much water is left?"

A **std::map** is a container of **key → value** pairs, where the arrow is read "maps to":

- the **key** is what you look things up by (here, the name of a supply);
- the **value** is what is stored under that key (here, how many there are).

Each key appears at most once, and the map keeps its pairs [[sorted by key|map-tree]].

### Making a map

```cpp
#include <map>
#include <string>

std::map<std::string, int> supplies;
```

Inside the [[angle brackets|template-args]] `< >` go two types: first the key's type, then the value's type. So this map goes from `std::string` names to `int` amounts. It starts empty.

### Storing and reading with [ ]

Square brackets with a key work like a labeled box:

```cpp
supplies["water"] = 40;                  // store 40 under "water"
supplies["oxygen"] = 12;
std::cout << supplies["water"] << "\n";  // prints 40
```

Storing under a key that is already there replaces the old value.

### A missing key starts at 0

If you use `[ ]` with a key the map does not have yet, the map adds that key with a value that [[starts at 0|starts-at-zero]], and then does what you asked:

```cpp
supplies["food"] += 3;    // "food" was missing: it starts at 0, then += 3 makes it 3
```

That makes counting easy. `++` adds one (you met it in `i++` in the loops lesson), so `counts[word]++` means "add one to this word's count", and it works even the very first time a word shows up.

### Looking without touching

`.count(key)` tells you whether a key is there: `1` if it is, `0` if not. It only looks; it never adds anything.

```cpp
supplies.count("fuel");   // 0: there is no "fuel" key
```

`.find(key)` also looks without adding. You will use it properly in the next course.

**Watch out:** *reading* `m[key]` also inserts the key if it is missing. `std::cout << supplies["fuel"];` prints `0`, and now the map has a `"fuel"` entry it did not have before. When you only want to check whether a key is there, use `m.count(key)` or `m.find(key)`, never `m[key]`.

### Going through every pair

A range-for loop (from the `std::vector` lesson) visits every pair, in key order:

```cpp
for (const auto& [name, qty] : supplies) {
    std::cout << name << " " << qty << "\n";
}
```

- `const auto&` means "each pair, by read-only reference": no copies, and no changes (like `const std::vector<int>&` in the references lesson).
- `[name, qty]` is a **[[structured binding|structured-bindings]]**: it unpacks each pair into two names, the key and the value.

For string keys, key order is [[alphabetical order|string-order]]. With `food` 3, `oxygen` 12 and `water` 40 stored, this prints:

```
food 3
oxygen 12
water 40
```

### Reading words out of a string

To count words you first need to pull the words out of one long string. In the reading-input lesson, `std::cin >> w` read the next value from the Input box, skipping spaces. A **std::istringstream** (input string stream) does the same thing, but reads from a string you give it. It needs `#include <sstream>`.

```cpp
std::istringstream in("go no go");
std::string w;
while (in >> w) {
    std::cout << w << "\n";
}
```

This prints `go`, `no` and `go`, one per line. Like `std::cin >> w`, `in >> w` is true while it managed to read a word, so the loop stops when the words run out. This works because `std::cin` and `std::istringstream` are both [[streams|streams]].

::: context map-tree A tree behind the scenes
A `std::map` does not keep its pairs in one long row. It keeps them in a *tree*: each key has smaller keys down its left branch and bigger keys down its right. To find a key, it starts at the top and goes left or right at each step. The map keeps the tree balanced, so a million keys need only about 20 steps (2 multiplied by itself 20 times is just over a million). This picture shows one shape the map can build for the words of "to be or not to be":

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 190" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="180" y1="45" x2="100" y2="95" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="180" y1="45" x2="260" y2="95" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <line x1="100" y1="115" x2="160" y2="150" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="140" y="20" width="80" height="30" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="40" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">or: 1</text>
  <rect x="60" y="90" width="80" height="30" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="100" y="110" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">be: 2</text>
  <rect x="220" y="90" width="80" height="30" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="260" y="110" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">to: 2</text>
  <rect x="120" y="145" width="80" height="30" rx="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="160" y="165" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">not: 1</text>
  <text x="20" y="75" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">smaller: left</text>
  <text x="270" y="75" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93">bigger: right</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context template-args Types that take types
`std::vector<int>` and `std::map<std::string, int>` are both *templates*: general-purpose recipes that become a real type once you fill in the angle brackets. The same `std::map` code works for a map from names to numbers, from numbers to names, or from part numbers to whole structs. You fill in the types; the compiler writes the specific version for you. Later, in the advanced course, you will write templates of your own.
:::

::: context starts-at-zero Where the zero comes from
When `[ ]` meets a missing key, the map creates a brand-new value for it the plain, empty way: `0` for an `int`, `0.0` for a `double`, and an empty string `""` for a `std::string`. Because `[ ]` may add a key, it cannot be used on a map you are only allowed to read, such as one passed in as `const std::map<std::string, int>&`: that is a compile error. For those, use `.count` or `.find`, or `.at(key)`, which reads an existing key and stops the program with an error if the key is missing.
:::

::: context structured-bindings Pairs, and unpacking them
Each element of a map is a `std::pair`: a tiny struct with two members, `.first` (the key) and `.second` (the value). Before C++17, the 2017 version of the language, you had to write `p.first` and `p.second`. Structured bindings, added in C++17, let you name both parts at once. The key part is always `const`: if you could change a key in place, it would end up in the wrong spot in the sorted order.
:::

::: context string-order Why "Zebra" comes before "apple"
Strings are compared one character at a time, from the left, using each character's number code. Every capital letter has a smaller code than every small letter: `'A'` is 65, `'Z'` is 90 and `'a'` is 97. So a map puts `"Zebra"` before `"apple"`. Digits compare the same way, which is why `"10"` comes before `"9"`: the first characters are `'1'` and `'9'`, and `'1'` is smaller. When you want numbers in number order, use `int` keys.
:::

::: context streams One way of reading, many sources
A *stream* is a flow of characters you read from (or write to) in order. `std::cin` reads from the keyboard or the Input box, `std::istringstream` reads from a string, and `std::ifstream` reads from a file, but all of them use the same `>>`. Learn it once and it works everywhere, which is why flight software can test its telemetry parser on a string before it ever touches a real radio link. The next course has a whole lesson on processing text with string streams.
:::
--- task
Write `std::map<std::string, int> count_words(const std::string& text)` that counts how many times each space-separated word appears in `text`. For example, for `"to be or not to be"` the map holds `be` → 2, `not` → 1, `or` → 1, `to` → 2.

Read the words out of the string with a `std::istringstream in(text);` and a `while (in >> w) { … }` loop, as in the explanation; it needs `<sstream>`, which the starter already includes. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <sstream>

std::map<std::string, int> count_words(const std::string& text) {
    std::map<std::string, int> counts;
    return counts;
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <sstream>

std::map<std::string, int> count_words(const std::string& text) {
    std::map<std::string, int> counts;
    std::istringstream in(text);
    std::string word;
    while (in >> word) {
        counts[word]++;
    }
    return counts;
}
--- hint
Two steps: pull the words out one at a time with a string stream, and add one to each word's count in the map.
--- hint
Before the `return`, make `std::istringstream in(text);` and `std::string word;`, then loop with `while (in >> word) { … }`.
--- hint
Inside the loop, `counts[word]++;` is all you need: a word seen for the first time starts at 0 and becomes 1.
--- check case | Counts repeated words
count_words("to be or not to be")
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"be", 2}, {"not", 1}, {"or", 1}, {"to", 2}}
--- check test | An empty string gives an empty map
count_words("").empty()

+++ practice | Counting letters
--- task
Write `std::map<char, int> letter_counts(const std::string& s)` that counts how many times each character appears in `s`, leaving out spaces. The keys are `char`s, and the values are the counts.

So `letter_counts("abca")` holds `'a'` → 2, `'b'` → 1, `'c'` → 1, and `letter_counts("a a")` holds just `'a'` → 2. An empty string gives an empty map. The starter includes `<map>` and `<string>`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>

std::map<char, int> letter_counts(const std::string& s) {
    std::map<char, int> counts;
    return counts;
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>

std::map<char, int> letter_counts(const std::string& s) {
    std::map<char, int> counts;
    for (char c : s) {
        if (c != ' ') {
            counts[c]++;
        }
    }
    return counts;
}
--- hint
Go through the string one `char` at a time with a range-for loop.
--- hint
For every character that is not a space, `counts[c]++`. A character seen for the first time starts at 0.
--- check case | letter_counts("abca")
letter_counts("abca")
=> std::map<char, int>{{'a', 2}, {'b', 1}, {'c', 1}}
--- check case | Spaces are left out
letter_counts("a a")
=> std::map<char, int>{{'a', 2}}
--- check test | An empty string gives an empty map
letter_counts("").empty()

+++ practice | Totals from a delivery list
--- task
A delivery arrives as two vectors of the same length: the item names, and how many of each came in. The same item can appear more than once.

Write `std::map<std::string, int> totals(const std::vector<std::string>& items, const std::vector<int>& amounts)` that returns the total amount for each item. Element `i` of `amounts` belongs to element `i` of `items`.

So items `{"water", "food", "water"}` with amounts `{10, 4, 5}` give `food` → 4 and `water` → 15. Two empty vectors give an empty map. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::map<std::string, int> totals(const std::vector<std::string>& items, const std::vector<int>& amounts) {
    std::map<std::string, int> sum;
    int n = items.size();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        sum[items[i]] = amounts[i];
    }
    return sum;
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

std::map<std::string, int> totals(const std::vector<std::string>& items, const std::vector<int>& amounts) {
    std::map<std::string, int> sum;
    int n = items.size();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        sum[items[i]] += amounts[i];
    }
    return sum;
}
--- hint
The starter keeps only the last amount for each item: `=` replaces what was there.
--- hint
Add each amount on with `+=`. A missing key starts at 0, so the first amount lands correctly too.
--- check case | Two lots of water add up
totals({"water", "food", "water"}, {10, 4, 5})
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"food", 4}, {"water", 15}}
--- check case | Each item once
totals({"o2", "n2"}, {3, 8})
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"n2", 8}, {"o2", 3}}
--- check test | Empty vectors give an empty map
totals({}, {}).empty()

+++ practice | The most common word
--- task
Write `std::string most_common(const std::string& text)` that returns the word that appears most often in `text`. Words are separated by spaces. If several words tie, return the one that comes first in alphabetical order. An empty text gives `""`.

So `most_common("go no go")` is `"go"`, `most_common("b a b a")` is `"a"` (a tie, and `a` comes first), and `most_common("solo")` is `"solo"`.

Count the words into a map first, as in the lesson. Then go through the map, which is already in alphabetical order, and keep the word with the highest count. The starter includes `<map>`, `<string>` and `<sstream>`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <sstream>

std::string most_common(const std::string& text) {
    std::istringstream in(text);
    std::string first;
    in >> first;
    return first;
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <sstream>

std::string most_common(const std::string& text) {
    std::map<std::string, int> counts;
    std::istringstream in(text);
    std::string w;
    while (in >> w) {
        counts[w]++;
    }
    std::string best;
    int best_count = 0;
    for (const auto& [word, n] : counts) {
        if (n > best_count) {
            best = word;
            best_count = n;
        }
    }
    return best;
}
--- hint
Two steps: count every word into a `std::map<std::string, int>`, then look through the map for the biggest count.
--- hint
Keep `best` and `best_count`, starting at `""` and 0. Replace them only when a count is strictly bigger, with `>`. Because the map visits words alphabetically, a later word with an equal count never replaces an earlier one.
--- check case | most_common("go no go") is "go"
most_common("go no go")
=> "go"
--- check case | A tie goes to the alphabetically first word
most_common("b a b a")
=> "a"
--- check case | One word
most_common("solo")
=> "solo"
--- check case | Empty text gives ""
most_common("")
=> ""
--- check case | The winner can come last in the text
most_common("x y z z")
=> "z"

+++ practice | Add up without adding keys
--- task
Write `int total_for(std::map<std::string, int>& stock, const std::vector<std::string>& names)` that returns the sum of the stock for every name in `names`. A name that is not in the map counts as 0.

The catch: the map must not change. Looking up a missing name must **not** add it to the map. So if `stock` holds `water` → 40 and `food` → 12, `total_for(stock, {"water", "fuel", "food"})` returns 52, and afterwards `stock` still has exactly 2 keys. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int total_for(std::map<std::string, int>& stock, const std::vector<std::string>& names) {
    int total = 0;
    for (std::string name : names) {
        total += stock[name];
    }
    return total;
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <vector>

int total_for(std::map<std::string, int>& stock, const std::vector<std::string>& names) {
    int total = 0;
    for (std::string name : names) {
        if (stock.count(name) == 1) {
            total += stock[name];
        }
    }
    return total;
}
--- hint
The starter gets the right total, but `stock[name]` quietly adds every missing name with a value of 0.
--- hint
Check with `stock.count(name)` first. It only looks. Use `stock[name]` only when the name is already there, and then it adds nothing.
--- check test | The total counts a missing name as 0
[] { std::map<std::string, int> s{{"water", 40}, {"food", 12}}; return total_for(s, {"water", "fuel", "food"}) == 52; }()
--- check test | The map does not gain any keys
[] { std::map<std::string, int> s{{"water", 40}, {"food", 12}}; total_for(s, {"water", "fuel", "air"}); return s.size() == 2; }()
--- check test | A name listed twice counts twice
[] { std::map<std::string, int> s{{"water", 40}}; return total_for(s, {"water", "water"}) == 80; }()
--- check test | No names gives 0
[] { std::map<std::string, int> s{{"water", 40}}; return total_for(s, {}) == 0; }()

+++ practice | Fix the restock
--- task
`restock(stock, amount)` should add `amount` to every item in the caller's map. So a map holding `food` → 3 and `water` → 10, restocked by 5, should hold `food` → 8 and `water` → 15.

Instead, nothing changes. The bug is on one line. Fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>

void restock(std::map<std::string, int>& stock, int amount) {
    for (auto [name, qty] : stock) {
        qty += amount;
    }
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>

void restock(std::map<std::string, int>& stock, int amount) {
    for (auto& [name, qty] : stock) {
        qty += amount;
    }
}
--- hint
The map itself is passed by reference. What about each pair the loop visits?
--- hint
Without `&`, each pair is copied, and the loop changes the copy. Make the loop's pair a reference, as in `for (int& x : v)`.
--- check test | Every item goes up by 5
[] { std::map<std::string, int> s{{"food", 3}, {"water", 10}}; restock(s, 5); return s == std::map<std::string, int>{{"food", 8}, {"water", 15}}; }()
--- check test | Restocking by 0 changes nothing
[] { std::map<std::string, int> s{{"air", 7}}; restock(s, 0); return s == std::map<std::string, int>{{"air", 7}}; }()
--- check test | An empty map stays empty
[] { std::map<std::string, int> s; restock(s, 5); return s.empty(); }()

+++ practice | Replay the supply log
--- task
The station's supply log is one long string of entries. Each entry is three words: `add` or `use`, an item name, and a whole number:

```
add water 40 add food 12 use water 15 use food 20 add air 3
```

Write `std::map<std::string, int> replay(const std::string& log)` that starts with nothing and applies each entry in order. `add` puts that many in; `use` takes that many out, but an item can never go below 0. So the log above gives `air` → 3, `food` → 0 and `water` → 25.

A `std::istringstream` reads numbers as well as words, just as `std::cin` does: `in >> action >> item >> amount` reads one whole entry, and is true while there is an entry to read. The starter includes `<map>`, `<string>` and `<sstream>`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <sstream>

std::map<std::string, int> replay(const std::string& log) {
    std::map<std::string, int> stock;
    return stock;
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <sstream>

std::map<std::string, int> replay(const std::string& log) {
    std::map<std::string, int> stock;
    std::istringstream in(log);
    std::string action;
    std::string item;
    int amount;
    while (in >> action >> item >> amount) {
        if (action == "add") {
            stock[item] += amount;
        } else {
            stock[item] -= amount;
            if (stock[item] < 0) {
                stock[item] = 0;
            }
        }
    }
    return stock;
}
--- hint
Declare `std::string action;`, `std::string item;` and `int amount;`, then loop `while (in >> action >> item >> amount)`.
--- hint
For `add`, `stock[item] += amount;`. For `use`, take it away, then set it back to 0 if it went below.
--- check case | The example log
replay("add water 40 add food 12 use water 15 use food 20 add air 3")
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"air", 3}, {"food", 0}, {"water", 25}}
--- check case | Using something never added leaves it at 0
replay("use fuel 5")
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"fuel", 0}}
--- check case | Adds pile up
replay("add o2 1 add o2 2 add o2 3")
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"o2", 6}}
--- check test | An empty log gives an empty map
replay("").empty()

=== cpp-13 | Pointers and ownership
--- teach
In the references lesson you gave an existing variable a second name, and last lesson a map let you find a value by its key. This lesson shows the tool that sits underneath both: the pointer, which finds a value by *where it lives* in memory.

### Every value lives at an address

Picture the computer's memory as one very long street of numbered mailboxes. Each variable lives in a box (or a few boxes next to each other), and every box has a number. That number is the variable's **[[address|addresses]]**: where it lives in memory.

`&x` is the **address-of** operator. Read `&x` as "the address of x".

```cpp
int x = 10;
std::cout << &x << "\n";   // prints an address, something like 0x7ffd5c3a1e4c
```

The address is printed in hexadecimal (base 16) and changes from run to run, so you will see a different number each time.

The `&` symbol now has two jobs. Written after a *type*, as in `int& n`, it makes a reference (the references lesson). Written in front of a *variable*, as in `&x`, it means "the address of".

### A pointer holds an address

A **pointer** is a variable whose value is an address. It is a slip of paper with a mailbox number written on it.

```cpp
int* p = &x;    // p points at x
```

`int*` is read "[[pointer to int|star-placement]]". So this line says: make a pointer to an `int` called `p`, and store in it the address of `x`. We say "p points at x".

### Following a pointer

Put `*` in front of a pointer to follow it to the value it points at. This is called **dereferencing**; read `*p` as "the thing p points at".

```cpp
*p = 20;                  // x is now 20
std::cout << x << "\n";   // prints 20
```

Nothing was written to `x` by name, yet `x` changed, because `p` held its address.

### Pointing at nothing

Sometimes a pointer has nothing to point at yet. For that there is **nullptr**, the null pointer: a pointer value that means "points at nothing".

```cpp
int* none = nullptr;   // points at nothing: never follow it
```

Following a null pointer (`*none`) is **[[undefined behavior|undefined-behaviour]]**: C++ makes no promise at all about what happens. Usually the program crashes.

**Watch out:** when a function is handed a pointer, it may be handed `nullptr`. Check before you follow it:

```cpp
if (p != nullptr) {
    *p = 0;
}
```

### Memory that outlives a function

A function's ordinary variables disappear when the function returns. Pointers are how C++ reaches [[memory that outlives a function|stack-and-heap]]. You can ask for a piece of memory with `new` (for example `new int(5)`), and it stays until you give it back with `delete`.

Memory you ask for must be given back **[[exactly once|new-delete-bugs]]**: forget, and it is never freed; do it twice, and the program is broken. So modern C++ does not use raw `new` and `delete` for this. Instead, a **smart pointer** owns the object: it is the one thing responsible for the object, and it frees it automatically.

### std::unique_ptr: one owner

**std::unique_ptr** is the smart pointer for "exactly one owner". `std::make_unique` makes a new object and hands back a `unique_ptr` that owns it. Both need `#include <memory>`.

```cpp
#include <memory>

auto p = std::make_unique<int>(42);   // p owns a new int holding 42
*p += 1;                              // follow it like a pointer: now 43
// the int is freed automatically when p goes out of scope
```

"Goes out of scope" means the program reaches the closing `}` of the block where `p` was made. Here `auto` lets the compiler work out that `p` is a `std::unique_ptr<int>`: a unique pointer that owns an `int`.

A function can make an object and hand ownership to its caller by returning the `unique_ptr`:

```cpp
std::unique_ptr<double> make_fuel_gauge(double litres) {
    return std::make_unique<double>(litres);
}
```

The rule of thumb: use raw pointers like `int*` to *look at* things, and [[std::unique_ptr to own them|owning-later]].

::: context addresses Memory is numbered boxes
Memory is made of bytes, and every byte has a number, its address. An `int` usually takes 4 bytes; its address is the number of its first byte. On a 64-bit computer a pointer takes 8 bytes, whatever it points at, because an address is always the same size. In the picture, the addresses are made small to fit; real ones are long hexadecimal numbers.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 170" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="head" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto">
      <path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#b4232c"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <text x="80" y="30" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">address 1000</text>
  <text x="265" y="30" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">address 1008</text>
  <rect x="40" y="40" width="80" height="40" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <rect x="210" y="40" width="110" height="40" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="80" y="65" font-size="14" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">10</text>
  <text x="265" y="65" font-size="14" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">1000</text>
  <text x="80" y="100" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">int x</text>
  <text x="265" y="100" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">int* p = &amp;x</text>
  <path d="M265,110 C265,160 80,160 80,112" fill="none" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#head)"/>
  <text x="172" y="160" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="middle">*p follows the address</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context star-placement Where the star belongs
`int* p` and `int *p` mean exactly the same thing; the spaces do not matter. But the star belongs to each name separately, which leads to a classic trap: `int* a, b;` makes `a` a pointer and `b` a plain `int`. Declaring one pointer per line avoids the trap completely.
:::

::: context undefined-behaviour When C++ makes no promise
The C++ rules list some mistakes, like following a null pointer or reading past the end of an array, as *undefined behavior*. The compiler is allowed to assume they never happen, so the result can be anything: a crash, a wrong answer, or a program that seems fine today and fails later. On most computers, following a null pointer crashes the program at once. Tony Hoare, who invented the null reference in 1965, later called it his "billion-dollar mistake" because of how many bugs it has caused.
:::

::: context stack-and-heap Two kinds of memory
A function's ordinary variables live on the *stack*: a pile that grows when a function starts and shrinks when it returns, so those variables vanish automatically. Memory from `new` comes from the *heap* (also called the free store), and it stays until someone gives it back. That is what lets an object outlive the function that made it. It is also why someone has to be clearly in charge of giving it back.
:::

::: context new-delete-bugs Leaks, double frees and flight rules
Forgetting `delete` is a *memory leak*: the memory stays taken, and a program that runs for days slowly runs out. Calling `delete` twice, or using the memory after it was deleted, is undefined behavior and can corrupt anything. Spacecraft software is so careful about this that NASA's Jet Propulsion Laboratory coding rules, the "Power of Ten", forbid asking for new memory at all once the program has finished starting up, and limit how pointers may be used.
:::

::: context owning-later Who owns what, and where it comes back
`std::unique_ptr` arrived in C++11 and `std::make_unique` in C++14. A `unique_ptr` cannot be copied, because two owners would both try to free the same memory; ownership can only be moved from one to another. The advanced course comes back to all of this: destructors that clean up automatically, moving, and `std::shared_ptr` for things with more than one owner.
:::
--- task
Write `void set_to_zero(int* p)` that sets the value `p` points at to `0`, and does nothing at all if `p` is `nullptr`.

Then write `std::unique_ptr<int> make_counter(int start)` that returns a newly owned `int` holding `start`. Use `std::make_unique`, not raw `new`. The starter already includes `<memory>`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <memory>

void set_to_zero(int* p) {
}

--- solution
#include <memory>

void set_to_zero(int* p) {
    if (p != nullptr) {
        *p = 0;
    }
}

std::unique_ptr<int> make_counter(int start) {
    return std::make_unique<int>(start);
}
--- hint
For `set_to_zero`, follow the pointer with `*p` to change the value, but only after checking that `p` is not `nullptr`.
--- hint
`if (p != nullptr) { *p = 0; }` is the whole body of `set_to_zero`.
--- hint
`make_counter` looks like `make_fuel_gauge` from the explanation, with `int` in place of `double`: `return std::make_unique<int>(start);`
--- check test | set_to_zero changes the value it points at
[] { int x = 7; set_to_zero(&x); return x == 0; }()
--- check test | set_to_zero(nullptr) is safe
[] { set_to_zero(nullptr); return true; }()
--- check test | make_counter owns a new int
[] { auto p = make_counter(41); *p += 1; return *p == 42; }()
--- check source absent | Does not use raw new
\bnew\s+int\b

+++ practice | A value or a fallback
--- task
Write `int value_or(int* p, int fallback)` that returns the value `p` points at. If `p` is `nullptr`, it points at nothing, so return `fallback` instead.

So with `int x = 7;`, `value_or(&x, 0)` is `7`, and `value_or(nullptr, -1)` is `-1`. No `main`.
--- starter
int value_or(int* p, int fallback) {
    return *p;
}
--- solution
int value_or(int* p, int fallback) {
    if (p == nullptr) {
        return fallback;
    }
    return *p;
}
--- hint
Following a null pointer is undefined behaviour, so check for `nullptr` before you follow `p`.
--- hint
If `p == nullptr`, return `fallback`. Otherwise return `*p`, the value it points at.
--- check test | Follows a real pointer
[] { int x = 7; return value_or(&x, 0) == 7; }()
--- check test | nullptr gives the fallback
[] { return value_or(nullptr, -1) == -1; }()
--- check test | A pointed-at 0 is still a real value
[] { int zero = 0; return value_or(&zero, 99) == 0; }()

+++ practice | An owned copy
--- task
Write `std::unique_ptr<int> make_copy(int* p)` that returns a newly owned `int` holding the same value that `p` points at. If `p` is `nullptr`, the new `int` holds `0`.

The copy is a separate object: changing the original afterwards must not change the copy. Use `std::make_unique`, not raw `new`. The starter includes `<memory>`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <memory>

std::unique_ptr<int> make_copy(int* p) {
    return std::make_unique<int>(0);
}
--- solution
#include <memory>

std::unique_ptr<int> make_copy(int* p) {
    if (p == nullptr) {
        return std::make_unique<int>(0);
    }
    return std::make_unique<int>(*p);
}
--- hint
`std::make_unique<int>(value)` makes a new `int` holding `value` and hands back its owner.
--- hint
The value to copy is `*p`, but only when `p` is not `nullptr`.
--- check test | The copy holds the same value
[] { int x = 41; auto c = make_copy(&x); return *c == 41; }()
--- check test | Changing the original leaves the copy alone
[] { int x = 5; auto c = make_copy(&x); x = 9; return *c == 5; }()
--- check test | nullptr gives an owned 0
[] { auto c = make_copy(nullptr); return *c == 0; }()
--- check source absent | Does not use raw new
\bnew\s+int\b

+++ practice | Point at the first negative
--- task
Write `int* first_negative(std::vector<int>& v)` that returns a pointer to the first element of `v` that is below 0, or `nullptr` if there is none.

The pointer points **into** the caller's vector, so the caller can change that element through it. For example, with `v` holding `{3, -1, -4}`, `*first_negative(v) = 0;` makes `v` hold `{3, 0, -4}`.

Go through the vector with a reference loop variable, `for (int& x : v)`. Then `&x`, the address of `x`, is the address of the element itself. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

int* first_negative(std::vector<int>& v) {
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x < 0) {
            return &x;
        }
    }
    return nullptr;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

int* first_negative(std::vector<int>& v) {
    for (int& x : v) {
        if (x < 0) {
            return &x;
        }
    }
    return nullptr;
}
--- hint
In the starter, `x` is a copy that disappears at the end of each pass. Its address points at nothing useful once the function returns.
--- hint
With `for (int& x : v)`, `x` is another name for the element, so `&x` is the element's own address.
--- check test | Points at the first negative
[] { std::vector<int> v{3, -1, -4}; int* p = first_negative(v); return p != nullptr && *p == -1; }()
--- check test | Changing through the pointer changes the vector
[] { std::vector<int> v{3, -1, -4}; *first_negative(v) = 0; return v == std::vector<int>{3, 0, -4}; }()
--- check test | No negatives gives nullptr
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 0, 2}; return first_negative(v) == nullptr; }()
--- check test | An empty vector gives nullptr
[] { std::vector<int> v; return first_negative(v) == nullptr; }()

+++ practice | Point at the larger one
--- task
Write `int* larger(int* a, int* b)` that returns whichever pointer points at the larger value. Pointers may be `nullptr`:

- if both are real, return the one pointing at the larger value; on a tie, return `a`;
- if only one is `nullptr`, return the other one;
- if both are `nullptr`, return `nullptr`.

It returns the pointer itself, not the value, so the caller can change the larger value through it. No `main`.
--- starter
int* larger(int* a, int* b) {
    if (*a >= *b) {
        return a;
    }
    return b;
}
--- solution
int* larger(int* a, int* b) {
    if (a == nullptr) {
        return b;
    }
    if (b == nullptr) {
        return a;
    }
    if (*a >= *b) {
        return a;
    }
    return b;
}
--- hint
The starter follows both pointers straight away, so a `nullptr` crashes it. Deal with the `nullptr` cases first.
--- hint
If `a` is `nullptr`, the answer is `b`, whatever `b` is, even if it is `nullptr` too. Then if `b` is `nullptr`, the answer is `a`. Only after that is it safe to compare `*a` and `*b`.
--- check test | Points at the larger value
[] { int x = 3, y = 8; return larger(&x, &y) == &y; }()
--- check test | A tie returns a
[] { int x = 5, y = 5; return larger(&x, &y) == &x; }()
--- check test | One nullptr returns the other
[] { int x = -2; return larger(nullptr, &x) == &x && larger(&x, nullptr) == &x; }()
--- check test | Two nullptrs give nullptr
[] { return larger(nullptr, nullptr) == nullptr; }()
--- check test | The caller can change the larger value
[] { int x = 10, y = 4; *larger(&x, &y) = 0; return x == 0 && y == 4; }()

+++ practice | Fix the clamp
--- task
`clamp_reading(reading, limit)` should make sure the value `reading` points at is not above `limit`: if it is, the value becomes `limit`. If `reading` is `nullptr`, it does nothing.

It never changes anything: after `int r = 150; clamp_reading(&r, 100);`, `r` is still 150. The bug is on one line. Fix it. No `main`.
--- starter
void clamp_reading(int* reading, int limit) {
    if (reading != nullptr && *reading > limit) {
        reading = &limit;
    }
}
--- solution
void clamp_reading(int* reading, int limit) {
    if (reading != nullptr && *reading > limit) {
        *reading = limit;
    }
}
--- hint
Does the wrong line change the value the pointer points at, or does it change the pointer?
--- hint
`reading = &limit;` makes the function's own copy of the pointer point somewhere else. To change the caller's value, follow the pointer: `*reading = limit;`.
--- check test | 150 is clamped to 100
[] { int r = 150; clamp_reading(&r, 100); return r == 100; }()
--- check test | 50 stays 50
[] { int r = 50; clamp_reading(&r, 100); return r == 50; }()
--- check test | Exactly the limit stays
[] { int r = 100; clamp_reading(&r, 100); return r == 100; }()
--- check test | nullptr is safe
[] { clamp_reading(nullptr, 100); return true; }()

+++ practice | Draw fuel evenly
--- task
A spacecraft has several fuel tanks, stored in a vector of their levels. To keep it balanced, fuel is drawn one unit at a time, always from the tank that is fullest at that moment. On a tie, the first of the fullest tanks is used. An empty tank (level 0) is never drawn from.

Write `int draw_balanced(std::vector<int>& tanks, int amount)` that draws `amount` units this way, changing the caller's vector, and returns how many units it really drew. If every tank runs dry before `amount` is reached, it stops early.

So `{5, 3, 4}` with `amount` 4 becomes `{2, 3, 3}` and returns 4. `{1, 0}` with `amount` 5 becomes `{0, 0}` and returns 1.

For each unit, find the fullest tank with a pointer: start with `int* fullest = nullptr;`, loop with `for (int& t : tanks)`, and point `fullest` at any tank above 0 that beats the one it points at so far. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

int draw_balanced(std::vector<int>& tanks, int amount) {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

int draw_balanced(std::vector<int>& tanks, int amount) {
    int drawn = 0;
    while (drawn < amount) {
        int* fullest = nullptr;
        for (int& t : tanks) {
            if (t > 0 && (fullest == nullptr || t > *fullest)) {
                fullest = &t;
            }
        }
        if (fullest == nullptr) {
            break;
        }
        *fullest -= 1;
        drawn++;
    }
    return drawn;
}
--- hint
One unit at a time: an outer loop that runs while fewer than `amount` units are drawn. Inside it, find the fullest tank, take 1 from it, and count the unit.
--- hint
A tank beats the current choice when it is above 0 and either nothing is chosen yet (`fullest == nullptr`) or it is strictly fuller (`t > *fullest`). Strictly, so the first tank wins a tie.
--- hint
If no tank was chosen, every tank is empty: `break` out of the outer loop. Take the unit with `*fullest -= 1;`.
--- check test | {5, 3, 4} drawing 4 becomes {2, 3, 3}
[] { std::vector<int> t{5, 3, 4}; int d = draw_balanced(t, 4); return d == 4 && t == std::vector<int>{2, 3, 3}; }()
--- check test | Running dry stops early
[] { std::vector<int> t{1, 0}; int d = draw_balanced(t, 5); return d == 1 && t == std::vector<int>{0, 0}; }()
--- check test | Drawing 0 changes nothing
[] { std::vector<int> t{2, 2}; int d = draw_balanced(t, 0); return d == 0 && t == std::vector<int>{2, 2}; }()
--- check test | No tanks at all
[] { std::vector<int> t; return draw_balanced(t, 3) == 0; }()
--- check test | Ties go to the first tank
[] { std::vector<int> t{4, 4, 1}; draw_balanced(t, 3); return t == std::vector<int>{2, 3, 1}; }()

This gate covers the whole course: printing and reading input, arithmetic, decisions and loops, functions and recursion, vectors, strings, references, structs and classes, maps and pointers. There are ten problems you have not seen, each mixing several lessons, and twelve questions that check you understand why the code works. You get no hints, so before you start, redo the practice problems of any lesson that felt hard, and make sure you can write each one without looking.
