+++ problem | The second-highest reading
--- task
Write `bool second_largest(const std::vector<int>& v, int& out)`. It looks for the second-largest **different** value in `v`: the largest value that is strictly smaller than the maximum.

- If there is one, it puts it in `out` and returns `true`. So for `{3, 9, 7}` it sets `out` to 7, and for `{9, 9, 7}` it also sets `out` to 7, because the two 9s are the same value.
- If there is none (the vector is empty, or every element is equal), it returns `false` and leaves `out` alone.

Values may be negative. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

bool second_largest(const std::vector<int>& v, int& out) {
    return false;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

bool second_largest(const std::vector<int>& v, int& out) {
    if (v.size() == 0) {
        return false;
    }
    int top = v[0];
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x > top) top = x;
    }
    bool found = false;
    int second = 0;
    for (int x : v) {
        if (x < top && (!found || x > second)) {
            second = x;
            found = true;
        }
    }
    if (found) {
        out = second;
    }
    return found;
}
--- check test | {3, 9, 7} gives 7
[] { int out = 0; bool ok = second_largest({3, 9, 7}, out); return ok && out == 7; }()
--- check test | A repeated maximum is one value: {9, 9, 7} gives 7
[] { int out = 0; bool ok = second_largest({9, 9, 7}, out); return ok && out == 7; }()
--- check test | All negative: {-1, -8, -3} gives -3
[] { int out = 0; bool ok = second_largest({-1, -8, -3}, out); return ok && out == -3; }()
--- check test | All equal gives false and leaves out alone
[] { int out = 42; bool ok = second_largest({5, 5, 5}, out); return !ok && out == 42; }()
--- check test | Empty gives false
[] { int out = 42; bool ok = second_largest({}, out); return !ok && out == 42; }()
--- check test | Two values: {1, 2} gives 1
[] { int out = 0; bool ok = second_largest({1, 2}, out); return ok && out == 1; }()

+++ problem | A telemetry summary
--- task
The Input tab holds sensor readings, one per line: a sensor name (one word), then a whole number. The same sensor appears several times, in any order:

```
TEMP 21
PRES 101
TEMP -5
VOLT 28
TEMP 30
PRES 99
VOLT -2
RAD -7
```

Write a whole program (with `main`) that reads every line until the input runs out, then prints one line per sensor, in alphabetical order of the names, giving how many readings it had and its largest reading:

```
PRES: count 2, max 101
RAD: count 1, max -7
TEMP: count 3, max 30
VOLT: count 2, max 28
```

A sensor's largest reading may be below 0.
--- stdin
TEMP 21
PRES 101
TEMP -5
VOLT 28
TEMP 30
PRES 99
VOLT -2
RAD -7
--- starter
#include <iostream>
#include <map>
#include <string>

int main() {
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>
#include <map>
#include <string>

int main() {
    std::map<std::string, int> count;
    std::map<std::string, int> best;
    std::string name;
    int value;
    while (std::cin >> name >> value) {
        if (count.count(name) == 0 || value > best[name]) {
            best[name] = value;
        }
        count[name]++;
    }
    for (const auto& [sensor, n] : count) {
        std::cout << sensor << ": count " << n << ", max " << best[sensor] << "\n";
    }
    return 0;
}
--- check output | One line per sensor, in name order
PRES: count 2, max 101
RAD: count 1, max -7
TEMP: count 3, max 30
VOLT: count 2, max 28
--- check source | Reads with std::cin
std::cin\s*>>
--- check source | Uses a std::map
std::map\s*<

+++ problem | Balanced brackets
--- task
Write `bool balanced(const std::string& s)` that returns `true` when the round brackets in `s` are balanced, and `false` when they are not. Every other character is ignored.

Balanced means every `(` has a matching `)` after it, and no `)` ever comes before its `(`. So `"(a(b)c)"` is balanced, and so are `""` and `"no brackets"`. But `")("`, `"(()"` and `"())("` are not. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>

bool balanced(const std::string& s) {
    int open = 0;
    int close = 0;
    for (char c : s) {
        if (c == '(') open++;
        if (c == ')') close++;
    }
    return open == close;
}
--- solution
#include <string>

bool balanced(const std::string& s) {
    int depth = 0;
    for (char c : s) {
        if (c == '(') {
            depth++;
        } else if (c == ')') {
            depth--;
            if (depth < 0) {
                return false;
            }
        }
    }
    return depth == 0;
}
--- check case | Nested brackets are balanced
balanced("(a(b)c)")
=> true
--- check case | No brackets at all is balanced
balanced("") && balanced("no brackets")
=> true
--- check case | A close before its open is not
balanced(")(")
=> false
--- check case | One left open is not
balanced("(()")
=> false
--- check case | Equal counts, wrong order, is not
balanced("())(")
=> false
--- check case | Side by side is balanced
balanced("()()(())")
=> true

+++ problem | Climbing the stairs, fast
--- task
You climb a staircase of `n` steps, taking either 1 or 2 steps at a time. Write `long long ways(int n)` that returns how many different ways there are to reach the top.

`ways(0)` is 1 (you are already there), `ways(1)` is 1, `ways(2)` is 2 (1 then 1, or 2 at once), and `ways(5)` is 8. In general, the last move was either 1 step or 2, so `ways(n)` is `ways(n - 1) + ways(n - 2)`.

The checker asks for `ways(90)`, which is over 4 × 10¹⁸. It must answer in well under a second, so work upwards in a loop instead of letting a recursive function call itself twice per step. No `main`.
--- starter
long long ways(int n) {
    if (n < 2) {
        return 1;
    }
    return ways(n - 1) + ways(n - 2);
}
--- solution
long long ways(int n) {
    long long before = 1;
    long long current = 1;
    for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++) {
        long long next = before + current;
        before = current;
        current = next;
    }
    return current;
}
--- check case | ways(0) is 1
ways(0)
=> 1
--- check case | ways(2) is 2
ways(2)
=> 2
--- check case | ways(5) is 8
ways(5)
=> 8
--- check case | ways(90), quickly
ways(90)
=> 4660046610375530309LL

+++ problem | Running statistics
--- task
Write a `class Stats` that keeps statistics on numbers as they arrive, without storing them all:

- `Stats()`: a constructor that takes nothing. A new `Stats` has seen no numbers.
- `void add(double x)`: takes in one more number.
- `int count() const`: how many numbers have been added.
- `double mean() const`: their average, or `0` if none have been added.
- `double min() const` and `double max() const`: the smallest and largest added so far, or `0` if none have been added.

Numbers may be negative. Keep the data private. No `main`.
--- starter
class Stats {
public:
    Stats() {}
    void add(double x) {}
    int count() const { return 0; }
    double mean() const { return 0; }
    double min() const { return 0; }
    double max() const { return 0; }
};
--- solution
class Stats {
public:
    Stats() : count_(0), sum_(0), min_(0), max_(0) {}

    void add(double x) {
        if (count_ == 0 || x < min_) min_ = x;
        if (count_ == 0 || x > max_) max_ = x;
        count_++;
        sum_ += x;
    }

    int count() const { return count_; }
    double mean() const {
        if (count_ == 0) {
            return 0;
        }
        return sum_ / count_;
    }
    double min() const { return min_; }
    double max() const { return max_; }

private:
    int count_;
    double sum_;
    double min_;
    double max_;
};
--- check test | A new Stats is all zero
[] { Stats s; return s.count() == 0 && s.mean() == 0 && s.min() == 0 && s.max() == 0; }()
--- check test | Three numbers
[] { Stats s; s.add(4); s.add(10); s.add(1); return s.count() == 3 && s.mean() == 5 && s.min() == 1 && s.max() == 10; }()
--- check test | All negative: the max is below zero
[] { Stats s; s.add(-3); s.add(-9); return s.max() == -3 && s.min() == -9 && s.mean() == -6; }()
--- check test | All positive: the min is above zero
[] { Stats s; s.add(2.5); s.add(7.5); return s.min() == 2.5 && s.max() == 7.5 && s.mean() == 5; }()

+++ problem | Rotate in place
--- task
Write `void rotate_left(std::vector<int>& v, int k)` that moves every element of the caller's vector `k` places to the left. Elements that fall off the front come back in at the end.

So `{1, 2, 3, 4, 5}` rotated by 2 becomes `{3, 4, 5, 1, 2}`. Rotating by 0, or by the vector's size, changes nothing, and rotating by 7 is the same as rotating by 2. `k` is never negative. An empty vector stays empty, whatever `k` is. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

void rotate_left(std::vector<int>& v, int k) {
    int n = v.size();
    std::vector<int> out;
    for (int i = k; i < n; i++) {
        out.push_back(v[i]);
    }
    v = out;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

void rotate_left(std::vector<int>& v, int k) {
    int n = v.size();
    if (n == 0) {
        return;
    }
    std::vector<int> original = v;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        v[i] = original[(i + k) % n];
    }
}
--- check test | {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} by 2
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 2, 3, 4, 5}; rotate_left(v, 2); return v == std::vector<int>{3, 4, 5, 1, 2}; }()
--- check test | By 0 changes nothing
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 2, 3}; rotate_left(v, 0); return v == std::vector<int>{1, 2, 3}; }()
--- check test | By the size changes nothing
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 2, 3}; rotate_left(v, 3); return v == std::vector<int>{1, 2, 3}; }()
--- check test | By 7 on five elements is the same as by 2
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 2, 3, 4, 5}; rotate_left(v, 7); return v == std::vector<int>{3, 4, 5, 1, 2}; }()
--- check test | An empty vector stays empty
[] { std::vector<int> v; rotate_left(v, 3); return v.size() == 0; }()

+++ problem | Parse a supply line
--- task
Supplies arrive as one line of `name=amount` pairs, separated by commas:

```
water=3,food=12,water=2
```

Write `std::map<std::string, int> parse_counts(const std::string& line)` that returns the total amount for each name. The same name can appear more than once, and amounts can be negative (a correction). So the line above gives `food` → 12 and `water` → 5, and `"x=-4,x=4"` gives `x` → 0. An empty line gives an empty map.

To turn the text of an amount into an `int`, read it from a string stream: `std::istringstream in(text); in >> amount;`. The starter includes `<map>`, `<string>` and `<sstream>`. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <sstream>

std::map<std::string, int> parse_counts(const std::string& line) {
    std::map<std::string, int> totals;
    return totals;
}
--- solution
#include <map>
#include <string>
#include <sstream>

std::map<std::string, int> parse_counts(const std::string& line) {
    std::map<std::string, int> totals;
    std::string rest = line;
    while (rest.size() > 0) {
        std::string pair = rest;
        auto comma = rest.find(',');
        if (comma == std::string::npos) {
            rest = "";
        } else {
            pair = rest.substr(0, comma);
            rest = rest.substr(comma + 1);
        }
        auto eq = pair.find('=');
        if (eq == std::string::npos) {
            continue;
        }
        std::istringstream in(pair.substr(eq + 1));
        int amount = 0;
        in >> amount;
        totals[pair.substr(0, eq)] += amount;
    }
    return totals;
}
--- check case | Repeated names add up
parse_counts("water=3,food=12,water=2")
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"food", 12}, {"water", 5}}
--- check case | One pair
parse_counts("air=1")
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"air", 1}}
--- check case | Negative amounts
parse_counts("x=-4,x=4,y=-1")
=> std::map<std::string, int>{{"x", 0}, {"y", -1}}
--- check test | An empty line gives an empty map
parse_counts("").empty()

+++ problem | Point at the closest value
--- task
Write `int* closest(std::vector<int>& v, int target)` that returns a pointer to the element of the caller's vector whose value is closest to `target`: the smallest distance, where the distance between two numbers is the bigger minus the smaller. On a tie, return the first such element. If the vector is empty, return `nullptr`.

So in `{10, 3, 7}` the element closest to 6 is the 7, and in `{4, 8}` the closest to 6 is the 4 (a tie, and 4 comes first). The caller can change the element through the pointer. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <vector>

int* closest(std::vector<int>& v, int target) {
    return nullptr;
}
--- solution
#include <vector>

int distance(int a, int b) {
    if (a > b) {
        return a - b;
    }
    return b - a;
}

int* closest(std::vector<int>& v, int target) {
    int* best = nullptr;
    for (int& x : v) {
        if (best == nullptr || distance(x, target) < distance(*best, target)) {
            best = &x;
        }
    }
    return best;
}
--- check test | Closest to 6 in {10, 3, 7} is the 7
[] { std::vector<int> v{10, 3, 7}; int* p = closest(v, 6); return p == &v[2]; }()
--- check test | A tie goes to the first
[] { std::vector<int> v{4, 8}; return closest(v, 6) == &v[0]; }()
--- check test | Negative numbers
[] { std::vector<int> v{-10, -4, 5}; return closest(v, -6) == &v[1]; }()
--- check test | Changing through the pointer changes the vector
[] { std::vector<int> v{1, 50, 100}; *closest(v, 60) = 0; return v == std::vector<int>{1, 0, 100}; }()
--- check test | Empty gives nullptr
[] { std::vector<int> v; return closest(v, 3) == nullptr; }()

+++ problem | Mission clock
--- task
The Input tab holds whole numbers of seconds since launch, as many as there are:

```
0 59 3600 45296
-5 86399 360000
```

Write a whole program (with `main`) that prints each one as a clock, `HH:MM:SS`, one per line. Hours, minutes and seconds each have **at least two digits**, with a leading `0` below 10. Hours are not limited to 24, so 360000 seconds is `100:00:00`. A negative number is not a time: print `invalid` for it.

```
00:00:00
00:00:59
01:00:00
12:34:56
invalid
23:59:59
100:00:00
```
--- stdin
0 59 3600 45296
-5 86399 360000
--- starter
#include <iostream>

int main() {
    int t;
    while (std::cin >> t) {
        std::cout << t / 3600 << ":" << (t / 60) % 60 << ":" << t % 60 << "\n";
    }
    return 0;
}
--- solution
#include <iostream>

void print2(int n) {
    if (n < 10) {
        std::cout << "0";
    }
    std::cout << n;
}

int main() {
    int t;
    while (std::cin >> t) {
        if (t < 0) {
            std::cout << "invalid\n";
            continue;
        }
        print2(t / 3600);
        std::cout << ":";
        print2((t / 60) % 60);
        std::cout << ":";
        print2(t % 60);
        std::cout << "\n";
    }
    return 0;
}
--- check output | Prints every time as a clock
00:00:00
00:00:59
01:00:00
12:34:56
invalid
23:59:59
100:00:00
--- check source | Reads with std::cin
std::cin\s*>>

+++ problem | Binary, by recursion
--- task
Write `std::string to_binary(int n)` that returns `n` written in binary, the base-2 number system that uses only the digits 0 and 1. `n` is never negative.

So `to_binary(5)` is `"101"`, `to_binary(1)` is `"1"`, `to_binary(0)` is `"0"`, and `to_binary(1024)` is `"10000000000"`. Use recursion: the last binary digit of `n` is `n % 2`, and the digits before it are `n / 2` written in binary. No leading zeros, except for 0 itself. No `main`.
--- starter
#include <string>

std::string to_binary(int n) {
    return "";
}
--- solution
#include <string>

std::string to_binary(int n) {
    std::string last = "0";
    if (n % 2 == 1) {
        last = "1";
    }
    if (n < 2) {
        return last;
    }
    return to_binary(n / 2) + last;
}
--- check case | to_binary(5) is "101"
to_binary(5)
=> "101"
--- check case | to_binary(0) is "0"
to_binary(0)
=> "0"
--- check case | to_binary(1) is "1"
to_binary(1)
=> "1"
--- check case | to_binary(255) is "11111111"
to_binary(255)
=> "11111111"
--- check case | to_binary(1024) is "10000000000"
to_binary(1024)
=> "10000000000"
--- check source | to_binary calls itself
\bto_binary\s*\([^)]*\)\s*\{[\s\S]*\bto_binary\s*\(

+++ question | Two divisions
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp
int a = 7;
int b = 2;
double x = a / b;
double y = static_cast<double>(a) / b;
std::cout << x << " " << y << "\n";
```
--- answer
3 3.5
--- why
`a / b` divides two ints, so the answer is the whole number 3, and only then is it stored in a `double` as 3.0, which prints as `3`. In the second line `a` is converted to a `double` first, so the division keeps its decimals: 3.5.

+++ question | A copy and a reference
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp
#include <iostream>

void bump(int n) { n = n + 1; }
void bump_ref(int& n) { n = n + 1; }

int main() {
    int a = 1;
    bump(a);
    bump_ref(a);
    bump_ref(a);
    std::cout << a << "\n";
}
```
--- answer
3
--- why
`bump` gets a copy of `a`, so its change is lost when it returns. `bump_ref` takes a reference: `n` is another name for `a`, so both of its calls really add one. 1 + 2 is 3.

+++ question | Down and back up
--- ask
What does `f(3)` print?

```cpp
void f(int n) {
    if (n == 0) {
        return;
    }
    std::cout << n;
    f(n - 1);
    std::cout << n;
}
```
--- answer
321123
--- why
Each call prints its `n` on the way down, before the recursive call, and again on the way back up, after the recursive call has finished. So the calls print 3, 2, 1 going down, reach the base case at 0, then print 1, 2, 3 as each waiting call finishes.

+++ question | Every pair
--- ask
`v` is a vector with `n` elements. How does the running time of this code grow as `n` grows?

```cpp
int pairs = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {
        if (v[i] == v[j]) pairs++;
    }
}
```
--- choice
O(n): it goes through the vector once.
--- choice
O(n log n): the inner loop gets shorter each time.
--- choice correct
O(n²): for each element, it looks at every element after it.
--- choice
O(1): it only counts, so it takes the same time for any size.
--- why
The inner loop runs n − 1 times, then n − 2, and so on down to 0: about n²/2 comparisons in all. A shrinking inner loop halves the work, but halving does not change how it grows: doubling `n` still makes it about four times slower.

+++ question | Recursion that calls itself twice
--- ask
How does the number of calls grow for this Fibonacci function, as `n` grows?

```cpp
long long fib(int n) {
    if (n < 2) return n;
    return fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2);
}
```
--- choice
O(n): it counts down from n to 0 once.
--- choice
O(n²): each call does a loop's worth of work.
--- choice correct
Exponential, roughly doubling with each step of n: every call makes two more calls, and the same values are worked out again and again.
--- choice
O(log n): each call makes the problem smaller.
--- why
`fib(n)` calls `fib(n - 1)` and `fib(n - 2)`, and each of those calls two more. The same small values are recomputed millions of times: `fib(50)` makes billions of calls. Working upwards in a loop, keeping the last two answers, takes only n steps.

+++ question | Which line is wrong?
--- ask
This function should return the largest element of a non-empty vector. It returns the wrong answer for some vectors. Which line is the bug?

```cpp
int max_value(const std::vector<int>& v) {
    int best = 0;                    // line 2
    for (int x : v) {                // line 3
        if (x > best) best = x;      // line 4
    }
    return best;                     // line 6
}
```
--- choice correct
Line 2: `best` should start at `v[0]`. If every element is negative, nothing beats 0, and it returns 0, which is not even in the vector.
--- choice
Line 3: the loop variable must be `int& x`, or `x` is not the real element.
--- choice
Line 4: it should use `>=`, or a tie for the largest is missed.
--- choice
Line 6: it should return `v[best]`.
--- why
The loop only reads the elements, so a copy in `x` is fine, and a tie gives the same value either way. The real trap is the starting value: "best so far" must start from something that is really in the vector.

+++ question | Why const and &
--- ask
Why do functions in this course take a vector as `const std::vector<int>& v` when they only read it?
--- choice
Because a vector cannot be passed to a function any other way.
--- choice correct
The `&` means the function uses the caller's vector instead of copying it, which could be millions of elements, and `const` promises the function will not change it.
--- choice
Because the `&` makes the function return the vector to the caller.
--- choice
Because `const` makes the loop over the vector run faster.
--- why
Passing `std::vector<int> v` does work, but it copies every element on each call. A reference costs almost nothing. Adding `const` keeps the safety of a copy: if the function tries to change the vector, it does not compile.

+++ question | Just looking?
--- ask
What does this print?

```cpp
std::map<std::string, int> stock;
stock["water"] = 40;
if (stock["fuel"] == 0) {
    std::cout << "no fuel\n";
}
std::cout << stock.size() << "\n";
```
--- choice
`no fuel`, then `1`
--- choice correct
`no fuel`, then `2`
--- choice
Only `1`: reading a missing key is an error, so the `if` is skipped.
--- choice
Nothing: the program stops because `"fuel"` is not in the map.
--- why
Reading `stock["fuel"]` with square brackets adds the key `"fuel"` with the value 0 if it is missing. So the test is true, and the map now has two keys. To check without adding, use `stock.count("fuel")`.

+++ question | One past the end
--- ask
`v` holds 4 elements. What happens when the program reads `v[4]`?
--- choice
The program stops with an "index out of range" error that names the line.
--- choice
It gives back 0, the default value.
--- choice correct
Nothing checks it. It reads whatever memory lies past the end, and the program's behaviour is undefined from then on.
--- choice
It wraps round and gives back `v[0]`.
--- why
Square brackets on a vector do no checking, to stay fast. Reading past the end is undefined behaviour: it might print garbage, crash, or seem to work today and fail later. The valid indexes are 0 to `size() - 1`.

+++ question | Not found
--- ask
`s` is a `std::string`. What does `s.find(':')` give back when there is no colon in `s`?
--- choice
`-1`
--- choice
`0`
--- choice correct
`std::string::npos`, a very large unsigned number that means "no position".
--- choice
An empty string, `""`.
--- why
`find` returns an index, and an index is an unsigned whole number, so it cannot be -1. `npos` is the largest value that type can hold. Always compare with `std::string::npos` before using the result: `npos + 1` wraps round to 0, which quietly gives a wrong answer.

+++ question | Why private
--- ask
In the `Account` class, `balance_` is private, and the class offers `deposit` and `balance()`. What does making `balance_` private achieve?
--- choice
Private members take up less memory.
--- choice
Private members can never change once the object is made.
--- choice correct
Only the class's own member functions can touch it, so no outside code can put a value in it that breaks the class's rules.
--- choice
Private members are faster to read.
--- why
`private` controls who may use a member, not how it is stored. Private data still changes, but only through the class's own functions, like `deposit`. So the class can guarantee its rules hold, however the rest of the program uses it.

+++ question | Owning with unique_ptr
--- ask
Why does modern C++ use `std::make_unique` and `std::unique_ptr` instead of raw `new` and `delete` to own an object?
--- choice
`new` and `delete` are no longer allowed in C++20.
--- choice correct
The `unique_ptr` frees the object automatically, exactly once, when it goes out of scope, so the memory can be neither forgotten nor freed twice.
--- choice
A `unique_ptr` makes a copy of the object, so it is safer to change.
--- choice
A `unique_ptr` stores the object in the program's source file, not in memory.
--- why
Memory from `new` must be given back with `delete` exactly once, on every way out of the code. Forget and it leaks; do it twice and the program is broken. A `unique_ptr` is the one owner, and its destructor frees the object when the owner's block ends, whichever way it ends.
